process-states-pcb · 20
OS-U1-Q01MCQeasy

In the classic five-state process model — New, Ready, Running, Waiting and Terminated — and assuming that no preemption occurs, which one of the following transitions is NOT part of the model?

  1. New → Ready
  2. Waiting → Ready
  3. Running → Waiting
  4. Running → Ready
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The five-state diagram has exactly these edges: New→Ready (admitted), Ready→Running (dispatched), Running→Waiting (a blocking request), Waiting→Ready (the event completes), Running→Terminated (exit). A process leaves the CPU only by blocking or by finishing.

Step by step.

  1. From Running the process either blocks (Running→Waiting) or exits (Running→Terminated).
  2. Without preemption nothing else can take the CPU away from a running process.
  3. So there is no edge from Running back to Ready.
  4. The edge Running→Ready appears only once preemption is added, giving the preemptive model.

Answer: Option (D) — Running → Ready is not an edge of the non-preemptive five-state model.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) New→Ready is the admission transition.
  • (B) Waiting→Ready happens when the awaited event or I/O completes.
  • (C) Running→Waiting happens on a blocking request.

Common misconception. Students answer (B) because they think a blocked process resumes straight on the CPU. It always returns to Ready first and waits to be dispatched.

Takeaway. In the non-preemptive model the only exits from Running are Waiting and Terminated; Running→Ready exists only in a preemptive model.

OS-U1-Q02MCQeasy

Which one of the following is NOT stored in the process control block (PCB) of a process?

  1. The program counter and the saved contents of the CPU registers
  2. The process state and the scheduling priority
  3. The source code of the program
  4. The list of open files and the memory-management information
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A PCB is the kernel's record of a process: it holds everything the kernel needs to stop the process and later restart it. The program's code lives in the process's address space, not in the PCB.

Step by step.

  1. The PCB holds process identification (PID, parent PID) and process state.
  2. It holds saved CPU context: program counter, stack pointer and general-purpose registers.
  3. It holds scheduling information, memory-management information (page tables, base and limit registers) and I/O status such as the open-file table.
  4. The executable code and its data are mapped in the process's own address space, so the source code (or even the machine code) is not a PCB field.

Answer: Option (C) — the source code is not part of the PCB.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The saved register state is the heart of a PCB.
  • (B) Process state and priority are standard PCB fields.
  • (D) Open files and memory-management data are standard PCB fields.

Common misconception. The PCB is confused with the process image. The process image contains the code, the data and the stack; the PCB is a small kernel structure that points at them.

Takeaway. PCB = kernel bookkeeping (identity, state, context, scheduling, memory, I/O). The code and data belong to the address space.

OS-U1-Q03MCQmedium

A process in the Running state issues a blocking read on a disk file. In the five-state model, which sequence of transitions does it make before it can execute again?

  1. Running → Waiting, then Waiting → Ready
  2. Running → Ready, then Ready → Running
  3. Running → Waiting, then Waiting → Running
  4. Running → Terminated, then New → Ready
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A blocking system call moves the process from Running to Waiting. When the I/O completes the process is moved to Ready, and only the scheduler can then move it to Running.

Step by step.

  1. The read cannot complete immediately, so the process cannot keep the CPU: Running → Waiting.
  2. The disk controller interrupts when the data has arrived, and the interrupt handler moves the process from Waiting to Ready.
  3. The process now competes for the CPU like any other ready process; the dispatcher moves it Ready → Running.
  4. So the sequence is Running → Waiting → Ready → Running, which is option (A).

Answer: Option (A) — Running → Waiting, then Waiting → Ready.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) skips the Waiting state altogether.
  • (C) has Waiting → Running, which never happens: a woken process must pass through Ready.
  • (D) terminates the process, but a completed read is not a failure.

Common misconception. The single most common error is Waiting → Running. There is no direct edge: every resumption goes through Ready, because the CPU may now be running something else.

Takeaway. Two rules to keep: a process enters Waiting only by its own blocking request, and it reaches Running only from Ready.

OS-U1-Q04MCQmedium

Which one of the following statements about a context switch is TRUE?

  1. It is pure overhead: no useful work of any user process is done while it executes.
  2. It saves the state of the outgoing process in the ready queue rather than in its PCB.
  3. It is needed every time a process makes a system call, even if no other process runs.
  4. Its cost is independent of the number of registers the CPU has.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A context switch saves the CPU context of the outgoing process into its PCB and loads the context of the incoming process from its PCB. While the kernel does this, no user instruction executes, so the time is pure overhead.

Step by step.

  1. The kernel copies the program counter, stack pointer and registers into the PCB of the outgoing process.
  2. It then restores the same set of values from the PCB of the incoming process.
  3. During this interval no user process makes progress, so the cost is overhead, not work.
  4. The more registers the machine has, the more values must be copied, so the cost grows with the register file.

Answer: Option (A) — a context switch is pure overhead.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) The saved state is written to the PCB; the ready queue only holds pointers to PCBs.
  • (C) A system call that does not cause a switch — a fast read from the page cache, for example — needs no context switch.
  • (D) Cost rises with the number of registers, not independently of it.

Common misconception. Students conflate a mode switch (user → kernel) with a context switch. A system call always causes a mode switch but only sometimes a context switch.

Takeaway. Context switch = save one PCB, restore another, no user work done. Mode switch alone is cheaper and much more frequent.

OS-U1-Q05MCQhard

In the seven-state process model, which adds Ready/Suspend and Blocked/Suspend, a process moves from Ready/Suspend to Ready when

  1. the event it was waiting for finally occurs.
  2. the scheduler dispatches it to the CPU.
  3. it is brought back from secondary storage into main memory.
  4. its parent process calls wait().
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two suspended states describe a process that has been swapped out to secondary storage. Leaving a suspended state always means being swapped back into main memory; the suspend/activate transitions are about memory residency, not about events or scheduling.

Step by step.

  1. Ready/Suspend means the process is ready to run but its image is on disk.
  2. It cannot run until its image is back in main memory, so the transition Ready/Suspend → Ready is the activate operation performed by the swapper.
  3. Blocked/Suspend → Blocked is the same activate operation for a blocked process.
  4. The reverse transitions, Ready → Ready/Suspend and Blocked → Blocked/Suspend, are the suspend (swap-out) operations.

Answer: Option (C) — it is brought back from secondary storage into main memory.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The event completing moves Blocked/Suspend → Ready/Suspend, not Ready/Suspend → Ready.
  • (B) Dispatch is the Ready → Running transition.
  • (D) A parent's wait() has nothing to do with swapping a child back in.

Common misconception. The suspended states are read as 'waiting' states. They are not: they are memory-residency states. A ready process can be suspended, which is exactly why the state exists.

Takeaway. Suspended = swapped out. Leaving a suspended state = being swapped in. The event logic is carried entirely by the Blocked pair.

OS-U1-Q06MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the process control block and process states are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A PCB is created when the process is created and is kept in kernel memory.
  2. A process can move directly from the Waiting state to the Running state.
  3. The saved register state of a blocked process is kept in its PCB.
  4. The PCB of a process that is currently running must be in main memory.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The PCB is the kernel's per-process record. It is allocated at creation, resides in kernel memory, and holds the saved context. State transitions always respect the diagram, so Waiting never goes straight to Running.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: the PCB is created by the kernel when the process is admitted, and the kernel keeps it until the process is reaped.
  2. (B) False: a woken process goes Waiting → Ready and must then be dispatched Ready → Running.
  3. (C) True: the program counter, stack pointer and registers of a blocked process are exactly what the PCB stores so the process can be restarted later.
  4. (D) True: the running process is executing from main memory and its PCB is the kernel's live record of it, so that PCB is resident.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the classic invalid edge: Waiting → Running does not exist in any standard model.

Common misconception. Option (D) is often marked false by students who think PCBs can be paged out. A PCB may be swapped for a non-running process, but the PCB of the process that is executing right now is in main memory, because the kernel is using it.

Takeaway. Three facts worth memorising: one PCB per process, allocated at creation in kernel memory, holding the saved context that makes resumption possible.

OS-U1-Q07NATeasy

A process executes the following two statements, with no other process creation and no process terminating before the program ends:
fork();
fork();
Assuming both calls succeed, how many processes exist when the program reaches its end?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. fork() creates one child that is an exact copy of the caller, so every fork() call doubles the number of processes running that same code.

Step by step.

  1. Before the first call there is 1 process.
  2. The first fork() turns 1 process into 2; both continue to the second statement.
  3. The second fork() is executed by each of those 2 processes, so each creates one more: 2 + 2 = 4.
  4. Hence 4 processes exist at the end.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Counting only the children (3) instead of all processes (4). After n fork() calls there are 2ⁿ processes in total, not 2ⁿ − 1.

Takeaway. Each fork() doubles the process count: 1, 2, 4, 8 … for 1, 2, 3 successive calls.

OS-U1-Q26MCQeasy

The CPU scheduler picks a process and the dispatcher hands the CPU over to it. Which one of the following is performed by the dispatcher and not by the CPU scheduler?

  1. Choosing the next process to run from among the ready processes
  2. Saving the context of the outgoing process, switching to user mode and jumping to the proper location in the incoming program
  3. Deciding how many processes are admitted into main memory
  4. Deciding whether the running process should be preempted
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The short-term scheduler selects; the dispatcher performs the switch. The dispatcher does three things — save the context of the outgoing process, switch from kernel to user mode, and jump to the proper location in the new program. The time taken to stop one process and start another is the dispatch latency.

Step by step.

  1. Selecting the victim or the successor is the scheduler's job, so option (A) belongs to the scheduler.
  2. The context save and the jump into the new program are the dispatcher's three actions, which is exactly option (B).
  3. Admission of processes is the long-term scheduler's decision, not the dispatcher's.
  4. The preemption decision is part of the scheduling policy.

Answer: Option (B) — the dispatcher saves the context, switches to user mode and jumps into the incoming program.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the short-term scheduler's selection step.
  • (C) is the long-term scheduler's admission decision.
  • (D) is the preemption policy, which the scheduler applies.

Common misconception. Treating "scheduler" and "dispatcher" as synonyms. The scheduler is a policy that chooses; the dispatcher is the mechanism that switches. Dispatch latency is a cost of the mechanism, not of the policy.

Takeaway. Whenever a question asks what a context switch costs, it is asking about dispatcher work — and that work does no useful work for any user process.

OS-U1-Q27MCQeasy

Which one of the following quantities is controlled by the long-term scheduler?

  1. The degree of multiprogramming, i.e. how many processes are in main memory at once
  2. The order in which ready processes are dispatched to the CPU
  3. The number of context switches performed per second
  4. The length of the time quantum given to each process
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The long-term scheduler decides which programs are admitted to the ready queue and therefore fixes the degree of multiprogramming. It should admit a good mix of I/O-bound and CPU-bound processes, because too many CPU-bound processes leave the I/O devices idle.

Step by step.

  1. The long-term scheduler admits a process into main memory; the count of admitted processes is the degree of multiprogramming, so (A) is its decision.
  2. The order of dispatch among ready processes is the short-term scheduler's decision.
  3. The number of context switches follows from the short-term policy and the quantum, not from admission.
  4. The quantum is a parameter of the short-term policy.

Answer: Option (A) — the long-term scheduler controls the degree of multiprogramming.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) belongs to the short-term scheduler.
  • (C) and (D) are consequences of the short-term policy.

Common misconception. Believing the medium-term scheduler also raises the degree of multiprogramming. It does the opposite: by swapping a process out it reduces the degree of multiprogramming.

Takeaway. Three schedulers, three questions: long-term = how many, medium-term = how many stay in memory, short-term = which one runs now.

OS-U1-Q28MCQmedium

A running process calls exec() successfully. Which one of the following is preserved across that call?

  1. The process identifier (PID)
  2. The contents of the process's heap and data segment
  3. The values that the general-purpose registers held at the moment of the call
  4. The instruction at which the process had been executing
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. exec() overwrites the process image — code, data, heap and stack — with the new program. What survives is the process's identity and its kernel-side bookkeeping: the PID, the parent relationship, the priority and, unless marked close-on-exec, the open file descriptors.

Step by step.

  1. The PID is assigned at fork() and is not changed by exec(), so it survives — option (A).
  2. The heap and data segment belong to the old program image and are discarded.
  3. The registers are reinitialised for the new program's entry point.
  4. Execution restarts at the new program's entry point, so the old instruction is gone.

Answer: Option (A) — the PID is preserved; the process image is replaced.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) The address space is rebuilt for the new program.
  • (C) The register set is reinitialised at the new entry point.
  • (D) Control passes to the new program's entry point.

Common misconception. Thinking exec() creates a new process. It creates no process at all: it replaces the calling process's image, which is why the PID is unchanged and why the call never returns on success.

Takeaway. fork() makes a copy, exec() replaces the image, and the PID belongs to the process, not to the program.

OS-U1-Q29MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about process creation and termination in a UNIX-like system are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. After fork() returns, the parent and the child both continue from the instruction that follows the call.
  2. A successful exec() replaces the process image but keeps the same PID.
  3. A process that has terminated but whose exit status has not yet been collected by its parent is called a zombie.
  4. A zombie process keeps executing in the background and continues to consume CPU time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. fork() returns twice — once in the parent, with the child's PID, and once in the child, with 0 — and both copies resume at the next instruction. exec() overlays a new program on the existing process. A terminated process whose parent has not yet called wait() stays in the process table as a zombie, holding only its exit status.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: fork() duplicates the calling process and both copies continue after the call.
  2. (B) is true: exec() changes the program, not the process identity.
  3. (C) is true: that leftover entry is exactly what "zombie" names.
  4. (D) is false: a zombie has already released its memory and has no code to run, so it uses no CPU. It occupies a process-table slot and nothing else.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Confusing a zombie with an orphan. A zombie has died and awaits reaping; an orphan is alive but its parent has died, so it is re-parented to init. Zombies waste table entries, not CPU time.

Takeaway. Read every statement for what it literally says: (D) fails on "keeps executing", not on "zombie".

OS-U1-Q30MCQeasy

What is the primary purpose of the medium-term scheduler (the swapper)?

  1. To decide which of the ready processes runs next on the CPU
  2. To remove a process from main memory temporarily, lowering the degree of multiprogramming and freeing memory
  3. To admit brand-new processes into the system
  4. To decide the order in which pending I/O requests are serviced
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The medium-term scheduler swaps a whole process out of main memory to disk and later back in. It is the tool that turns the five-state model into the seven-state model, adding Ready/Suspend and Blocked/Suspend. Its effect is to lower the degree of multiprogramming without destroying the process.

Step by step.

  1. Swapping a process out of memory is exactly what the medium-term scheduler does — option (B).
  2. Choosing the next ready process is the short-term scheduler.
  3. Admitting new processes is the long-term scheduler.
  4. Ordering I/O requests is disk scheduling, part of the I/O subsystem.

Answer: Option (B) — the medium-term scheduler swaps processes out of memory to reduce the degree of multiprogramming.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the short-term scheduler.
  • (C) is the long-term scheduler.
  • (D) is the disk scheduler.

Common misconception. Thinking swapping improves throughput automatically. Swapping relieves memory pressure at the cost of disk traffic, so it can reduce throughput if it is used too eagerly — the thrashing problem of Unit 5.

Takeaway. The medium-term scheduler is the only one that can make a process leave main memory while it is still alive.

OS-U1-Q31NATmedium

Three CPU-bound processes each need exactly 12 ms of CPU time. They are run one after another on a single CPU by a non-preemptive scheduler. Every context switch between two processes costs 0.4 ms and there is no other overhead. What is the total elapsed time, in milliseconds, from the instant the first process starts running to the instant the third process finishes? (Enter a number; one decimal place is allowed.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Useful CPU work is the sum of the bursts. On top of that, every switch between two processes costs the dispatch latency. With three processes run back to back there are exactly two switches — P1 → P2 and P2 → P3.

Step by step.

  1. Useful work = 3 × 12 = 36 ms.
  2. Switches between processes = 3 − 1 = 2.
  3. Switch overhead = 2 × 0.4 = 0.8 ms.
  4. Total elapsed time = 36 + 0.8 = 36.8 ms.

Answer: 36.8 ms.

Common misconception. Counting three switches and adding 1.2 ms. The interval measured runs from the start of P1 to the end of P3, so the switch into P1 and the switch out of P3 lie outside it; only the two switches between processes are inside.

Takeaway. Number of context switches between n processes run back to back = n − 1. Multiply by the switch cost and add the useful work.

OS-U1-Q32MCQmedium

The seven-state process model adds Ready/Suspend and Blocked/Suspend to the five-state model. Which one of the following transitions is NOT possible?

  1. Blocked → Blocked/Suspend
  2. Ready/Suspend → Ready
  3. Running → Ready/Suspend
  4. Blocked/Suspend → Ready/Suspend
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In the seven-state model a process leaves the CPU only through the ordinary five-state edges. To be suspended it must first be off the CPU, in Ready or Blocked; only then can the swapper move it to disk. There is therefore no edge that takes a running process straight to a suspended state.

Step by step.

  1. Blocked → Blocked/Suspend: the swapper moves a waiting process out of memory — possible.
  2. Ready/Suspend → Ready: the process is swapped back in — possible.
  3. Running → Ready/Suspend: the running process would have to be both displaced and written out in one step, which the model does not allow — not possible.
  4. Blocked/Suspend → Ready/Suspend: the awaited event completes while the process is on disk — possible.

Answer: Option (C) — Running → Ready/Suspend is not an edge of the seven-state model.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the ordinary swap-out of a blocked process.
  • (B) is the ordinary swap-in of a ready process.
  • (D) is the event-completion edge while the process is suspended.

Common misconception. Assuming that suspension is something the scheduler can do to a running process. Suspension is done by the medium-term scheduler to a process that is already off the CPU, and the model always routes it through Ready or Blocked first.

Takeaway. Any transition in this model either leaves or enters the CPU through the five-state edges; the suspend states are reached only from Ready and Blocked.

OS-U1-Q51MCQeasy

A process has finished executing but its parent has not yet called wait(). Which one of the following describes the process and what the kernel keeps for it?

  1. It is a zombie: its PCB and exit status stay in the process table until the parent reaps it.
  2. It is an orphan: its PCB is released at once and its children are reparented to init.
  3. It is a daemon: it stays in the Ready state until the parent exits.
  4. It is suspended: it is swapped out to disk until the parent is dispatched.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A terminated process whose parent has not yet collected its exit status is a zombie. The kernel releases the address space, open files and other resources but keeps the PCB, because the exit status has to be delivered to the parent through wait().

Step by step.

  1. The child calls exit(); the kernel frees its memory, file descriptors and other resources.
  2. It cannot free the PCB, because the exit status still has to reach the parent.
  3. The child therefore stays in the process table in the Terminated state: a zombie.
  4. When the parent calls wait() the status is copied out and the PCB is freed. If the parent dies first, init adopts the zombie and reaps it.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) describes an orphan — a live child whose parent has died — and an orphan's PCB is certainly not released while the child runs.
  • (C) A daemon is a background process unrelated to termination.
  • (D) Suspension is the medium-term scheduler's action on a live process, not the end state of a dead one.

Common misconception. Students think exit() removes the process. It removes the address space; the process-table entry survives until the parent reaps it, which is exactly why a buggy parent can fill the process table with zombies.

Takeaway. Zombie = dead child, live parent, PCB retained. Orphan = dead parent, live child, reparented.

OS-U1-Q52MCQeasy

A process is in the Waiting (blocked) state. Which one of the following is TRUE about it?

  1. It is on the ready queue, because it can run as soon as the CPU is free.
  2. It is on the wait queue of the event or device it is blocked on, and it is not on the ready queue.
  3. It is on the job queue, because it has not yet been admitted to memory.
  4. It is on no kernel list at all, because a blocked process holds no kernel resources.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The kernel keeps three kinds of process list. The job queue holds processes not yet admitted to memory; the ready queue holds admitted processes that can run; the device (wait) queue holds processes blocked on a particular event or device. Each device has its own wait queue.

Step by step.

  1. The process issues a blocking request and the kernel moves it out of Running.
  2. It is placed on the wait queue of the specific device or event it is waiting for.
  3. It leaves the ready queue, because it cannot be dispatched while the event is pending.
  4. When the event completes, the interrupt handler moves it from the wait queue to the ready queue.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A blocked process cannot use the CPU however free it is; it is not on the ready queue.
  • (C) The job queue is for processes not yet admitted; this one is already in memory.
  • (D) A blocked process still holds a PCB, its address space and often open files.

Common misconception. Treating the ready queue as the only list. The distinction between the ready queue and a device wait queue is what makes the wake-up step (Waiting → Ready) meaningful.

Takeaway. Blocked means queued on an event, not queued for the CPU.

OS-U1-Q53NATmedium

A system admits at most 4096 processes at any instant. Each PCB occupies 1.5 KB, and the kernel additionally reserves a fixed 64 KB for its process-table overhead. What is the total kernel memory, in KB, that must be reserved for process bookkeeping?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. One PCB exists per process, and the kernel holds every live PCB in its own memory. The total is therefore (maximum processes) × (PCB size) plus any fixed table overhead.

Step by step.

  1. PCB bytes: 4096 × 1.5 KB = 6144 KB.
  2. Add the fixed overhead: 6144 + 64 = 6208 KB.

Answer: 6208 KB.

Common misconception. Multiplying by the number of running processes. Every admitted process, running, ready, blocked or suspended, owns a PCB, so the bound uses the maximum process count, not the CPU count.

Takeaway. PCB memory scales with the process limit, which is one reason a high process limit costs real kernel memory.

OS-U1-Q54MCQmedium

Which one of the following gives the correct order of the steps performed when the CPU is switched from process P to process Q?

  1. Restore Q's context, save P's context, update P's state, select Q.
  2. Select Q, restore Q's context, save P's context, update P's state.
  3. Save P's context in P's PCB, update P's state, select Q, restore Q's context from Q's PCB.
  4. Update P's state, restore Q's context, save P's context, select Q.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A context switch has four steps, and their order is forced by two dependencies: P's registers must be saved before anything overwrites them, and Q must be chosen before Q's saved registers can be loaded.

Step by step.

  1. Save P's program counter, registers and memory-management state into P's PCB — the CPU still holds P's values, so this must come first.
  2. Change P's state from Running to Ready (or Waiting, if it blocked).
  3. Run the scheduler to select Q from the ready queue.
  4. Restore Q's context from Q's PCB and set Q's state to Running.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) restores Q before saving P, so P's registers are destroyed by Q's.
  • (B) restores Q's context before Q has even been selected, and before P has been saved.
  • (D) saves P only after Q has already been loaded into the registers.

Common misconception. Thinking the scheduler runs first. The running process must be saved while its state is still in the hardware; selection happens after the save.

Takeaway. Save → change state → select → restore. Saving precedes selecting, and selecting precedes restoring.

OS-U1-Q55MSQhard

Which of the following statements about zombie and orphan processes are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A zombie occupies an entry in the process table but receives no CPU time.
  2. An orphan is a process whose parent terminated before it did.
  3. A zombie's entry is released when its parent calls wait(), or when the parent itself terminates and the zombie is reparented to init, which reaps it.
  4. Every orphan is also a zombie at the moment its parent dies.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two terms describe opposite half-dead states. A zombie has finished but its parent has not collected the exit status. An orphan is still alive but its parent has gone.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: the zombie holds a process-table slot, but it has no runnable code, so it is never dispatched.
  2. (B) True: that is the definition of an orphan.
  3. (C) True: the exit status is collected by wait(); if the parent dies first, the zombie is reparented to init (or a subreaper), which reaps it promptly.
  4. (D) False: an orphan is a live process, so it is not a zombie. It becomes a zombie only later, when it terminates and its parent has not waited.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other option is wrong.

  • (D) confuses the two. The orphan's parent is dead; the zombie's parent is alive.

Common misconception. Believing a zombie can be killed with a signal. It has no code to run, so the only cure is for the parent to reap it — or for the parent to exit.

Takeaway. Zombie: dead child, live parent. Orphan: live child, dead parent. Neither is a scheduling state; both are bookkeeping consequences of the parent-child relation.

OS-U1-Q56MCQeasy

A running process issues a blocking read on the keyboard. Which one of the following describes the transition it makes and the event that ends it?

  1. Running → Ready; the scheduler later moves it to Running.
  2. Running → Waiting; the timer interrupt moves it back to Running.
  3. Running → Terminated; the read completes and the process exits.
  4. Running → Waiting; the keyboard interrupt signalling I/O completion moves it to Ready.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A blocking request takes the process out of Running and into Waiting. Completion of the I/O raises an interrupt; the handler wakes the process by moving it to Ready, never directly to Running.

Step by step.

  1. The process executes the read and the kernel finds the data unavailable.
  2. The kernel puts the process on the keyboard's wait queue: Running → Waiting.
  3. The key press raises an interrupt; the device driver moves the process to the ready queue: Waiting → Ready.
  4. The scheduler eventually dispatches it: Ready → Running, and the read returns the data.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A blocking call does not leave the process runnable.
  • (B) The timer does not end an I/O wait; the device interrupt does, and the process lands in Ready, not Running.
  • (C) A read completing does not terminate the process.

Common misconception. Waking a process straight into Running. The woken process must still compete for the CPU in the ready queue.

Takeaway. Voluntary transitions come from the process itself (block, exit); the interrupt only ever moves a process to Ready.

threads-user-vs-kernel · 18
OS-U1-Q08MCQeasy

Which one of the following is TRUE of user-level threads?

  1. The kernel schedules each thread of the process separately.
  2. A blocking system call made by one thread blocks every thread in that process.
  3. They cannot be created unless the operating system provides a thread library in the kernel.
  4. Each thread has its own private address space.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With user-level threads the kernel sees only one process and one schedulable entity. The thread library multiplexes several threads onto that single kernel entity, so the kernel cannot tell them apart.

Step by step.

  1. Because the kernel knows only the process, it schedules the process, not the threads.
  2. When one user-level thread makes a blocking system call, the kernel blocks the whole process, so every thread in it stops.
  3. No kernel thread support is needed at all — that is the point of user-level threads.
  4. Threads of one process share the address space; only their stacks and registers differ.

Answer: Option (B) — a blocking call by one user-level thread blocks the whole process.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The kernel schedules the process as a single entity.
  • (C) User-level threads need no kernel support; the library does all the work.
  • (D) Threads share one address space; that is what makes them cheap.

Common misconception. Students assume threads are always independently schedulable. That is only true when the threads are kernel-level.

Takeaway. User-level threads: fast, no kernel involvement, but one blocking call stalls them all. Kernel-level threads: independently schedulable, but heavier.

OS-U1-Q09MCQeasy

Which one of the following is shared by all threads of the same process?

  1. The stack
  2. The program counter
  3. The set of CPU registers
  4. The heap and the global data segment
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Threads are the unit of execution; a process is the unit of resource ownership. Everything that is a resource — code, global data, heap, open files — is shared; everything that is execution state — stack, registers, program counter — is private.

Step by step.

  1. Each thread needs its own stack to hold its own local variables and call frames.
  2. Each thread needs its own program counter, because the threads are at different points in the code.
  3. Each thread needs its own register set for the same reason.
  4. The heap and global data are resources of the process and are shared by every thread, which is why unsynchronised access to them causes data races.

Answer: Option (D) — the heap and the global data segment.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) are all per-thread execution state.

Common misconception. The heap is often assumed to be per-thread because malloc is called per thread. malloc is thread-safe but the heap itself is one shared arena.

Takeaway. Shared: code, globals, heap, files. Private: stack, registers, program counter. That single split answers most thread questions.

OS-U1-Q10MCQmedium

Which threading model cannot exploit a multicore processor, however many threads the process creates?

  1. Many-to-one
  2. One-to-one
  3. Many-to-many
  4. Both many-to-one and many-to-many
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Parallel execution on several cores requires several kernel-schedulable entities. The many-to-one model maps every user thread onto a single kernel thread, so at most one thread can be on a CPU at any instant.

Step by step.

  1. Many-to-one: all user threads share one kernel thread, so the kernel can place that one entity on at most one core.
  2. One-to-one: each user thread has its own kernel thread, so the kernel can spread them across cores.
  3. Many-to-many: several user threads map onto a smaller set of kernel threads, but that set can still be larger than one, so parallelism is possible.
  4. Only many-to-one is strictly limited to a single core.

Answer: Option (A) — many-to-one.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) One-to-one supports true parallelism.
  • (C) Many-to-many supports parallelism as long as more than one kernel thread is used.
  • (D) is wrong because many-to-many can use several cores.

Common misconception. Students pick many-to-many because they reason that the multiplexing must serialise the threads. Multiplexing limits the degree of parallelism; it does not force it to one.

Takeaway. Parallelism needs more than one kernel thread. Many-to-one has exactly one, so it is confined to a single core.

OS-U1-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about user-level and kernel-level threads are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Kernel-level threads of one process can execute in parallel on different cores of a multiprocessor.
  2. Creating a user-level thread is typically cheaper than creating a kernel-level thread.
  3. If one kernel-level thread of a process blocks in a system call, all the other threads of that process block as well.
  4. Switching between two user-level threads of the same process does not require a switch to kernel mode.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Kernel-level threads are separate schedulable entities, so the kernel can run them on different cores and block one without blocking the others. User-level threads are managed entirely inside the process, which makes creation and switching cheap.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: each kernel thread can be dispatched to a different core.
  2. (B) True: a user-level thread needs only a stack and a small control block allocated by the library, with no kernel trap.
  3. (C) False: the kernel blocks only the thread that made the call; the other threads of the process stay runnable.
  4. (D) True: the library saves and restores registers in user space, so no trap occurs.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) describes the many-to-one, user-level situation, not kernel-level threads.

Common misconception. Option (C) is the exact property that separates the two kinds of thread, and it is sometimes attached to the wrong kind.

Takeaway. Kernel-level: block one, the others run. User-level: block one, block all — but creation and switching are much cheaper.

OS-U1-Q12MCQhard

A process uses a many-to-one user-level thread library whose scheduler is non-preemptive. It has three threads, and one of them enters a long computation that contains no system call and never yields. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. All three threads continue to make progress, because the kernel time-slices them.
  2. The computing thread keeps the CPU and the other two threads never run.
  3. The kernel preempts the computing thread after one time quantum and schedules another thread of the process.
  4. The other two threads run on other cores of the processor.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In many-to-one, the kernel schedules one entity — the process. The thread library schedules the threads inside that entity, and a non-preemptive library can only switch when the running thread yields or blocks.

Step by step.

  1. The kernel sees a single schedulable entity and can only preempt that entity, not the individual threads inside it.
  2. A timer interrupt therefore returns control to the same thread, because the library's dispatcher is never entered.
  3. The computing thread makes no system call and never yields, so the library never gets a chance to switch.
  4. The other two threads remain ready inside the library and never execute.

Answer: Option (B) — the computing thread keeps the CPU and the other two threads never run.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) The kernel cannot time-slice threads it does not know about.
  • (C) A quantum expiry re-enters the same thread; the library scheduler is not invoked.
  • (D) Many-to-one uses one kernel thread, so only one core can be used.

Common misconception. Students assume a non-preemptive library is safe because the OS preempts the process. The OS does preempt the process, but it resumes the same thread.

Takeaway. A non-preemptive user-level scheduler makes cooperative multitasking mandatory: a thread that never yields starves its siblings.

OS-U1-Q13NATeasy

A process starts with a single thread and then creates four more threads with pthread_create. Each thread, including the original one, is given its own stack by the thread library. How many distinct thread stacks exist in the process?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every thread of a process has a private stack, because each needs its own call frames and local variables. The count of stacks therefore equals the count of threads.

Step by step.

  1. The process begins with 1 thread, which already has a stack.
  2. Four more threads are created, each with a new stack.
  3. Total threads = 1 + 4 = 5.
  4. Total stacks = 5, one per thread.

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Answering 4 by counting only the newly created threads. The original thread has a stack too, so the total is threads, not creations.

Takeaway. Stacks are per thread; the heap and the code are per process. One stack per thread, always.

OS-U1-Q33MCQeasy

Under the one-to-one threading model, a process creates n user-level threads. How many kernel threads exist for that process?

  1. Exactly 1
  2. Exactly n
  3. About n / 2
  4. As many as the processor has cores
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In the one-to-one model each user-level thread is backed by its own kernel thread, so the counts match. This is what Linux and Windows implement; the price is that every user thread consumes kernel resources.

Step by step.

  1. One-to-one means a one-to-one binding, so n user threads need n kernel threads.
  2. That is option (B).
  3. The single kernel thread in the options describes the many-to-one model.
  4. The number of cores limits how many run simultaneously, not how many exist.

Answer: Option (B) — exactly n kernel threads.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes many-to-one.
  • (C) is not a real model; many-to-many maps m user threads onto n ≤ m kernel threads, not m/2.
  • (D) confuses existence with simultaneous execution.

Common misconception. Confusing parallelism with existence. A process may hold thousands of kernel threads on an eight-core machine; only eight can execute at any instant.

Takeaway. Model names are literal: one-to-one = n threads to n threads; many-to-one = many to one; many-to-many = many to a smaller number.

OS-U1-Q34MCQeasy

Which one of the following best describes the many-to-many threading model?

  1. Each user-level thread is permanently bound to one kernel thread.
  2. Many user-level threads are multiplexed onto a smaller or equal number of kernel threads, and the binding may change over time.
  3. All user-level threads of a process are multiplexed onto a single kernel thread.
  4. The kernel creates one thread per process and the user-level library creates no threads at all.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Many-to-many multiplexes m user-level threads onto n kernel threads with n ≤ m, and the library is free to re-assign a user thread to a different kernel thread. It combines the cheap creation of many-to-one with the true parallelism of one-to-one.

Step by step.

  1. The defining feature is m user threads over n kernel threads with n ≤ m and a binding that may change — option (B).
  2. A permanent one-to-one binding is the one-to-one model.
  3. A single kernel thread for all user threads is the many-to-one model.
  4. A model with no user-level threads is not a threading model at all.

Answer: Option (B) — many user-level threads multiplexed onto a smaller or equal number of kernel threads.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is one-to-one.
  • (C) is many-to-one.
  • (D) describes a process with a single thread of control.

Common misconception. Believing many-to-many is only a performance trick. Its real point is that the kernel can block one kernel thread without stalling the other user threads, while the library still creates threads cheaply.

Takeaway. Many-to-many is the model that keeps both advantages: cheap user-level creation and genuine multicore execution.

OS-U1-Q35MCQeasy

A thread is cancelled using deferred cancellation. When does the thread actually stop executing?

  1. Immediately, at whatever instruction it happens to be executing
  2. At the next cancellation point — a place where the thread checks whether it should terminate, such as a blocking I/O or synchronisation call
  3. Only when the whole process terminates
  4. Only after every other thread of the process has terminated
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deferred cancellation sets a flag on the target thread; the thread itself notices the flag when it next reaches a cancellation point. Asynchronous cancellation, by contrast, stops the target at once — which can leave shared data in an inconsistent state.

Step by step.

  1. Deferred means "not now, at a safe point", so the thread continues until a cancellation point — option (B).
  2. Stopping at the current instruction is asynchronous cancellation.
  3. Waiting for process termination is not cancellation at all.
  4. Threads are independent; one thread's cancellation does not wait for its siblings.

Answer: Option (B) — at the next cancellation point.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes asynchronous cancellation.
  • (C) and (D) confuse cancellation with process exit.

Common misconception. Thinking deferred cancellation is unreliable. It is precisely the safe variant: the thread is only killed where the library knows it holds no inconsistent state.

Takeaway. Asynchronous = kill now, risk inconsistency. Deferred = kill at the next safe checkpoint.

OS-U1-Q36MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about threads are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A thread pool avoids the cost of creating a fresh thread for every incoming request.
  2. Thread-local storage is shared by all the threads of a process.
  3. Two kernel threads of the same process can be scheduled on two different processors at the same time.
  4. Creating a kernel thread is typically more expensive than creating a user-level thread.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A thread pool keeps a set of idle threads ready, so a request is served by an existing thread. Thread-local storage is the opposite of shared: each thread gets its own copy. Kernel threads are scheduled independently, so they can run in parallel. A kernel thread costs a kernel data structure and a system call, so it is dearer than a library-level thread.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true — that is the whole purpose of a pool.
  2. (B) is false — thread-local storage exists precisely so that a name can hold a different value in each thread.
  3. (C) is true — the kernel schedules each thread as its own dispatchable entity.
  4. (D) is true — the kernel must allocate and track the thread, whereas the library only manages user space.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading "thread-local storage" as "storage local to the process". The name says the storage is local to a thread; sharing it would defeat its purpose.

Takeaway. For any "is it shared?" claim, sort the item first: code, globals and heap are per process; stack, registers, program counter and thread-local storage are per thread.

OS-U1-Q37MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about thread context switches are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A switch between two user-level threads of the same process is typically cheaper than a switch between two kernel threads.
  2. On a system with one-to-one threads, the kernel schedules threads rather than processes.
  3. Every thread context switch requires the translation lookaside buffer to be flushed.
  4. With many-to-many threads, the library multiplexes user threads over kernel threads, so the kernel need not know about every user thread.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A user-level thread switch stays inside one process's address space and needs no trap, so it is cheaper. With one-to-one the kernel's dispatchable unit is the thread. A TLB flush is needed only when the address space changes, which does not happen between threads of one process. Many-to-many hides the user threads from the kernel by design.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true — no mode switch and no address-space change.
  2. (B) is true — that is what one-to-one means at the kernel level.
  3. (C) is false — threads of the same process share one address space, so the TLB entries stay valid. A flush is a cost of switching between processes.
  4. (D) is true — the library owns the mapping.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming every context switch flushes the TLB. Only a switch to a different address space does; a thread switch inside one process does not.

Takeaway. Rank switch costs: user-thread switch < kernel-thread switch in one process < switch between processes (which may also flush the TLB).

OS-U1-Q38MCQhard

A server uses the one-to-one threading model and creates one kernel thread per connection. At around 20 000 simultaneous connections the machine slows sharply and refuses new connections, even though the CPU is nearly idle and plenty of RAM is free. Which one of the following is the most likely cause?

  1. The per-process limit on threads, or the kernel memory needed to track each thread, has been reached — every kernel thread costs kernel resources.
  2. The many-to-one mapping has collapsed all the threads onto a single kernel thread.
  3. User-level threads cannot block, so all the connections have deadlocked.
  4. The round-robin time quantum has become too small.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In the one-to-one model each user thread consumes a kernel thread: a kernel stack, a task structure and scheduler bookkeeping. Those come out of kernel memory and from a per-process limit on threads, not out of user RAM, so the machine can refuse new threads while free RAM and idle CPU remain.

Step by step.

  1. The symptom is failure to create threads while CPU and user memory are fine, which points at a resource that is neither CPU nor user RAM.
  2. Kernel thread bookkeeping is exactly such a resource, and one-to-one makes it proportional to the number of connections — option (A).
  3. The many-to-one model would show a different symptom: no parallelism at all, not a hard refusal.
  4. The quantum affects responsiveness, not the ability to create a thread.

Answer: Option (A) — exhaustion of the kernel-side per-thread resources.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) contradicts the stated one-to-one model.
  • (C) is false: threads may block, and blocking is what a connection thread does.
  • (D) would slow response, not refuse connections.

Common misconception. Looking only at user-visible memory. A thread costs kernel stack and task-structure space that is not part of the process's heap, so "RAM is free" does not mean "threads are available".

Takeaway. One-to-one trades cheap creation for kernel overhead. A thread pool or an event-driven design caps that overhead; one thread per connection does not.

OS-U1-Q57MCQeasy

A web server replaces its one-thread-per-request design with a fixed pool of 8 worker threads fed by a request queue. Which one of the following is the main benefit?

  1. Thread creation and destruction cost is paid once and the number of runnable threads is bounded.
  2. Each request now runs in its own address space.
  3. Requests are now executed strictly in arrival order.
  4. A blocking call in one worker no longer affects the other workers.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A thread pool creates its workers once and reuses them, so the cost of pthread_create and thread teardown is amortised over many requests, and the number of threads competing for the CPU is fixed in advance.

Step by step.

  1. Without a pool, every request pays create plus destroy, which is tens of microseconds each.
  2. With a pool, a worker takes the next request from the queue and loops, so no thread is created for it.
  3. The pool size caps concurrency, which bounds memory use and avoids thrashing the scheduler with thousands of runnable threads.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Threads of one process share one address space; a pool changes nothing there.
  • (C) Ordering depends on the queue discipline, not on pooling.
  • (D) A blocking call in a worker still blocks that worker; pooling does not make it asynchronous.

Common misconception. Expecting a pool to make a blocking worker harmless. It bounds the damage — only one of eight workers is stuck — but it does not prevent the block.

Takeaway. A pool trades peak concurrency for predictable cost and a bounded thread count.

OS-U1-Q58MCQmedium

A thread of a multi-threaded process calls exit(0) rather than pthread_exit(). What happens?

  1. Only the calling thread terminates; the others keep running.
  2. The whole process terminates, including every one of its threads.
  3. The calling thread becomes a zombie until another thread joins it.
  4. The calling thread returns to the ready queue with its state reset.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. exit() is a process call: it terminates the process and with it every thread. pthread_exit() is a thread call: it terminates only the calling thread and leaves the rest running.

Step by step.

  1. The thread executes exit(0), which requests termination of the whole process.
  2. The kernel terminates all threads of the process, not just the caller.
  3. Buffered output may or may not be flushed depending on the language runtime, which is why the choice matters in practice.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes pthread_exit(), not exit().
  • (C) A thread has no zombie state; only a process does.
  • (D) A terminated thread is not re-queued.

Common misconception. Assuming every thread-level call is thread-local. The rule is in the name: exit exits the process, pthread_exit exits the thread.

Takeaway. Use pthread_exit() (or simply return from the thread function) to end one thread and keep the process alive.

OS-U1-Q59NATeasy

A server receives 10 000 requests per second. Creating a thread costs 40 μs and destroying it costs 20 μs. A thread pool removes both costs because its workers are reused. How many milliseconds of CPU time per second does the pool save?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The per-request saving is the create cost plus the destroy cost. Multiply by the request rate, then convert microseconds to milliseconds.

Step by step.

  1. Per request: 40 μs + 20 μs = 60 μs.
  2. Per second: 10 000 × 60 μs = 600 000 μs.
  3. Convert: 600 000 μs = 600 ms.

Answer: 600 ms per second.

Common misconception. Adding the two costs to the burst time instead of counting them as pure overhead. They buy no useful work, so the pool recovers the whole 600 ms — 60% of a single core.

Takeaway. At high request rates, thread lifecycle cost can dominate; a pool converts it into a one-off charge.

OS-U1-Q60MSQmedium

In a multi-threaded process, which of the following are maintained per thread rather than per process? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The program counter
  2. The stack
  3. The page-table base register
  4. The register set
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Threads of one process share the address space and therefore the page tables. What must differ is everything that describes where that thread is in its own execution: its program counter, its registers and its stack.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: each thread is at a different point in its code, so the PC is per thread.
  2. (B) True: each thread needs its own call frames; sharing one stack would corrupt returns.
  3. (C) False: the page-table base register defines the address space, which all threads share, so it is per process.
  4. (D) True: the general-purpose registers hold the thread's live values at the moment it is switched out.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other option is wrong.

  • (C) If each thread had its own page table the threads could not see each other's data, which is the whole point of threads.

Common misconception. Assuming the heap is per thread because each thread allocates. malloc returns memory from the shared heap; only the allocation bookkeeping is serialised, not the memory itself.

Takeaway. Per thread: PC, registers, stack, thread-local storage. Per process: address space, page tables, open files, signal handlers.

OS-U1-Q61MCQeasy

Which one of the following is a genuine advantage of user-level threads over kernel-level threads?

  1. A blocking system call in one thread does not block the others.
  2. Several threads of the process can run on several cores at once.
  3. Switching between two threads needs no trap into the kernel.
  4. The kernel can schedule each thread independently.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. User-level threads are managed entirely by a library in user space. Because the kernel knows only the process, a thread switch is a library call that saves and restores registers — no trap, no mode change, no kernel scheduler.

Step by step.

  1. A kernel-level thread switch requires a system call into the kernel and a scheduling decision.
  2. A user-level switch is a function call inside the process; it is typically an order of magnitude cheaper.
  3. The price is that the kernel cannot see the threads, so it cannot run them in parallel or unblock one of them independently.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the opposite: a blocking call blocks the whole process under the many-to-one model.
  • (B) A user-level library cannot put two threads on two cores; only the kernel can.
  • (D) Independent per-thread scheduling is exactly what the kernel needs to know about the threads to do, which user-level threads deny it.

Common misconception. Assuming cheaper means better. The cheap switch is paid for with the loss of parallelism and of independent blocking.

Takeaway. User-level threads: cheap switch, no parallelism. Kernel threads: real parallelism, expensive switch.

OS-U1-Q62MCQhard

A process uses the many-to-many model with 4 kernel threads and 10 user-level threads. All 10 user threads are CPU-bound and make no system calls. On an 8-core machine, how many of the user threads can be executing at the same instant?

  1. 1
  2. 4
  3. 8
  4. 10
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In the many-to-many model the kernel schedules the kernel threads and the library multiplexes user threads onto them. Only a user thread that currently has a kernel thread can be on a CPU, so the instantaneous parallelism is bounded by the smaller of the kernel-thread count and the core count.

Step by step.

  1. Kernel threads available: 4.
  2. Cores available: 8.
  3. User threads can occupy at most one kernel thread each, so at most min(4, 8) = 4 run at once.
  4. The other 6 user threads are runnable inside the library but have no kernel thread to run on.

Answer: Option (B), 4.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) One would be the many-to-one answer; here there are 4 kernel threads.
  • (C) 8 is the core count, but there are only 4 kernel threads to occupy them.
  • (D) 10 is the user-thread count, which no model can run in parallel beyond its kernel threads.

Common misconception. Reading the many-to-many model as unlimited. The multiplexing ratio bounds concurrency from above; the kernel thread count is the real ceiling.

Takeaway. Parallelism = min(kernel threads, cores); the user-thread count only decides how much work is queued behind them.

preemptive-nonpreemptive-scheduling · 17
OS-U1-Q14MCQeasy

Which one of the following CPU scheduling algorithms is inherently non-preemptive?

  1. Round robin
  2. Shortest remaining time first
  3. First come first served
  4. Preemptive priority scheduling
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Preemption needs a rule that can remove a running process. FCFS has none: it dispatches the oldest ready process and lets it run until it blocks or terminates.

Step by step.

  1. FCFS hands the CPU to the process that arrived earliest and never takes it back; the only exits are blocking and termination.
  2. Round robin preempts on quantum expiry — that is its whole mechanism.
  3. SRTF preempts whenever a new arrival has a shorter remaining time.
  4. Preemptive priority preempts whenever a higher-priority process becomes ready.

Answer: Option (C) — first come first served.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (D) are all defined by their preemption rule.

Common misconception. Students sometimes call FCFS preemptive because an arriving process with a short burst 'should' run first. FCFS ignores burst lengths entirely.

Takeaway. Non-preemptive algorithms in the GATE syllabus: FCFS and non-preemptive SJF. Everything with 'remaining time', a quantum, or a preemptive priority is preemptive.

OS-U1-Q15MCQmedium

Which one of the following does NOT by itself cause the running process to be preempted?

  1. The interval timer expires the running process's time quantum.
  2. A process with a higher priority becomes ready.
  3. The I/O request of a blocked process completes, so that process becomes ready.
  4. Under SRTF, a newly arrived process has a shorter remaining burst than the running process.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Preemption means the running process is displaced by the scheduler's policy. A process merely becoming ready is not enough; the policy must also rank it above the running one.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Quantum expiry is the round-robin preemption trigger.
  2. (B) Under preemptive priority a higher-priority arrival outranks the running process, so it preempts.
  3. (C) I/O completion only moves a process from Waiting to Ready. Whether it then preempts depends on the policy; in FCFS or non-preemptive SJF it simply waits.
  4. (D) SRTF compares remaining times, so a shorter newcomer does preempt.

Answer: Option (C) — an I/O completion by itself does not preempt.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (D) are each a defined preemption trigger under the stated policy.

Common misconception. Students treat 'becomes ready' as synonymous with 'preempts'. Becoming ready is a state change; preemption is a scheduling decision on top of it.

Takeaway. Ask which policy is in force. A ready process preempts only if that policy ranks it above the running one.

OS-U1-Q16MSQmedium

Which of the following scheduling algorithms can be preemptive? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Shortest remaining time first (SRTF)
  2. Round robin
  3. Non-preemptive shortest job first (SJF)
  4. Priority scheduling in its preemptive form
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An algorithm is preemptive if it can remove a running process before that process blocks or finishes. SRTF compares remaining times, round robin uses a quantum, and preemptive priority compares priorities — all three can displace a running process.

Step by step.

  1. (A) SRTF is preemptive by definition: the shortest remaining time always runs.
  2. (B) Round robin preempts on quantum expiry.
  3. (C) Non-preemptive SJF, as its name states, lets the chosen job run to completion.
  4. (D) The preemptive form of priority scheduling displaces the running process when a higher-priority process becomes ready.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is explicitly non-preemptive; SJF has a separate preemptive version called SRTF.

Common misconception. SJF and SRTF are treated as one algorithm, so SJF is marked preemptive. They are two algorithms: SJF is non-preemptive, SRTF is its preemptive counterpart.

Takeaway. SJF ↔ SRTF is the standard non-preemptive/preemptive pair. Priority scheduling has the same pair, and round robin is preemptive by construction.

OS-U1-Q17MSQeasy

Under non-preemptive scheduling, which of the following can take the CPU away from the running process? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The process terminates.
  2. The process issues a blocking I/O request.
  3. A process with a higher priority arrives in the ready queue.
  4. The process's time quantum expires.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a non-preemptive scheme the running process gives up the CPU voluntarily. Only two events are voluntary: finishing, and blocking on something.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Termination releases the CPU, so the scheduler picks the next process.
  2. (B) A blocking request moves the process to Waiting and releases the CPU.
  3. (C) A higher-priority arrival is exactly what preemption would act on — and non-preemptive scheduling refuses to act.
  4. (D) There is no time quantum to expire in a non-preemptive algorithm.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the definition of preemption, which is absent here.
  • (D) A quantum belongs to round robin, which is preemptive.

Common misconception. Option (D) is chosen because students think every algorithm has a quantum. A quantum is a round-robin parameter, not a universal one.

Takeaway. Non-preemptive = the process keeps the CPU until it blocks or exits. Nothing else can take it away.

OS-U1-Q18NATmedium

Four processes are scheduled on one CPU by preemptive priority scheduling, where a smaller priority number means a higher priority. All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurstPriority
P10205
P2211
P3412
P4613
How many times is a running process preempted by a newly arrived process?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Preemptive priority compares the priority of the running process with that of every ready process, at every arrival. A preemption happens only when the newcomer outranks the process currently on the CPU.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: only P1 has arrived, so P1 runs.
  2. t = 2: P2 (priority 1) arrives and outranks P1 (priority 5) — P1 is preempted. Preemption 1. P2 runs for its 1 ms and finishes at t = 3.
  3. t = 3: P1 is the only ready process, so P1 resumes and runs from t = 3.
  4. t = 4: P3 (priority 2) arrives and outranks P1 (priority 5) — P1 is preempted. Preemption 2. P3 runs 1 ms and finishes at t = 5; P1 resumes.
  5. t = 6: P4 (priority 3) arrives and outranks P1 (priority 5) — P1 is preempted. Preemption 3. P4 runs 1 ms and finishes at t = 7.
  6. t = 7: P1 runs its remaining 17 ms and finishes at t = 23.
  7. Total preemptions = 3.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Counting the dispatches of P3 and P4 as preemptions is right here only because P1 happened to be running each time. The trap is the opposite error: counting a preemption when the CPU was idle or when a process had just finished, which is a fresh dispatch, not a preemption.

Takeaway. A preemption needs a victim that is actually running. Trace the timeline second by second and ask, at each arrival, whether the newcomer outranks whoever holds the CPU.

OS-U1-Q39MCQeasy

Preemptive scheduling is essential for which one of the following?

  1. Maximising the throughput of a batch system
  2. Letting an interactive process obtain the CPU promptly, because no process can hold the CPU until it blocks or exits
  3. Eliminating context switches
  4. Guaranteeing that no process ever starves
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Without preemption the running process keeps the CPU until it blocks or terminates. A single CPU-bound process would then be able to hold the CPU for seconds at a time, and an interactive process would wait that long. Preemption — by quantum expiry or by a higher-priority arrival — is what bounds that wait.

Step by step.

  1. The benefit of preemption is bounded response time, which is what an interactive system needs — option (B).
  2. Batch throughput usually favours fewer switches, so preemption does not help it.
  3. Preemption increases context switches rather than removing them.
  4. Preemption can itself cause starvation (a stream of high-priority arrivals); it does not prevent it.

Answer: Option (B) — prompt response for interactive processes.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Preemption adds switches, which costs throughput.
  • (C) It adds switches.
  • (D) Priority-based preemption is a classic cause of starvation; aging is the cure.

Common misconception. Equating preemption with fairness. Preemption bounds how long a process can hold the CPU; it says nothing about whether a particular process ever gets it.

Takeaway. Preemption buys response time and costs switch overhead. Those two are the trade-off in every preemptive design.

OS-U1-Q40MCQeasy

Which one of the following is a difficulty that preemptive scheduling introduces and non-preemptive scheduling does not?

  1. A process can be stopped in the middle of an update to shared data, so the operating system must protect its own data structures against concurrent access.
  2. A ready queue has to be maintained.
  3. Each process needs a process control block.
  4. A dispatcher has to exist.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When a process can be displaced at an arbitrary instant, the kernel itself may be interrupted while it is modifying a shared structure such as the ready queue. That is why kernels disable interrupts or use locks around such critical sections, and why user-level code needs synchronisation primitives. Non-preemptive scheduling only switches at well-defined points.

Step by step.

  1. The new hazard of preemption is an interruption at an arbitrary instant — option (A).
  2. A ready queue exists in both schemes.
  3. Every process has a PCB in both schemes.
  4. A dispatcher exists in both schemes.

Answer: Option (A) — a process may be interrupted mid-update of shared data.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B), (C) and (D) are common to both preemptive and non-preemptive scheduling.

Common misconception. Thinking preemption is free. It costs switches and it forces the kernel to treat its own data structures as critical sections.

Takeaway. Preemption is the reason a kernel needs interrupt disabling and locks around scheduler data structures — a direct link to the synchronisation unit.

OS-U1-Q41MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about preemptive and non-preemptive scheduling are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Under a strictly non-preemptive policy, a process that has started running cannot be starved of the CPU before it finishes.
  2. For the same workload, preemptive scheduling can perform more context switches than non-preemptive scheduling.
  3. Under non-preemptive SJF, a newly arrived short process may have to wait for a long process that is already running.
  4. A preemptive scheduler can never starve a process.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Non-preemptive means the CPU is released only voluntarily, so once a process is running it completes. The corollary is that a short arrival may wait behind a long runner. Preemption adds forced switches, so it can only increase the switch count. Preemption does not guarantee fairness: a stream of higher-priority arrivals can starve a low-priority process indefinitely.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true — release is voluntary only, so the runner finishes.
  2. (B) is true — quantum expiry and priority arrivals are extra switch triggers.
  3. (C) is true — that is precisely the weakness of non-preemptive SJF.
  4. (D) is false — preemptive priority scheduling without aging starves low-priority processes.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing preemption implies fairness. Preemption bounds a process's turn on the CPU, not its waiting time; only aging or a fair-share policy bounds the latter.

Takeaway. Non-preemptive = no starvation once running, but bad response. Preemptive = good response, but starvation is possible and switches cost more.

OS-U1-Q42NATmedium

Four processes are scheduled on one CPU by preemptive shortest remaining time first (SRTF). All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P109
P223
P346
P482
If a newly arrived process has exactly the same remaining time as the running process, the running process continues. How many times is the running process preempted by a newly arrived process? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SRTF preempts the running process only when a newly arriving process has a strictly shorter remaining time. Count only those forced displacements; a process that finishes normally, or that is merely passed over at the moment of a dispatch, is not a preemption.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: only P1 has arrived, so P1 runs. At t = 2 its remaining time is 7.
  2. t = 2: P2 arrives with 3 ms, which is less than 7 — P1 is preempted (count 1). P2 runs 2 → 5 and finishes.
  3. t = 5: ready are P1 (7) and P3 (6). P3 is shorter, but this is a dispatch decision after P2 finished, not a preemption.
  4. P3 runs 5 → 8. At t = 8 its remaining time is 3.
  5. t = 8: P4 arrives with 2 ms, which is less than 3 — P3 is preempted (count 2). P4 runs 8 → 10 and finishes.
  6. t = 10: P3 (3) then P1 (7) run to completion with no further arrivals.
  7. Total preemptions = 2.

Answer: 2 preemptions.

Common misconception. Counting every change of the running process. A switch made because the previous process finished or blocked is not a preemption; only an arrival that displaces a running process is. Here the P2 → P3 change at t = 5 is of that harmless kind.

Takeaway. Preemption counting is a separate skill from completion times. Fix the rule (strictly shorter remaining time) and walk the timeline once, marking only displacements.

OS-U1-Q43MCQhard

Three processes are scheduled by preemptive priority scheduling, where a smaller priority number means a higher priority. All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurstPriority
P1063
P2241
P3422
What is the completion time of P1?

  1. 8 ms
  2. 10 ms
  3. 12 ms
  4. 14 ms
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Under preemptive priority the process with the smallest priority number runs whenever it is ready, and a newly arriving higher-priority process displaces the runner immediately.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: only P1 is present, so P1 runs. At t = 2 it has used 2 ms and 4 ms remain.
  2. t = 2: P2 arrives with priority 1, higher than P1's 3 — P2 preempts P1 and runs 2 → 6, finishing at 6.
  3. t = 4: P3 arrives with priority 2. P2 has priority 1, which is higher, so P3 does not preempt.
  4. t = 6: ready are P1 (priority 3, 4 ms left) and P3 (priority 2, 2 ms). P3 is higher, so P3 runs 6 → 8 and finishes.
  5. t = 8: only P1 remains; it runs its last 4 ms, 8 → 12.
  6. P1 completes at 12 ms.

Answer: Option (C) — P1 completes at 12 ms.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 8 ms is when P3 completes.
  • (B) 10 ms results from letting P3 preempt P2 at t = 4, which priority 1 forbids.
  • (D) 14 ms results from running P1 to completion before P3 is considered at all.

Common misconception. Letting any new arrival preempt. Preemption needs a strictly higher priority; P3's priority 2 cannot displace P2's priority 1.

Takeaway. Trace preemptive priority arrival by arrival and compare the newcomer's priority with the runner's each time; do not assume every arrival preempts.

OS-U1-Q44NAThard

Four processes are scheduled by preemptive priority scheduling, where a smaller priority number means a higher priority. All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurstPriority
P1084
P2141
P3293
P4352
What is the average waiting time? (Enter a number; decimals are allowed.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Waiting time = completion time − arrival time − burst time. Under preemptive priority the highest priority ready process always runs, so the timeline has to be built one arrival at a time.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: only P1 is present, so P1 runs. At t = 1 it has used 1 ms and 7 ms remain.
  2. t = 1: P2 arrives with priority 1, higher than 4 — P2 preempts and runs 1 → 5, finishing at 5.
  3. t = 2: P3 (priority 3) arrives, but P2's priority 1 is higher, so nothing changes.
  4. t = 3: P4 (priority 2) arrives, again lower than P2's 1, so nothing changes.
  5. t = 5: ready are P1 (priority 4, 7 ms left), P3 (priority 3, 9 ms) and P4 (priority 2, 5 ms). P4 has the highest priority, so P4 runs 5 → 10 and finishes at 10.
  6. t = 10: P3 (priority 3) beats P1 (priority 4); P3 runs 10 → 19 and finishes.
  7. t = 19: P1 runs 19 → 26 and finishes.
  8. Waiting times: P1 = 26 − 0 − 8 = 18; P2 = 5 − 1 − 4 = 0; P3 = 19 − 2 − 9 = 8; P4 = 10 − 3 − 5 = 2.
  9. Average = (18 + 0 + 8 + 2) / 4 = 28 / 4 = 7 ms.

Answer: 7 ms.

Common misconception. Forgetting the arrival times when computing waiting time. P2 arrives at t = 1, so its waiting time is 5 − 1 − 4 = 0, not 5 − 4 = 1.

Takeaway. Build the Gantt chart first, read off completion times, then apply WT = C − A − B to every process in one pass.

OS-U1-Q63MCQeasy

Which one of the following is TRUE of non-preemptive scheduling?

  1. A process keeps the CPU until it blocks or terminates.
  2. A process is removed from the CPU as soon as a higher-priority process becomes ready.
  3. It needs a periodic timer interrupt in order to function.
  4. It always produces a lower average waiting time than preemptive scheduling.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Non-preemptive means the scheduler runs only when the running process gives up the CPU voluntarily — by blocking on I/O, by calling exit(), or by yielding.

Step by step.

  1. The dispatcher hands the CPU to a process.
  2. The process keeps it; no arrival of another process can displace it.
  3. Only a voluntary relinquish returns control to the scheduler.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Displacing a running process on a higher-priority arrival is precisely preemption.
  • (C) A timer is what enables preemption; a non-preemptive scheduler does not need one.
  • (D) No such general claim holds; preemptive SRTF has the minimum average waiting time.

Common misconception. Believing non-preemptive means no interrupts. Device interrupts still occur; they just do not cause a scheduling decision for the running process.

Takeaway. Preemption is about who may take the CPU away — only the running process itself, or the kernel as well.

OS-U1-Q64MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about response time are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Response time is measured from submission of the process to the first instant it gets the CPU.
