  2. For a long queue of CPU-bound jobs, preemptive round robin bounds the worst-case response time, while first come first served does not.
  3. Response time and waiting time are the same quantity.
  4. Under a non-preemptive scheduler the response time of a newly arrived process can be as large as the longest remaining burst in the system.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Response time is the delay before the first CPU service; waiting time is the total time spent in the ready queue. For a process that is dispatched once and never preempted they coincide, which is why they are so often confused.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: that is the definition.
  2. (B) True: with quantum q and n ready processes, a new arrival waits at most (n − 1)·q before its first slice; under FCFS it waits for every burst queued ahead of it.
  3. (C) False: a process preempted many times has a large waiting time but its response time was small.
  4. (D) True: a new arrival cannot start until the running process gives up the CPU, which may take as long as its remaining burst.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other option is wrong.

  • (C) They differ exactly when preemption occurs, which is the interesting case.

Common misconception. Treating response time as an average. It is usually quoted as a worst case, because an interactive user cares about the longest stall, not the mean.

Takeaway. Response time = first service; waiting time = all ready-queue time. Preemption improves the first and can worsen the second.

OS-U1-Q65NATmedium

Four processes arrive as shown and are scheduled by preemptive shortest remaining time first (SRTF). All times are in milliseconds. How many times is a running process preempted? Do not count the initial dispatch of the first process.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P109
P225
P342
P461
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SRTF compares remaining times at every arrival instant. A preemption occurs only when a newly arrived process has strictly less remaining time than the running one; the running process is never displaced by one that is merely equal or longer.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: only P1 exists, so P1 runs. Remaining: P1 = 9.
  2. t = 2: P2 arrives with burst 5. P1 has 7 left, and 5 < 7, so P2 preempts P1. Preemption 1.
  3. t = 4: P2 has 3 left; P3 arrives with burst 2, and 2 < 3, so P3 preempts P2. Preemption 2.
  4. t = 6: P3 completes. P4 arrives at t = 6 with burst 1. Waiting are P2 with 3 left and P1 with 7 left; 1 is the smallest, so P4 runs — but P3 had already finished, so this is a dispatch, not a preemption.
  5. t = 7: P4 completes. P2 (3 left) runs to t = 10.
  6. t = 10: P1 runs its remaining 7 ms and completes at t = 17.

Answer: 2 preemptions.

Common misconception. Counting every change of the running process. A dispatch after the previous process completed is not a preemption; the count is only of processes displaced while still runnable.

Takeaway. Draw the Gantt chart and mark only those boundaries at which the outgoing process still had work left.

OS-U1-Q66MCQeasy

Why does a preemptive scheduler need a hardware timer?

  1. To measure the burst time of each process.
  2. To regain control of the CPU from a process that neither blocks nor terminates.
  3. To choose the next process when several are ready.
  4. To save the context of the preempted process.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The kernel runs only when it is entered — by a system call, an exception or an interrupt. A CPU-bound process that makes no system calls never enters the kernel on its own, so without a periodic timer interrupt the kernel could never take the CPU back.

Step by step.

  1. The timer is programmed to interrupt after the quantum.
  2. The interrupt transfers control to the kernel's handler.
  3. The handler charges the running process, marks the quantum expired and invokes the scheduler.
  4. The scheduler may then switch to another process.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Burst times are not measured by the timer; they are observed or predicted.
  • (C) Selection is the scheduler's job, not the timer's.
  • (D) The context switch routine saves the context; the timer only triggers it.

Common misconception. Thinking the timer chooses the next process. It only guarantees the kernel gets a chance to choose.

Takeaway. The timer is the mechanism that makes preemption possible; the scheduler is the policy that uses it.

OS-U1-Q67MCQhard

Which one of the following statements comparing preemptive shortest remaining time first (SRTF) with non-preemptive shortest job first (SJF) is TRUE?

  1. For the same set of processes, SRTF never has a larger average waiting time than non-preemptive SJF.
  2. For the same set of processes, non-preemptive SJF never has a larger average waiting time than SRTF.
  3. The two always give the same average waiting time, because both always pick the shortest job.
  4. SRTF can only be implemented as one level of a multilevel feedback queue.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SRTF is the optimal algorithm for average waiting time among all schedulers that must decide without knowing future arrivals. It re-decides at every arrival, so its set of feasible schedules contains every schedule non-preemptive SJF can produce, and it always takes the best of them.

Step by step.

  1. Non-preemptive SJF fixes an order when the CPU is free; a long job that starts cannot be interrupted by a short arrival.
  2. SRTF interrupts it, which can only move short jobs earlier.
  3. Shortening the jobs that run early cannot increase the total waiting time, so SRTF's average is at most SJF's — and strictly smaller whenever a short job arrives during a long one.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the containment: SJF cannot beat the optimum.
  • (C) They differ whenever an arrival has a shorter remaining time than the running process.
  • (D) SRTF is an ordinary priority scheduler with remaining time as the key; a multilevel feedback queue only approximates it when bursts are unknown.

Common misconception. Assuming optimality carries over to turnaround time. SRTF minimises average waiting time; it can increase turnaround for long jobs.

Takeaway. SRTF ≤ SJF on average waiting time, always, and SRTF can starve the long job that SJF would at least have started.

OS-U1-Q68MCQmedium

Two processes update a shared counter with an ordinary read-modify-write sequence and no synchronisation. Which one of the following failures becomes possible only because the scheduler is preemptive?

  1. A page fault on the page holding the counter.
  2. A deadlock in which each process waits for a resource the other holds.
  3. A process being stopped because its time quantum has expired.
  4. A lost update: both processes read the same old value and one of the increments is overwritten.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A read-modify-write is not atomic. If the scheduler can stop a process between the read and the write, another process can read the same old value, and one of the two increments disappears. Under non-preemptive scheduling a process runs that sequence to completion, so the interleaving cannot occur.

Step by step.

  1. Both processes read the counter, say 5.
  2. P1 is preempted before it writes.
  3. P2 computes 6 and writes 6.
  4. P1 resumes, computes 6 from its stale 5 and writes 6. One increment is lost.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Page faults arise from memory access patterns, not from the scheduling policy.
  • (B) Deadlock needs each process to hold one resource and wait for another; it can happen with or without preemption.
  • (C) Quantum expiry is the normal operation of preemption, not a failure caused by it.

Common misconception. Believing that a single machine instruction is automatically atomic with respect to other processes. Only genuinely atomic instructions, or critical sections, give that guarantee.

Takeaway. Preemption turns any non-atomic shared update into a race; that is why critical sections exist.

fcfs-sjf-srtf-roundrobin-priority-multilevel · 20
OS-U1-Q19MCQeasy

Three processes arrive as shown and are scheduled by first come first served. All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P103
P216
P324
What is the average waiting time?

  1. 3 ms
  2. 4 ms
  3. 5 ms
  4. 7 ms
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Under FCFS the processes run in arrival order without interruption. Waiting time is turnaround time minus burst time, and turnaround time is completion time minus arrival time.

Step by step.

  1. P1 runs from 0 to 3: completion 3, waiting time 3 − 0 − 3 = 0.
  2. P2 runs from 3 to 9: completion 9, waiting time 9 − 1 − 6 = 2.
  3. P3 runs from 9 to 13: completion 13, waiting time 13 − 2 − 4 = 7.
  4. Average = (0 + 2 + 7) / 3 = 9 / 3 = 3 ms.

Answer: Option (A) — 3 ms.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) comes from forgetting to subtract the arrival time of P2 and P3.
  • (C) and (D) come from adding arrival times instead of subtracting them.

Common misconception. Using arrival time as the start time for every process. P2 does not start at t = 1; it starts when P1 releases the CPU.

Takeaway. Waiting time = completion − arrival − burst. Always compute completion times first.

OS-U1-Q20MCQeasy

The convoy effect — several short CPU-bound processes queued behind one long CPU-bound process, producing a poor average waiting time — is most closely associated with which algorithm?

  1. Round robin
  2. First come first served
  3. Shortest remaining time first
  4. Multilevel feedback queue
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The convoy effect is a direct consequence of serving processes in arrival order regardless of length, which is exactly what FCFS does.

Step by step.

  1. Under FCFS one long job at the head of the queue makes every short job behind it wait.
  2. The short jobs also hold up the I/O device they use only briefly, so device utilisation suffers too.
  3. SRTF would move the short jobs ahead, and round robin would interleave them, so neither produces the convoy.
  4. Hence the convoy effect belongs to FCFS.

Answer: Option (B) — first come first served.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Round robin interleaves jobs and bounds the wait by the quantum.
  • (C) SRTF always runs the shortest remaining job, which dissolves the convoy.
  • (D) Multilevel feedback queues promote short jobs, which also dissolves it.

Common misconception. The convoy effect is sometimes attached to SJF because SJF is 'the fix'. SJF avoids the convoy; FCFS causes it.

Takeaway. Convoy effect = FCFS + one long job at the head. The standard remedies are SJF, SRTF and round robin.

OS-U1-Q21MCQmedium

Three processes arrive at time t = 0 with CPU bursts of 12 ms, 18 ms and 30 ms. The scheduler knows the burst lengths in advance. What is the minimum achievable average waiting time for these three processes under a non-preemptive scheduler?

  1. 12 ms
  2. 14 ms
  3. 16 ms
  4. 18 ms
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For a fixed set of processes with no arrival spread, the non-preemptive algorithm that minimises average waiting time is shortest job first: run the shortest burst first and the longest last.

Step by step.

  1. Order the bursts: 12, 18, 30.
  2. SJF runs them in that order. Waiting times: first job 0, second 12, third 12 + 18 = 30.
  3. Average = (0 + 12 + 30) / 3 = 42 / 3 = 14 ms.
  4. Any other order increases the sum, so 14 ms is the minimum.

Answer: Option (B) — 14 ms.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 12 ms is the waiting time of the second job alone.
  • (C) and (D) come from averaging turnaround times, or from running the jobs in arrival order and dividing incorrectly.

Common misconception. Averaging turnaround times instead of waiting times. With all arrivals at t = 0 the turnaround times are 12, 30, 60, whose average is 34 ms — a different quantity.

Takeaway. All arriving together + non-preemptive + 'minimum average waiting time' means sort the bursts ascending and accumulate: 0, b₁, b₁ + b₂.

OS-U1-Q22MCQhard

Three processes arrive as shown and are scheduled by round robin with a time quantum of 2 ms. Processes that arrive together join the ready queue in arrival order, and a process whose quantum expires joins the back of the queue. All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P105
P213
P321
What is the completion time of P1?

  1. 7 ms
  2. 8 ms
  3. 9 ms
  4. 11 ms
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Simulate round robin one quantum at a time. Keep a FIFO ready queue and note that the completion time is the instant the last unit of a process's burst finishes.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0–2: P1 runs its first quantum, 2 ms, leaving 3 ms. P2 arrived at t = 1 and is already queued.
  2. t = 2–4: P1's quantum has expired and it goes to the back, so P2 runs 2 ms, leaving 1 ms. P3 arrived at t = 2 and is queued behind P1.
  3. t = 4–6: P1 runs its second quantum, 2 ms, leaving 1 ms.
  4. t = 6–7: P3 runs its 1 ms and completes at t = 7.
  5. t = 7–8: P2 runs its last 1 ms and completes at t = 8.
  6. t = 8–9: P1 runs its last 1 ms and completes at t = 9.
  7. Completion time of P1 = 9 ms.

Answer: Option (C) — 9 ms.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 7 ms is the completion time of P3.
  • (B) 8 ms is the completion time of P2.
  • (D) 11 ms comes from giving P1 an extra quantum somewhere in the trace.

Common misconception. Charging the first quantum from t = 0 to t = 1 for P1. The quantum is 2 ms, so P1 runs from 0 to 2, and P2 — which arrives at t = 1 — must wait.

Takeaway. Write the queue state after every quantum. For round robin, the answer is in the bookkeeping, not in the formula.

OS-U1-Q23MSQhard

Which of the following statements about CPU scheduling algorithms are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. SRTF can starve a long process indefinitely if short processes keep arriving.
  2. Increasing the time quantum of round robin increases the number of context switches.
  3. Among non-preemptive algorithms, SJF minimises the average waiting time.
  4. FCFS always gives the minimum average turnaround time for a given set of processes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Starvation, quantum size and SJF optimality are the three classic scheduling properties; FCFS optimality is the classic false claim.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: SRTF always picks the shortest remaining time, so a long job loses to every short arrival and may never run.
  2. (B) False: a larger quantum means fewer expiries, hence fewer context switches — with a quantum larger than every burst, round robin degenerates into FCFS.
  3. (C) True: SJF is provably optimal for average waiting time among non-preemptive algorithms, by an exchange argument on adjacent jobs.
  4. (D) False: FCFS can be arbitrarily bad — the convoy effect — so it cannot be optimal.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the relationship: the number of switches falls as the quantum grows.
  • (D) is false; SJF, not FCFS, is the optimal non-preemptive rule for waiting time.

Common misconception. Option (B) is chosen because a smaller quantum is associated with 'more responsive', which students turn into 'more switches when the quantum grows'. It is the other way round.

Takeaway. Quantum up → switches down, response worse. Quantum down → switches up, overhead up. SJF is optimal for waiting time; nothing is optimal for turnaround in general except in special cases.

OS-U1-Q24NATmedium

Four processes arrive as shown and are scheduled by preemptive shortest remaining time first (SRTF). All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P107
P224
P341
P454
What is the average waiting time?
(Enter a number; decimals are allowed.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Under SRTF the process with the smallest remaining burst always holds the CPU, and the comparison is repeated at every arrival. Waiting time = completion − arrival − burst.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: P1 runs. At t = 2 its remaining time is 5.
  2. t = 2: P2 arrives with burst 4 < 5, so P2 preempts P1.
  3. t = 4: P3 arrives with burst 1 < P2's remaining 2, so P3 preempts P2.
  4. t = 5: P3 completes. Remaining: P2 = 2, P1 = 5, P4 = 4 (P4 arrived at t = 5). P2 runs.
  5. t = 7: P2 completes. Remaining: P4 = 4, P1 = 5. P4 runs.
  6. t = 11: P4 completes. P1 runs its remaining 5 ms and completes at t = 16.
  7. Completion times: P1 = 16, P2 = 7, P3 = 5, P4 = 11.
  8. Waiting times: P1 = 16 − 0 − 7 = 9; P2 = 7 − 2 − 4 = 1; P3 = 5 − 4 − 1 = 0; P4 = 11 − 5 − 4 = 2.
  9. Average = (9 + 1 + 0 + 2) / 4 = 12 / 4 = 3 ms.

Answer: 3 ms.

Common misconception. Letting P1 finish its first stretch because it started first. SRTF is preemptive: P2's burst of 4 beats P1's remaining 5 at t = 2, so P1 is displaced.

Takeaway. Re-evaluate the shortest remaining time at every arrival instant, not only at completions.

OS-U1-Q25NAThard

Four processes arrive as shown and are scheduled by preemptive shortest remaining time first (SRTF). All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P106
P212
P321
P433
What is the average turnaround time?
(Enter a number; decimals are allowed.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Turnaround time is completion time minus arrival time; it includes both the waiting and the execution of the process. Under SRTF the schedule is rebuilt at every arrival.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: P1 runs. At t = 1 its remaining time is 5.
  2. t = 1: P2 arrives with burst 2 < 5, so P2 preempts P1.
  3. t = 2: P3 arrives with burst 1, which ties P2's remaining time of 1. The running process P2 continues (the tie does not change the totals either way).
  4. t = 3: P2 completes. Ready: P3 = 1, P4 = 3 (arrived at t = 3), P1 = 5. P3 runs.
  5. t = 4: P3 completes. P4 = 3 < P1 = 5, so P4 runs from t = 4 to t = 7.
  6. t = 7: P4 completes. P1 runs its remaining 5 ms and completes at t = 12.
  7. Completion times: P1 = 12, P2 = 3, P3 = 4, P4 = 7.
  8. Turnaround times: P1 = 12 − 0 = 12; P2 = 3 − 1 = 2; P3 = 4 − 2 = 2; P4 = 7 − 3 = 4.
  9. Average = (12 + 2 + 2 + 4) / 4 = 20 / 4 = 5 ms.

Answer: 5 ms.

Common misconception. Reporting the average waiting time instead. Here the waiting times are 6, 0, 1, 1, whose average is 2 ms — a different number, and the usual wrong answer.

Takeaway. Turnaround = completion − arrival. Waiting = turnaround − burst. Read which one the question asks for before dividing.

OS-U1-Q45MCQeasy

Four processes are scheduled by first come first served. All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P105
P213
P328
P442
What is the average turnaround time?

  1. 8.25 ms
  2. 9.50 ms
  3. 10 ms
  4. 11.25 ms
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FCFS runs the processes in arrival order without interruption. Turnaround time = completion time − arrival time.

Step by step.

  1. P1 runs 0 → 5; completion 5, turnaround 5 − 0 = 5.
  2. P2 runs 5 → 8; completion 8, turnaround 8 − 1 = 7.
  3. P3 runs 8 → 16; completion 16, turnaround 16 − 2 = 14.
  4. P4 runs 16 → 18; completion 18, turnaround 18 − 4 = 14.
  5. Average = (5 + 7 + 14 + 14) / 4 = 40 / 4 = 10 ms.

Answer: Option (C) — 10 ms.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 8.25 ms is the average turnaround under non-preemptive SJF, not FCFS.
  • (B) and (D) come from using start times in place of completion times.

Common misconception. Using the arrival time as the start time. P3 arrives at t = 2 but does not start until t = 8, and the turnaround time is measured from arrival, not from start.

Takeaway. Turnaround counts the whole wait plus the run; waiting time subtracts the burst. Keep the two formulas apart.

OS-U1-Q46MCQeasy

The same four processes are now scheduled by non-preemptive shortest job first (SJF). All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P105
P213
P328
P442
What is the average waiting time?

  1. 2.75 ms
  2. 3.75 ms
  3. 4.50 ms
  4. 5.50 ms
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Non-preemptive SJF picks, at each completion, the arrived process with the smallest burst. Waiting time = completion time − arrival time − burst time.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: only P1 has arrived, so P1 must run 0 → 5. At t = 5 the remaining processes P2 (3), P3 (8) and P4 (2) have all arrived.
  2. Shortest is P4 with 2 ms, so P4 runs 5 → 7.
  3. Next shortest is P2 with 3 ms, so P2 runs 7 → 10.
  4. P3 runs 10 → 18.
  5. Waiting times: P1 = 5 − 0 − 5 = 0; P4 = 7 − 4 − 2 = 1; P2 = 10 − 1 − 3 = 6; P3 = 18 − 2 − 8 = 8.
  6. Average = (0 + 1 + 6 + 8) / 4 = 15 / 4 = 3.75 ms.

Answer: Option (B) — 3.75 ms.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 2.75 ms comes from forgetting P1's forced first run.
  • (C) 4.50 ms comes from choosing the shortest arrival instead of the shortest burst.
  • (D) 5.50 ms is the average waiting time under FCFS for the same table.

Common misconception. Applying SJF at t = 0 as though all four processes were already available. Only P1 has arrived at t = 0, so SJF has no choice at the start — that is why non-preemptive SJF and preemptive SRTF differ.

Takeaway. SJF with arrivals must be applied at each completion instant over the processes that have arrived by then.

OS-U1-Q47MSQhard

Which of the following statements about multilevel and multilevel feedback queue scheduling are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. In a multilevel feedback queue, a process that has used a great deal of CPU time is usually moved to a lower-priority queue.
  2. A multilevel queue with absolute priority between its queues can starve the lower-priority queues.
  3. In a multilevel feedback queue, a process that has been demoted to a lower queue can never return to a higher one.
  4. Aging — gradually raising the priority of a waiting process — can prevent starvation in a priority-based multilevel queue.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A multilevel feedback queue lets processes move between queues. The usual rule is that a process which exhausts its quantum is demoted, so CPU-bound work sinks and interactive work stays high. With absolute priority between queues, lower queues run only when higher ones are empty, which starves them; aging, or time slicing between queues, is the standard cure.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true — demotion on quantum exhaustion is the defining feedback rule.
  2. (B) is true — absolute priority means the low queue waits for every high-queue arrival.
  3. (C) is false — promotion is normally allowed (by aging or by a return to interactive behaviour), and it is what makes the scheme starvation-free.
  4. (D) is true — that is exactly what aging does.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading "feedback" as "one-way". The feedback in the name is the movement of processes between queues, and it normally runs in both directions; a scheme that only ever demotes would starve its bottom queue.

Takeaway. Multilevel queue = fixed assignment, absolute priority, starvation possible. Multilevel feedback queue = migration between queues, and the cure for starvation is aging.

OS-U1-Q48NATmedium

Three processes are scheduled by round robin with a time quantum of 4 ms. A process that arrives at the same instant as a quantum expires joins the ready queue before the preempted process is put back at the end. All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P106
P213
P327
What is the average turnaround time? (Enter a number; decimals are allowed.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Round robin gives each process at most one quantum, then sends it to the back of the FIFO ready queue. Turnaround time = completion time − arrival time.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: only P1 has arrived, so P1 runs. It uses its quantum 0 → 4 with 2 ms left.
  2. t = 4: the queue holds P2 (arrived 1) and P3 (arrived 2), and P1 is put back behind them — order P2, P3, P1.
  3. P2 runs 4 → 7 and finishes (burst 3 is less than the quantum).
  4. P3 runs 7 → 11 and uses its full quantum, with 3 ms left.
  5. P1 runs 11 → 13 and finishes.
  6. P3 runs 13 → 16 and finishes.
  7. Completion times: P1 = 13, P2 = 7, P3 = 16.
  8. Turnaround times: P1 = 13 − 0 = 13; P2 = 7 − 1 = 6; P3 = 16 − 2 = 14.
  9. Average = (13 + 6 + 14) / 3 = 33 / 3 = 11 ms.

Answer: 11 ms.

Common misconception. Putting the preempted process back at the front of the queue, or admitting arrivals only at the start of the next quantum. The stated rule puts arrivals first and the preempted process last; that is what makes P2 run before P1 at t = 4.

Takeaway. Write the ready queue down at every quantum boundary. Round robin is a queue discipline before it is an arithmetic problem.

OS-U1-Q49MCQmedium

A two-level multilevel feedback queue gives the top queue a time quantum of 8 ms and runs the bottom queue by FCFS. A process that exhausts its quantum in the top queue is demoted to the bottom queue, and demoted processes are never promoted. A single process that needs 20 ms of CPU arrives to an otherwise idle system. How many times does it run in the top queue?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The top queue gives one quantum. Exhausting it demotes the process, and with no promotion it never returns, so the answer is one run in the top queue no matter how long the process is.

Step by step.

  1. The process runs in the top queue for its full 8 ms quantum; 12 ms remain.
  2. Exhausting the quantum demotes it to the bottom queue.
  3. The bottom queue is FCFS and never promotes, so it runs the remaining 12 ms there.
  4. Number of runs in the top queue = 1.

Answer: Option (A) — 1 run in the top queue.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) would need the process to return to the top queue after 8 ms, which requires promotion.
  • (C) and (D) would need repeated promotion, which the scheme explicitly does not allow.

Common misconception. Dividing 20 by 8 and rounding up, giving 3. The number of quanta is not the number of top-queue runs, because after the first demotion the process is in a different queue.

Takeaway. In a feedback queue, count runs per queue, not quanta. A one-way scheme answers with 1 for any process that needs more than one quantum.

OS-U1-Q50NAThard

Four processes are scheduled by round robin with a time quantum of 2 ms. A process that arrives at the same instant as a quantum expires joins the ready queue before the preempted process is put back at the end. All times are in milliseconds.

ProcessArrivalBurst
P105
P214
P322
P433
What is the average turnaround time? (Enter a number; decimals are allowed.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Round robin with a 2 ms quantum, a FIFO ready queue, arrivals admitted before a preempted process is re-queued. Turnaround time = completion time − arrival time.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0: P1 runs 0 → 2, 3 ms left. At t = 1 P2 arrived and at t = 2 P3 arrives, so the queue becomes P2, P3 and then P1 — order P2, P3, P1.
  2. P2 runs 2 → 4, 2 ms left. P4 arrives at t = 3; at t = 4 the queue becomes P3, P1, P4 and then P2.
  3. P3 runs 4 → 6 and finishes; completion 6.
  4. P1 runs 6 → 8, 1 ms left; it goes to the back — queue P4, P2, P1.
  5. P4 runs 8 → 10, 1 ms left; queue P2, P1, P4.
  6. P2 runs 10 → 12 and finishes; completion 12.
  7. P1 runs 12 → 13 and finishes; completion 13.
  8. P4 runs 13 → 14 and finishes; completion 14.
  9. Turnaround: P1 = 13 − 0 = 13; P2 = 12 − 1 = 11; P3 = 6 − 2 = 4; P4 = 14 − 3 = 11.
  10. Average = (13 + 11 + 4 + 11) / 4 = 39 / 4 = 9.75 ms.

Answer: 9.75 ms.

Common misconception. Re-queueing the preempted process ahead of the arrivals, which changes the whole order and gives a different total. Fix the tie-break rule from the question before tracing, and apply it at every quantum boundary.

Takeaway. For round robin, the ready-queue order at each boundary decides everything. Trace it explicitly, one quantum at a time, and read completion times off the trace.

OS-U1-Q69MCQeasy

Round robin is run with a time quantum that is at least as large as the longest burst in the ready queue. Which algorithm does it then behave like?

  1. First come first served
  2. Shortest job first
  3. Shortest remaining time first
  4. Priority scheduling
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Round robin preempts only when the quantum expires. If the quantum is longer than any burst, no process is ever preempted, so each runs to completion in arrival order — which is exactly FCFS.

Step by step.

  1. A process is dispatched from the head of the ready queue.
  2. It finishes before the quantum expires, so it is never preempted.
  3. The next process is taken from the head, which is the earliest arrival.
  4. The order is therefore arrival order: FCFS.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) and (C) require comparing burst or remaining lengths, which round robin never does.
  • (D) requires a priority field, which round robin ignores.

Common misconception. Thinking a huge quantum is harmless. It removes the responsiveness that round robin exists to provide.

Takeaway. Quantum → ∞ is FCFS; quantum → 0 is processor sharing with unbounded switch overhead.

OS-U1-Q70NATmedium

Three processes arrive as shown and are scheduled by round robin with a time quantum of 2 ms. A process that arrives at the same instant a quantum expires is placed in the queue ahead of the process that has just been preempted. All times are in milliseconds. What is the average waiting time?

ProcessArrivalBurst
P106
P224
P342
(Enter a number; decimals are allowed.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Under round robin the preempted process goes to the tail of the ready queue, so every runnable process gets one slice before it gets a second. Waiting time = completion − arrival − burst.

Step by step.

  1. t = 0–2: P1 runs (6 → 4 left).
  2. t = 2–4: P2 runs (4 → 2 left). P1 rejoined the queue behind P2.
  3. t = 4–6: P3 arrived at t = 4, and it is queued ahead of the just-preempted P2; P3 runs (2 → 0, completes at 6).
  4. t = 6–8: P1 runs (4 → 2 left).
  5. t = 8–10: P2 runs (2 → 0, completes at 10).
  6. t = 10–12: P1 runs (2 → 0, completes at 12).
  7. Completion times: P1 = 12, P2 = 10, P3 = 8.
  8. Waiting times: P1 = 12 − 0 − 6 = 6; P2 = 10 − 2 − 4 = 4; P3 = 8 − 4 − 2 = 2.
  9. Average = (6 + 4 + 2) / 3 = 12 / 3 = 4 ms.

Answer: 4 ms.

Common misconception. Appending the preempted process ahead of the newcomer. Round robin puts the preempted process at the tail; the newcomer joins ahead of it, which is what gives the newcomer its first slice at t = 4.

Takeaway. Draw the queue explicitly at each instant; the tie-break rule at an arrival boundary decides the whole schedule.

OS-U1-Q71MCQeasy

Which one of the following correctly relates turnaround time, waiting time and burst time?

  1. Waiting = completion − arrival; turnaround = waiting − burst.
  2. Response time = turnaround − waiting time.
  3. Turnaround = completion − arrival; waiting = turnaround − burst.
  4. Waiting time counts only the interval before the first dispatch.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Turnaround time is the total time from arrival to completion. Waiting time is that total minus the time actually spent executing, which is the burst.

Step by step.

  1. Turnaround = completion time − arrival time.
  2. Turnaround = waiting time + burst time, because a process either waits or runs.
  3. Therefore waiting time = turnaround − burst.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) swaps the two definitions.
  • (B) is not an identity: response time is the first-service delay, unrelated to turnaround minus waiting in general.
  • (D) describes response time, not waiting time.

Common misconception. Using completion − arrival as the waiting time and forgetting to subtract the burst. For a preempted process the two differ substantially.

Takeaway. One identity does most of the work: turnaround = waiting + burst, with turnaround = completion − arrival.

OS-U1-Q72NATmedium

A scheduler predicts the next burst of a process by exponential averaging, τn+1 = α tn + (1 − α) τn. The initial prediction is τ0 = 10 ms, the next two actual bursts are 14 ms and 6 ms, and α = 0.5. What is the prediction after these two observations, in ms?
(Enter a number; decimals are allowed.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Exponential averaging folds the newest observation into the old estimate with weight α, so recent behaviour dominates when α is large and history dominates when α is small. It is how SJF is approximated without knowing the next burst.

Step by step.

  1. τ1 = 0.5 × 14 + 0.5 × 10 = 7 + 5 = 12 ms.
  2. τ2 = 0.5 × 6 + 0.5 × 12 = 3 + 6 = 9 ms.

Answer: 9 ms.

Common misconception. Applying the formula to the initial estimate instead of the previous prediction — that is, computing 0.5 × 6 + 0.5 × 10 = 8. Each step uses the prediction produced by the previous step, not τ0.

Takeaway. Update once per observation, carrying the new τ forward; the estimate lags the true burst by a factor that shrinks as α grows.

OS-U1-Q73MCQhard

A low-priority process holds a lock that a high-priority process needs, and a medium-priority process that needs no lock is runnable. The holder is repeatedly preempted by the medium-priority process, so the high-priority process waits far longer than its priority warrants. What is this situation called, and what is the standard remedy?

  1. Starvation; aging steadily raises the priority of every waiting process.
  2. Deadlock; the scheduler breaks the lock and restarts the holder.
  3. The convoy effect; the scheduler falls back to first come first served.
  4. Priority inversion; priority inheritance temporarily raises the lock holder's priority to that of the highest waiter.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Priority inversion is a high-priority task being delayed by a lower-priority one that holds a resource it needs, made worse by an unrelated middle-priority task. Priority inheritance makes the holder run at the waiter's priority until it releases the lock, so the middle-priority task can no longer preempt it.

Step by step.

  1. L holds the lock and is preempted by M, which is runnable and has higher priority than L.
  2. H becomes ready, but cannot proceed because the lock is held.
  3. L cannot run because M keeps taking the CPU, so H waits for M — a task of lower priority than itself.
  4. With inheritance, L runs at H's priority while it holds the lock, so M cannot preempt it and L releases the lock quickly.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Aging addresses a process that never gets the CPU; here the problem is a lock held by a process that is being preempted.
  • (B) There is no circular wait, so this is not deadlock.
  • (C) The convoy effect is short jobs queuing behind a long one, not a lock dependency.

Common misconception. Calling it deadlock. Nothing is permanently stuck: the high-priority task would finish if the medium-priority one were not interfering.

Takeaway. Priority inversion needs a lock, three priority levels and preemption; priority inheritance removes the third factor's effect.

OS-U1-Q74MSQhard

Which of the following statements about priority scheduling are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. With static priorities, a low-priority process can be starved indefinitely by a steady stream of higher-priority arrivals.
  2. Aging raises the priority of a process the longer it waits, which bounds the wait.
  3. In UNIX-like systems a smaller numeric priority value conventionally means a higher scheduling priority.
  4. Priority scheduling can never be preemptive, because the priority is fixed when the process is created.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Priority scheduling picks the highest-priority ready process. Its two classic problems are starvation and the preemptive/non-preemptive choice; its standard cure for starvation is aging.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: if arrivals of higher priority never stop, the low-priority process is never selected.
  2. (B) True: aging increments the priority of a waiting process, so eventually it outranks every arrival and its wait is bounded.
  3. (C) True: UNIX and Linux nice values run from −20 (most favourable) to +19 (least favourable), so smaller means higher priority.
  4. (D) False: priority scheduling is commonly preemptive — a higher-priority arrival displaces the running process.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other option is wrong.

  • (D) confuses the value of the priority with the policy; whether priorities are static has nothing to do with whether the scheduler preempts.

Common misconception. Reading "smaller number, higher priority" as a universal rule. It is a UNIX convention; many textbooks use the opposite, so always check the question's stated convention.

Takeaway. Priority scheduling is preemptive or not by configuration; aging is the standard defence against starvation.

OS-U1-Q75MSQmedium

A multilevel queue scheduler keeps separate queues with absolute priority between them, and a process never moves from one queue to another. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A process in a lower-priority queue can be starved by a continuous stream of arrivals in a higher-priority queue.
  2. It differs from a multilevel feedback queue in that a process does not migrate between queues.
  3. Any scheduling algorithm may be used inside each queue.
  4. It guarantees a bounded response time for every process.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A multilevel queue partitions the ready set permanently — typically foreground, background, system — and services the queues in a fixed priority order. Each queue may use its own internal algorithm. The multilevel feedback queue is the variant that lets processes move between queues.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True: absolute priority means a lower queue is served only when every higher queue is empty, so a continuous higher-priority stream starves it.
  2. (B) True: that is precisely the difference — feedback queues demote and promote.
  3. (C) True: round robin in the interactive queue and FCFS in the batch queue is the standard arrangement.
  4. (D) False: a lower-priority process has no bound at all under absolute priority.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other option is wrong.

  • (D) contradicts (A); only a time-sliced share between queues would give a bound.

Common misconception. Treating multilevel queue and multilevel feedback queue as synonyms. The word feedback is the migration.

Takeaway. Fixed partition → starvation possible; feedback → migration, starvation reduced but not eliminated.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 2 — Inter-Process Communication & Synchronization
critical-section-problem-peterson · 12
OS-U2-Q01MCQeasy

Which one of the following best describes the critical section of a process?

  1. The part of the program that must finish before any other process is allowed to start.
  2. The segment of code in which the process accesses shared resources, and which must therefore not be executed by more than one process at the same time.
  3. The part of the process image that holds the executable code.
  4. The region of physical memory reserved for the kernel.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The critical section is a piece of code, not a piece of memory and not a phase of the process's life. It is the region that touches shared data, so concurrent execution of it by two processes would break mutual exclusion.

Step by step.

  1. A process alternates between its critical section and its remainder section.
  2. The critical section is defined by which shared variables it reads and writes, not by how long it runs.
  3. Because the shared data can be left inconsistent if two processes interleave inside that region, the protocol must allow at most one process inside at a time.
  4. That is exactly option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) confuses the critical section with a start-up phase; every process may run for a long time before reaching its critical section.
  • (C) confuses code with the code segment of the address space.
  • (D) confuses the critical section with kernel memory.

Common misconception. Candidates read 'critical section' as 'the important part of the program'. It is defined purely by shared-data access.

Takeaway. Critical section = code that touches shared state. Mutual exclusion is required inside it and nowhere else.

OS-U2-Q05MCQeasy

A solution to the critical-section problem must satisfy three requirements. Which one of the following is NOT one of them?

  1. Mutual exclusion
  2. Progress
  3. Bounded waiting
  4. Absence of deadlock anywhere else in the system
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three textbook requirements are mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting. Deadlock freedom is a desirable property of the whole system, but it is not one of the three named requirements on the critical-section protocol itself.

Step by step.

  1. Mutual exclusion: at most one process is inside its critical section at a time.
  2. Progress: if no process is inside and some wish to enter, one of them must be admitted, and the choice cannot be postponed indefinitely by processes in their remainder sections.
  3. Bounded waiting: after a process has requested entry, there is a bound on how many times others may enter before it does.
  4. Deadlock freedom in the rest of the system is not among these three, so it is option (D).

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) are exactly the three requirements.

Common misconception. Candidates who have seen 'deadlock-free' used loosely about critical-section solutions pick (D) as if it were required.

Takeaway. Memorise the triple: mutual exclusion, progress, bounded waiting.

OS-U2-Q10MCQmedium

In Peterson's two-process solution, what is the role of the shared variable turn?

  1. It records which process is currently inside its critical section.
  2. It breaks the tie when both processes want to enter, by naming the process that must give way.
  3. It counts how many times each process has entered its critical section.
  4. It stores the other process's flag value.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each process raises its own flag to show that it wants in. If both flags are raised, the two are in conflict, and turn resolves it: the process that wrote turn last is the one that waits.

Step by step.

  1. Process i executes flag[i] = true; turn = j; and then waits while flag[j] && turn == j.
  2. If both set their flags, the later write to turn wins, so exactly one of them sees turn == j and defers.
  3. turn therefore decides who yields in the contested case, which is option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is wrong: neither process sets turn on entering, and turn is meaningless once both flags are clear.
  • (C) is wrong: turn holds a process index, not a count.
  • (D) is wrong: each process reads the other's flag directly.

Common misconception. The tempting error is to think turn is a permission token that must be held. It is the opposite — it names the process that must step aside.

Takeaway. In Peterson's solution: flags say 'I want in'; turn says 'you go first'.

OS-U2-Q19MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about Peterson's two-process solution are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. It guarantees mutual exclusion.
  2. It guarantees progress.
  3. It guarantees bounded waiting.
  4. It requires the flag[] array and turn to be updated together as one atomic step.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Peterson's solution satisfies all three critical-section requirements using only single-word reads and writes, which is what makes it remarkable — it needs no special hardware instruction.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Mutual exclusion: if both are inside, both must have read turn as the other's index, which is impossible because turn holds one value.
  2. (B) Progress: a process in its remainder section has its flag clear, so it cannot block the other.
  3. (C) Bounded waiting: once a process raises its flag, the other can enter at most once before it does.
  4. (D) is false: only individual reads and writes of flag[i] and turn need be atomic; the updates are separate statements.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. (D) is the trap. Peterson's solution is prized precisely because it needs no atomic read-modify-write of several variables — only atomic single-variable access.

Takeaway. Peterson's solution: three requirements met with ordinary reads and writes, no hardware primitive.

OS-U2-Q20MCQhard

A proposed critical-section protocol satisfies mutual exclusion and progress, but a process that has announced its intention may be overtaken arbitrarily many times before it finally enters. Which requirement does the protocol violate?

  1. Mutual exclusion
  2. Progress
  3. Bounded waiting
  4. Atomicity of the entry code
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Bounded waiting is precisely the guarantee that, after a process requests entry, there is a limit on how many times other processes may enter ahead of it. A protocol that lets others overtake without limit violates it while still keeping mutual exclusion and progress.

Step by step.

  1. Mutual exclusion is stated to hold.
  2. Progress is stated to hold, so someone is always admitted when the section is free.
  3. Unbounded overtaking is the definition of a violation of bounded waiting.
  4. The answer is option (C).

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) are explicitly given as satisfied.
  • (D) is a property of the implementation, not one of the three requirements, and nothing in the statement suggests it fails.

Common misconception. Bounded waiting and progress sound similar. Progress is about the system always making some progress; bounded waiting is about a particular process eventually getting its turn.

Takeaway. Progress = someone gets in. Bounded waiting = you get in after a bounded number of others.

OS-U2B-Q01MCQeasy

Peterson's two-process solution needs shared data between the two processes. Which one of the following is exactly the shared data it uses?

  1. Two boolean flags flag[0], flag[1] plus one integer variable turn.
  2. One integer variable turn only, shared by both processes.
  3. A single boolean lock variable that both processes test and set.
  4. One integer per process, holding the identity of the process that is inside the critical section.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Peterson's solution combines a per-process declaration of intent with a single tie-breaking variable.

Step by step.

  1. Each process owns one boolean, flag[i], which it sets to true on entering the entry section and clears on leaving the exit section.
  2. The single integer turn records which of the two processes has the right of way when both want to enter.
  3. No other variable is read or written, so the shared state is exactly the two flags and turn.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the strict-alternation protocol, which uses only turn and therefore violates progress.
  • (C) is a test-and-set lock, a hardware-primitive solution rather than Peterson's algorithm.
  • (D) describes an 'owner' variable, which Peterson's algorithm does not need because mutual exclusion follows from the flags together with turn.

Common misconception. Students remember 'Peterson uses turn' and forget the flag array; without the flags the algorithm cannot express that a process is interested.

OS-U2B-Q02MCQeasy

Which one of the following states the bounded-waiting requirement on a critical-section solution?

  1. At most one process may be executing inside its critical section at any instant.
  2. If no process is inside its critical section, a process that wishes to enter must be allowed to do so without being delayed by processes in their remainder sections.
  3. There must be a limit on the number of times other processes may enter their critical sections after a given process has requested entry and before that request is granted.
  4. Every process must eventually terminate after a finite number of critical-section entries.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Bounded waiting is a fairness condition, not a safety condition.

Step by step.

  1. Mutual exclusion bounds how many processes may be inside at once; progress says the choice of the next entrant cannot be postponed indefinitely.
  2. Bounded waiting adds a counting guarantee: once process Pi has announced its request, only a bounded number of other entries may occur before Pi is admitted.
  3. That is what prevents a fast process from overtaking a slow one arbitrarily many times.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is mutual exclusion.
  • (B) is progress.
  • (D) is a liveness property of the process, not of the entry protocol.

Common misconception. Progress and bounded waiting are often treated as one idea. Progress forbids indefinite postponement in the aggregate; bounded waiting forbids an unbounded number of overtakings of one specific requester.

OS-U2B-Q03MCQmedium

Two processes P0 and P1 use Peterson's solution. Initially flag[0] = flag[1] = false and turn = 0. The entry sections are then interleaved exactly as follows: P0 executes flag[0] = true and then turn = 1; next P1 executes flag[1] = true and then turn = 0. Both now evaluate their spin conditions. Which process enters the critical section first?

  1. P0, because turn = 0 at the moment the tests are made.
  2. P1, because it was the last process to write turn.
  3. Both enter, so this interleaving breaks mutual exclusion.
  4. Neither enters; each waits for the other and the two processes deadlock.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The spin condition of Pi is flag[j] && turn == j; the process with turn != j proceeds.

Step by step.

  1. After the interleaving, flag[0] = flag[1] = true and turn = 0.
  2. P0 tests flag[1] && turn == 1: flag[1] is true but turn is 0, so the condition is false and P0 leaves the loop.
  3. P1 tests flag[0] && turn == 0: both parts are true, so P1 keeps spinning.
  4. P0 therefore enters first, and P1 enters only after P0 clears flag[0] on exit.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the meaning of turn: the last writer yields, it does not win.
  • (C) and (D) cannot happen here because exactly one of the two conditions evaluates to true when both flags are set.

Common misconception. It is tempting to read turn = 0 as 'P0's turn to wait'. In Peterson's algorithm the process that writes turn = j is offering the other one priority.

OS-U2B-Q04MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the three requirements on a critical-section solution are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Mutual exclusion forbids two processes from executing inside their critical sections at the same time.
  2. Progress forbids a process that wants to enter from being delayed indefinitely by processes that are in their remainder sections.
  3. Bounded waiting limits the number of other processes' entries that may intervene between a request and the granting of that request.
  4. A protocol that satisfies progress necessarily satisfies bounded waiting.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three requirements are independent: a protocol can satisfy any two of them and fail the third.

Step by step.

  1. Mutual exclusion is the safety property: at most one process inside at any instant.
  2. Progress is a liveness property about the decision: if the critical section is free, only processes that are actually trying to enter may take part in choosing the next entrant, and that choice cannot be postponed for ever.
  3. Bounded waiting is a per-process fairness property: after a request is made, a finite bound applies to the number of intervening entries by others.
  4. Progress constrains the group, not the individual, so it cannot imply a per-process bound.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the classic confusion: a protocol can guarantee that somebody always enters (progress) while letting one unlucky process be overtaken indefinitely.

Common misconception. Progress and bounded waiting are routinely merged into one 'no starvation' idea. Starvation-freedom for an individual process is bounded waiting; progress is only the guarantee that the decision procedure keeps working.

OS-U2B-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following pairings of a protocol with a requirement it fails are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A protocol that guarantees mutual exclusion is thereby guaranteed to guarantee progress as well.
  2. Strict alternation using a single shared turn variable, where Pi enters only while turn != i, violates progress.
  3. A bare test-and-set lock with no queue and no ordering rule violates bounded waiting.
  4. Peterson's two-process solution satisfies mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each requirement is a separate obligation, and each of these three protocols fails exactly one of them.

Step by step.

  1. Strict alternation: if P1 is in its remainder section, P0 cannot enter twice in a row, although the critical section is free. That is a failure of progress, not of mutual exclusion.
  2. A bare test-and-set lock: a process that releases the lock can immediately re-acquire it, so another waiting process may be overtaken arbitrarily many times. Mutual exclusion and progress both hold; bounded waiting does not.
  3. Peterson's algorithm satisfies all three for two processes: the flags give mutual exclusion, turn guarantees that one of the two must proceed, and a waiting process can be overtaken at most once.
  4. Statement (A) is false because mutual exclusion says nothing about whether the next entrant is ever chosen.

Answer: Options (B), (C) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the pairing to reject: mutual exclusion is a safety property and implies no liveness property at all.

Common misconception. Candidates often mark strict alternation as correct because it 'looks like' a working protocol. It protects the shared data perfectly; what it destroys is the ability of one process to use a free critical section.

OS-U2B-Q06MCQhard

Peterson's two-process solution is implemented on a modern multicore processor with per-core store buffers, and no memory barriers are emitted. It can fail to provide mutual exclusion because:

  1. the spin loop while (flag[j] && turn == j); can never terminate on a processor that caches flag[j].
  2. an integer variable cannot be written atomically, so turn may be torn between the two writers.
  3. the compiler is allowed to hoist the load of flag[j] out of the loop, and that alone destroys the proof of mutual exclusion.
  4. the store to flag[i] and the store to turn may become visible to the other core in an order different from program order, so each process can read a value of turn that lets it in.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Peterson's correctness argument assumes sequential consistency: that all processes observe every store in one common order.

Step by step.

  1. The proof relies on the fact that the write to turn by one process is seen by the other before that other process reads it.
  2. With a store buffer, the write to flag[i] can still be pending when turn = j becomes visible; the other core then sees the flag as false, skips the spin, and both processes enter.
  3. The cure is a memory barrier (or a release/acquire fence) between the two stores, not a change of algorithm.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the opposite failure — a stale cached value would make a process spin longer, not enter wrongly.
  • (B) is false: an aligned 32-bit integer store is atomic; tearing is not the issue here.
  • (C) is a real hazard on a uniprocessor with an optimising compiler, but it produces a stuck loop rather than two processes inside the critical section, and a barrier at the loop fixes it.

Common misconception. The textbook algorithm is correct on a sequentially consistent machine. On real hardware the atomicity of individual stores is not enough; the ordering between them is what the proof needs.

OS-U2B-Q07NAThard

Peterson's solution generalises to n processes using n - 1 levels, level 1 being the outermost, with one turn variable per level. A process must pass through every level before it may enter its critical section. For n = 6, through how many levels must a process pass?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The n-process generalisation replaces the single turn with one variable per level and makes every process climb all levels.

Step by step.

  1. Level k is entered by announcing interest and then setting the level's turn variable to the process's own index.
  2. A process leaves a level only when no other process is interested in that level, or when the level's turn variable no longer names it.
  3. There are n - 1 levels in total, and the critical section sits above the last one.
  4. For n = 6: 6 - 1 = 5 levels.

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Answering n (6) by counting processes instead of levels, or n - 2 by forgetting that level 1 is used.

hardware-primitives-test-and-set · 12
OS-U2-Q02MCQeasy

The instruction test-and-set(lock) is executed atomically by the hardware. Which one of the following does it do?

  1. It reads the current value of lock, sets lock to true, and returns the value it read.
  2. It sets lock to true and returns nothing.
  3. It sets lock to false unconditionally.
  4. It reads lock twice and returns the larger of the two values.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. test-and-set is a read-and-write pair that the hardware performs indivisibly. The value it returns is the old value, and the value it leaves behind is true. That combination is what makes a lock possible.

Step by step.

  1. The atomic action is: old = lock; lock = true; return old.
  2. If the returned old value is false, the caller has just taken the lock, because nobody else held it.
  3. If the returned old value is true, somebody else already held the lock, so the caller must retry.
  4. This is option (A).

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) loses the information the caller needs: without the old value the process cannot tell whether it acquired the lock.
  • (C) would release the lock rather than acquire it.
  • (D) is not an atomic read-modify-write at all.

Common misconception. The common error is to remember only that test-and-set 'sets the flag to true' and to forget that its return value is the previous flag.

Takeaway. test-and-set: returns the old value, leaves true behind. The returned value decides who got in.

OS-U2-Q07MCQeasy

A spin lock is implemented with test-and-set. While one process is inside its critical section, what are the other processes that want the lock doing?

  1. Sleeping in a blocked queue maintained by the kernel.
  2. Repeatedly executing the test-and-set loop, consuming CPU time.
  3. Waiting on a condition variable.
  4. Nothing — they have been terminated.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A spin lock has no queue and no sleep. A waiting process keeps executing the acquire loop until the lock becomes free, so it burns CPU cycles for as long as it waits. This is why spin locks are useful only when the expected wait is shorter than two context switches.

Step by step.

  1. The acquire loop is while (test-and-set(lock));.
  2. While the lock is held, every iteration returns true and the loop repeats.
  3. No kernel structure records the waiter and no process is descheduled, so the CPU is spent spinning.
  4. This is option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes a blocking lock built on semaphores or a mutex, not a spin lock.
  • (C) describes condition variables, which do suspend the waiter.
  • (D) is not a synchronisation behaviour at all.

Common misconception. The word 'wait' makes candidates picture a sleeping process. In a spin lock, waiting means running.

Takeaway. Spin lock = busy waiting: the waiter stays runnable and consumes the CPU.

OS-U2-Q11MCQeasy

The swap instruction used to build a lock is atomic. Which of the following is the correct acquire idiom?

  1. key = true; do { swap(lock, key); } while (key); — the process enters when key is false.
  2. key = false; do { swap(lock, key); } while (key == false); — the process enters when key is true.
  3. swap(lock, key) is executed once, after which the lock is permanently held.
  4. swap is used only to release the lock, never to acquire it.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. swap(lock, key) exchanges the two values atomically. Setting key = true first means that after the swap the lock is true and key holds whatever the lock used to be. A returned false means the lock was free and is now ours.

Step by step.

  1. Set key = true to express 'I want the lock'.
  2. Execute swap(lock, key): the lock becomes true and key receives the old lock value.
  3. If the old value was false, nobody held the lock and we now do — exit the loop.
  4. If the old value was true, somebody else holds it — loop and try again. That is option (A).

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) inverts the test, so it exits exactly when the lock is taken.
  • (C) ignores that the lock may already be held.
  • (D) is wrong: release is a plain store of false and needs no atomic instruction.

Common misconception. The direction of the flag is easy to get backwards. Fix it by asking what value must be in key for us to know we won: false.

Takeaway. Acquire with swap: load true into your key, swap, and win when you get false back.

OS-U2-Q15MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about test-and-set based locks are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. test-and-set must be executed atomically by the hardware for the lock to be correct.
  2. A plain test-and-set lock guarantees bounded waiting by itself.
  3. A test-and-set lock makes waiting processes busy-wait, consuming CPU cycles.
  4. On a uniprocessor, a spin lock held by a process that has just been preempted can waste an entire time quantum.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Atomicity gives mutual exclusion; it does not give fairness. Bounded waiting needs an ordering rule on top of the atomic primitive.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: if the read and the write could be interleaved, two processes could both observe false and both enter.
  2. (B) is false: the hardware primitive has no notion of arrival order, so a process can be overtaken arbitrarily many times.
  3. (C) is true: the waiter stays runnable and re-executes the acquire loop.
  4. (D) is true: the holder cannot run because the spinner has the CPU, so the quantum can be wasted before the holder ever releases the lock.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. (B) is the standard confusion: atomicity is not fairness. A test-and-set lock is correct but not starvation-free.

Takeaway. Atomic primitive gives mutual exclusion; add a queue or an ordering rule to get bounded waiting.

OS-U2-Q18NATmedium

A lock is acquired with key = true; do { swap(lock, key); } while (key);, where swap(a, b) exchanges two variables atomically and lock is initially false. Three processes attempt to acquire the lock one after another and none of them ever releases it. How many of the three swap calls leave true in the caller's key? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. swap(lock, key) makes lock equal to the caller's key (true) and gives the caller the lock's previous value. A returned true therefore means the lock was already held.

Step by step.

  1. Process 1: key = true, lock = false. After swap, lock = true and key = false. One call returned false.
  2. Process 2: key = true, lock = true. After swap, lock = true and key = true. Returned true.
  3. Process 3: identical to process 2, because the lock was never released. Returned true.
  4. Two of the three calls leave true in key.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Candidates count the first call as returning true because the lock 'became true'. The relevant value is the one handed back to the caller, which is the old lock.

Takeaway. In a swap lock, the value you get back is the old lock: false means you won, true means keep spinning.

OS-U2-Q25MSQhard

Which of the following statements about hardware synchronisation primitives are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. test-and-set and swap are both atomic hardware instructions from which a mutual-exclusion lock can be built.
  2. compare-and-swap returns the old value of a memory location and writes the new value only if the old value still equals an expected value.
  3. Bounded waiting cannot be obtained from a bare test-and-set lock without adding a queue or an explicit ordering rule.
  4. A test-and-set lock never causes a context switch while a process is spinning.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Hardware gives atomicity, not policy. Every fairness guarantee must be layered on top of the atomic primitive by software.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: both are single atomic read-modify-write instructions and each is enough to build a correct lock.
  2. (B) is the definition of compare-and-swap: it succeeds only if the value has not changed since it was read.
  3. (C) is true: the primitive has no record of who arrived first, so overtaking is unbounded unless a queue or ticket scheme is added.
  4. (D) is false: a spinning process stays runnable and can be preempted by the scheduler like any other.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. (D) is the trap. Spinning does not disable preemption; the spinner can be switched out at any time, and on a uniprocessor the lock holder may then be unable to run.

Takeaway. Atomicity from hardware; fairness from software. A spin lock is correct but not fair.

OS-U2B-Q08MCQeasy

A spin lock differs from a blocking mutex in the way a waiting process behaves. Which one of the following describes the spin lock's waiter?

  1. It is placed on a queue and descheduled by the kernel until the lock is released.
  2. It repeatedly executes the acquire instruction on the processor until the lock becomes free.
  3. It cannot be preempted once it has started waiting.
  4. It consumes no processor time while it waits.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A spin lock trades processor cycles for the absence of a context switch.

Step by step.

  1. The acquire step is a short loop around an atomic instruction, for example while (test_and_set(&lock)) ;.
  2. The waiter stays runnable and keeps executing that loop, so it is burning a processor rather than sleeping.
  3. A blocking mutex instead moves the waiter to a wait queue and wakes it on release, which costs two context switches but frees the processor.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes a blocking mutex, not a spin lock.
  • (C) is false: a spinning process can be preempted at any time.
  • (D) is the opposite of what spinning does.

Common misconception. The word 'wait' suggests the process is idle. A spinning process is fully active and competes for the CPU with everyone else.

OS-U2B-Q09MCQeasy

On a uniprocessor, spinning on a lock is a poor choice when the critical section is long. Which one of the following is the correct reason?

  1. The spinning process keeps the processor busy, and the process that holds the lock cannot run until the spinner's time quantum ends.
  2. The instruction test-and-set is not available on uniprocessors.
  3. Spin locks cannot enforce mutual exclusion when there is only one processor.
  4. The lock variable lives in a register and is lost at a context switch.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. On one processor, spinning and holding are mutually exclusive uses of the only CPU.

Step by step.

  1. If the lock holder has been preempted, it cannot make progress until it is scheduled again.
  2. A spinner that keeps its quantum runs the acquire loop for the whole quantum, so the holder waits at least that long.
  3. A blocking mutex puts the waiter to sleep, which lets the holder run immediately; that is why spin locks are recommended only for very short critical sections or for multiprocessors.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) and (C) are false: atomic test-and-set exists and mutual exclusion works on a uniprocessor.
  • (D) is false: the lock is a memory word, not a register.

Common misconception. Spin locks are not 'wrong' on a uniprocessor, just wasteful; the loss is bounded-waiting time, not correctness.

OS-U2B-Q10MCQmedium

The instruction fetch-and-add(X, 1) atomically returns the old value of X and then increments X. A ticket lock gives each arriving process the ticket returned by fetch-and-add on a counter, and lets the process proceed when a separate now-serving counter reaches its ticket. Compared with a bare test-and-set lock, the ticket lock additionally guarantees:

  1. freedom from busy waiting, because a waiting process is descheduled.
  2. atomicity of the critical section itself, not merely of the acquire step.
  3. bounded waiting, because the lock is granted in the order in which tickets were taken.
  4. that no memory barrier is needed on a multicore machine.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A ticket lock adds a queue implicitly: the ticket number is the position in that queue.

Step by step.

  1. Each arrival obtains a distinct ticket, so the arrival order is recorded in the counter itself.
  2. The release increments the now-serving counter, which hands the lock to exactly one ticket holder — the next in order.
  3. Because a process can be overtaken only by the processes already ahead of it in the queue, the number of intervening entries is bounded by the number of earlier arrivals.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: a ticket lock still spins.
  • (B) is false: atomicity of the acquire is what test-and-set already gives.
  • (D) is false: a ticket lock still needs fences to order the critical-section accesses around the acquire and release.

Common misconception. Every lock built from an atomic instruction gives mutual exclusion; what separates them is fairness. test-and-set gives none, ticket locks give FIFO order.

OS-U2B-Q11MSQmedium

Each statement below is about an atomic hardware primitive. Which of them are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The atomicity of test-and-set and swap is provided by the memory system, so no interleaving of two processors can occur inside one such instruction.
  2. A spin lock avoids the overhead of a context switch but consumes processor cycles while it waits.
  3. A single atomic instruction can update two unrelated memory words indivisibly.
  4. compare-and-swap can implement both a lock and a lock-free counter, because it writes only when the observed value is still the expected one.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Atomic instructions move the mutual exclusion down into the hardware for one word at a time.

Step by step.

  1. test-and-set and swap are executed as indivisible read-modify-write cycles on the memory bus, which is why they cannot be interleaved.
  2. The trade-off of spinning is explicit: no context switch, but the waiting processor is busy.
  3. compare-and-swap returns the old value and stores only if it still equals the expected value, which is exactly what both a lock and a lock-free increment need.
  4. One instruction operates on one memory word; protecting a multi-word invariant still needs a lock or a transactional mechanism.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the statement to reject: atomicity is per memory location, not across two independent locations.

Common misconception. It is easy to over-read 'atomic': the guarantee covers a single word. Two counters updated by two atomic instructions are not jointly atomic.

OS-U2B-Q12MCQhard

A lock is acquired with while (test_and_set(&lock)) ; and released with lock = false;. On a multicore machine with per-core store buffers, mutual exclusion can break down unless memory barriers are used. Which one of the following is the correct explanation?

  1. test-and-set is not atomic when more than one processor executes it.
  2. the release store may be reordered with the critical-section accesses, so another processor can observe the lock as free while the critical-section writes are still pending in the buffer.
  3. the compiler removes the empty body of the loop, so the lock is never tested.
  4. the lock variable must be declared volatile for the instruction itself to remain atomic.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The release is a store, and a store can be delayed behind other stores.

Step by step.

  1. Inside the critical section the holder writes shared data; those writes may sit in its store buffer.
  2. The release writes lock = false to the same buffer. Without a fence the two writes can reach memory out of order, so a second core sees the lock free and enters while the first core's data writes are still invisible.
  3. A release barrier before the unlock store, and an acquire barrier after the successful test-and-set, restore the ordering the algorithm assumes.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: test-and-set is atomic per memory operation.
  • (C) would produce a non-terminating loop, not two processes inside the critical section.
  • (D) is false: volatile prevents some compiler optimisations but does not order memory operations on the hardware.

Common misconception. Atomicity of each instruction is not the same as ordering between instructions. Most real spin-lock bugs on multicore machines are ordering bugs.

OS-U2B-Q13NATeasy

A single test-and-set lock is contended by 8 processes. While exactly one of them is inside its critical section, what is the maximum number of processes that can be spinning on that lock at the same instant?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The lock is a single word with two values, so at most one holder and every other contender spins.

Step by step.

  1. The process that succeeded in test-and-set holds the lock and is inside the critical section.
  2. Every other contending process fails the test, sees the lock busy, and repeats the loop.
  3. With 8 contenders and one holder, at most 8 - 1 = 7 are spinning.

Answer: 7.

Common misconception. Answering 8 by counting every contender, including the holder, as a spinner.

semaphores-binary-counting · 14
OS-U2-Q03MCQeasy

How does a binary semaphore differ from a counting semaphore?

  1. A binary semaphore can take only the values 0 and 1, whereas a counting semaphore can take any non-negative integer value.
  2. A binary semaphore cannot be used to provide mutual exclusion.
  3. A binary semaphore never blocks the process that waits on it.
  4. A counting semaphore cannot be implemented using binary semaphores.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Both kinds of semaphore support the same two operations, P (wait) and V (signal). They differ only in the range of values the counter may take.

Step by step.

  1. A binary semaphore's counter is confined to {0, 1}, so it behaves exactly like a mutex.
  2. A counting semaphore's counter may be any non-negative integer and therefore models a pool of N identical resources.
  3. Hence the distinction is the range of the counter, which is option (A).

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is false: the binary semaphore is the standard mutex.
  • (C) is false: P on a binary semaphore at 0 blocks exactly as it does on a counting semaphore.
  • (D) is false: a counting semaphore initialised to N can be built from N binary semaphores, and a general counting semaphore can be built from a binary semaphore plus a counter.

Common misconception. Students assume the binary semaphore is 'weaker'. It is not weaker — it is the same object restricted to two values.

Takeaway. Same operations, different counter range. Binary = mutex; counting = pool of resources.

OS-U2-Q06NATeasy

A counting semaphore S is initialised to 3. The operations P, P, V, P, P, P, V, V, P are then executed in that order on a single semaphore, where P waits and V signals. The semaphore allows its value to become negative, and the magnitude of a negative value is then the number of processes blocked on it. What is the final value of S? (Enter an integer; it may be zero or negative.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A semaphore's value is simply incremented by V and decremented by P. A negative value is not an error: it records how many processes are waiting.

Step by step.

  1. Start: S = 3.
  2. P → 2; P → 1; V → 2; P → 1; P → 0; P → −1 (one process now blocked);
  3. V → 0 (the blocked process is woken, and the value returns to 0); V → 1; P → 0.
  4. Final value: S = 0.

Answer: 0.

Common misconception. The usual slip is to clamp the counter at 0 and then lose count of how many V operations were 'absorbed' by blocked processes.

Takeaway. Trace the counter one operation at a time and let it go negative; a V that wakes a sleeper restores the value to 0, not to 1.

OS-U2-Q09MCQeasy

A semaphore S has the value 4, and no process is blocked on it. A process now executes P(S). What is the new value of S, and what does the process do?

  1. S becomes 5 and the process continues.
  2. S becomes 3 and the process continues.
  3. S becomes 3 and the process blocks.
  4. S stays 4 and the process blocks.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. P(S) decrements the semaphore and blocks the caller only if the resulting value is negative. Decrementing 4 gives 3, which is not negative, so the process simply carries on.

Step by step.

  1. P(S): S ← S − 1.
  2. S becomes 3.
  3. Because the new value is ≥ 0, no resource shortage is implied and the caller is not blocked.
  4. This is option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) increments, which is what V does.
  • (C) blocks even though the count is still positive.
  • (D) neither changes the count nor explains a block.

Common misconception. Some presentations describe P as 'wait until S > 0, then decrement'. Both descriptions agree here: 4 > 0, so the process decrements and proceeds.

Takeaway. P decrements and blocks only when the result goes negative; V increments and wakes one waiter.

OS-U2-Q12NATmedium

A counting semaphore S is initialised to 5. Exactly seven P(S) operations and four V(S) operations are then executed, in any order, and every one of them completes. What is the final value of S? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution
