Concept. The final value of a semaphore depends only on how many P and V operations were performed, never on their order, because each P subtracts one and each V adds one.

Step by step.

  1. Each P(S) subtracts 1 from S.
  2. Each V(S) adds 1 to S.
  3. Net change = −7 + 4 = −3.
  4. Final value = 5 − 3 = 2.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Order does matter for which processes block and for how long, but it never matters for the final count. Candidates sometimes try to trace an interleaving and lose track.

Takeaway. Final semaphore value = initial + (#V) − (#P). Order affects blocking, not the final count.

OS-U2-Q14MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about semaphores are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. P(S) decrements S and blocks the caller if the resulting value is negative.
  2. V(S) increments S and wakes a blocked process if any is waiting.
  3. A binary semaphore can be used to provide mutual exclusion.
  4. A counting semaphore initialised to 1 always allows two processes into the critical section at the same time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two operations are duals: P consumes one unit of a resource and blocks when none is left, V returns one unit and wakes a waiter if there is one.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the definition of P, including the blocking rule when the count goes negative.
  2. (B) is the definition of V, including the wake-up rule.
  3. (C) is true: a binary semaphore restricted to {0, 1} is exactly a mutex.
  4. (D) is false: a counting semaphore initialised to 1 admits one process; a second process blocks until the first signals.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. (D) is the trap: initialising a counting semaphore to 1 makes it behave as a binary semaphore, which excludes a second process, not one that admits two.

Takeaway. P blocks when the count would go negative; V wakes one waiter. An initial value of 1 means one process at a time.

OS-U2-Q17MCQmedium

Two processes share two counting semaphores A and B, both initialised to 1. Process P1 executes P(A); P(B); and process P2 executes P(B); P(A);. Which of the following can happen?

  1. Nothing can go wrong, because semaphores prevent every synchronisation hazard.
  2. A deadlock: P1 may hold A and wait for B while P2 holds B and waits for A.
  3. Only a livelock, never a deadlock.
  4. Both processes proceed, because counting semaphores are re-entrant.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Semaphores enforce mutual exclusion on each resource individually. They say nothing about the order in which a process acquires several resources, and that is exactly the circular-wait condition for deadlock.

Step by step.

  1. Suppose P1 completes P(A) and is preempted; A is now 0 and P1 holds it.
  2. P2 completes P(B); B is now 0 and P2 holds it.
  3. P1 now waits on B, held by P2; P2 now waits on A, held by P1. Each waits for a resource the other holds.
  4. This is a circular wait, so the system deadlocks. Option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: semaphores protect each resource, not the acquisition sequence.
  • (C) is false: the processes are blocked, not spinning.
  • (D) is false: semaphores are not re-entrant in this sense, and P(B) by P1 simply blocks.

Common misconception. Candidates believe 'we used semaphores, so we are safe'. Mutual exclusion and deadlock freedom are different properties.

Takeaway. Deadlock is avoided by imposing a global order on resource acquisition, not by using semaphores correctly one at a time.

OS-U2-Q21MCQhard

A binary semaphore mutex is initialised to 1 and used to protect a critical section. A process enters the critical section and, before leaving, executes P(mutex) a second time. What is the result?

  1. It enters the critical section a second time, since binary semaphores are re-entrant.
  2. It blocks on itself and the system deadlocks, because the value is already 0 and no other process can ever signal it.
  3. The value of mutex becomes 2.
  4. The kernel detects the error and terminates the process.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A binary semaphore has no notion of an owner, so the second P is treated as an ordinary request by a stranger. The value is already 0, so the process blocks on a resource that only it could release.

Step by step.

  1. On entry the process executed P(mutex), taking the value from 1 to 0.
  2. The second P(mutex) decrements 0 to −1 and blocks the caller.
  3. The only process that could execute V(mutex) to release it is the one now blocked.
  4. The process waits for itself: a self-deadlock. Option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: semaphores are not re-entrant; recursive mutexes are a different construct and must be requested explicitly.
  • (C) is false: P decrements, it never increments.
  • (D) is false: the kernel does not police the user's protocol.

Common misconception. Candidates assume that because the process already 'owns' the lock, the second P is a no-op. A semaphore records a count, not an owner.

Takeaway. Re-entering a non-recursive mutex self-deadlocks. Ownership is a mutex concept, not a semaphore concept.

OS-U2-Q24MSQhard

Which of the following statements about the classic synchronisation problems and their semaphore solutions are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. In the bounded-buffer problem, full is initialised to 0 and empty to the buffer capacity.
  2. In the dining-philosophers problem, the simple solution in which every philosopher picks up the left fork first can deadlock.
  3. In the readers–writers problem, several readers may read at the same time, but a writer needs exclusive access.
  4. A counting semaphore cannot be used in a solution to the dining-philosophers problem.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each classic problem has a standard initialisation and a standard failure mode; knowing both is what the examiner tests.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: at the start the buffer holds nothing, so full = 0, and all N slots are free, so empty = N.
  2. (B) is true: if all five pick up their left fork simultaneously, each holds one fork and each waits for the right one, held by a neighbour — a circular wait.
  3. (C) is true: readers do not modify the data, so they can share; a writer must exclude everyone.
  4. (D) is false: a common fix uses a counting semaphore of 4 (or one binary semaphore per fork) to admit at most four philosophers at once.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. (D) is the trap. The dining-philosophers problem is solved with semaphores all the time; what fails is the naive ordering, not the primitive.

Takeaway. Know the initialisation and the failure mode of each classic problem; the fix is almost always a constraint on ordering or on the number admitted.

OS-U2B-Q14MCQeasy

A counting semaphore S has the value 0 and no process is blocked on it. A process now executes P(S). Which one of the following is the outcome under the convention in which a negative value counts the blocked processes?

  1. S remains 0 and the calling process continues into its critical section.
  2. S becomes 1 and the calling process continues.
  3. The calling process is terminated because the semaphore is exhausted.
  4. S becomes −1 and the calling process blocks.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. P decrements first and only then decides whether the process may continue.

Step by step.

  1. The decrement takes S from 0 to −1.
  2. A negative value means the resource is over-committed, so the calling process is added to the semaphore's wait queue.
  3. The magnitude, 1, records that one process is now waiting; a later V(S) will return S to 0 and wake it.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) would be right only if the value had been positive before the decrement.
  • (B) reverses the direction of P.
  • (C) describes no semaphore semantics at all.

Common misconception. The test is 'was the value positive before the decrement', not 'is the value positive after it'. Decrement-then-test is what makes the negative value meaningful.

OS-U2B-Q15MCQeasy

A counting semaphore S has the value −2, with two processes blocked on it. A process executes V(S). What is the result?

  1. S becomes −1 and exactly one blocked process is woken.
  2. S becomes −3 and one more process is added to the wait queue.
  3. S becomes 0 and both blocked processes are woken.
  4. S becomes −1 and both blocked processes are woken.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. V increments the value and hands the resource to one waiter if there is one.

Step by step.

  1. The increment takes S from −2 to −1.
  2. Because waiters exist, the increment is immediately consumed: one process is removed from the queue and resumed.
  3. The remaining −1 records that one process is still waiting.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the direction of V.
  • (C) would require two increments.
  • (D) wakes too many waiters for a single V.

Common misconception. One V wakes one waiter. Waking all of them, as in a broadcast, is a different operation and is not what V means.

OS-U2B-Q16NATeasy

A counting semaphore used to control access to 5 identical printers is initialised to 5. At some instant its value is −2, under the convention in which a negative value counts the blocked processes. How many processes are blocked on the semaphore at that instant?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Under the negative-value convention the magnitude of the value is exactly the length of the wait queue.

Step by step.

  1. The semaphore started at 5, one permit per printer.
  2. Each blocked process contributes 1 to the negative part of the value.
  3. The value is −2, so |−2| = 2 processes are waiting for a printer.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Reporting 5 − 2 = 3 by treating the value as a count of free printers; a negative value counts waiters, not permits.

OS-U2B-Q17MCQmedium

Three processes share a binary semaphore mutex initialised to 1. Process A is inside its critical section, having executed P(mutex). Processes B and C now both execute P(mutex) and block. What is the value of mutex, and how many processes are blocked?

  1. 0, with one process blocked.
  2. −2, with two processes blocked.
  3. 1, with no process blocked.
  4. −1, with two processes blocked.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The same decrement-then-test rule applies to a binary semaphore; only its permitted values on the way up are bounded by its use as a mutex.

Step by step.

  1. Initial value 1. A executes P: 1 → 0, and A continues because the value was positive before the decrement.
  2. B executes P: 0 → −1, and B blocks.
  3. C executes P: −1 → −2, and C blocks.
  4. After B the value is −1 and after C it is −2; the magnitude 2 is exactly the number of waiters.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) stops counting after the first waiter.
  • (B) double-counts by decrementing once for the holder as well.
  • (C) ignores the two blocked calls entirely.

Common misconception. A binary semaphore's value is binary only while it is non-negative. Once waiters queue up, the value goes negative and counts them.

OS-U2B-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about semaphore values and semaphore operations are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Blocking inside P rather than spinning avoids wasting a processor while the process waits.
  2. A counting semaphore initialised to 0, with no blocked process, means that no unit of the resource is currently available.
  3. If P were not executed atomically, two processes could both observe a positive value and both decrement it, so mutual exclusion would fail.
  4. A counting semaphore initialised to 5 gives mutual exclusion for a critical section in exactly the same way as one initialised to 1.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A semaphore is a counter plus an atomic decrement-or-block and an atomic increment-or-wake.

Step by step.

  1. The wait queue is the point of the construct: the waiting process is descheduled instead of burning cycles.
  2. The initial value is the number of permits, so 0 permits means every unit is in use.
  3. The atomicity of P is what prevents the lost-update race between two decrements.
  4. A semaphore initialised to 5 admits five processes into the section at once, which is the opposite of mutual exclusion.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (D) is the statement to reject: the initial value must be 1 for the semaphore to act as a mutex.

Common misconception. The initial value is not decoration — it is the admission count. Only 1 gives mutual exclusion.

OS-U2B-Q19MCQhard

One implementation of P and V disables interrupts while the semaphore value is tested and updated, which makes the pair atomic on a uniprocessor. On a multiprocessor the same implementation is unsafe because:

  1. disabling interrupts is illegal while a process is in user mode, so P and V could never be called.
  2. the interrupt mask is per processor, so another processor can execute P on the same semaphore at the same instant and the update is no longer atomic.
  3. disabling interrupts makes P and V take unbounded time, which breaks bounded waiting.
  4. the semaphore value is held in a register rather than in memory, so the mask does not protect it.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An interrupt mask stops local preemption; it says nothing about another processor running in parallel.

Step by step.

  1. On a uniprocessor, disabling interrupts prevents any other process from being scheduled in the middle of the update, which is enough for atomicity.
  2. On a multiprocessor, the other processors keep running with their own masks, so two P operations can still interleave.
  3. The correct primitive is an atomic instruction (test-and-set, swap, compare-and-swap) on the semaphore's word, or a spin lock around the update.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is a privilege question, not the atomicity question being asked.
  • (C) is a timing concern, not a correctness one.
  • (D) is false: the semaphore is a shared memory word.

Common misconception. Interrupt masking is the standard uniprocessor answer and the standard multiprocessor mistake. The mask is local to one processor.

classic-problems-dining-philosophers-producer-consumer · 12
OS-U2-Q04MCQeasy

Which classic synchronisation problem is modelled by a fixed-size array into which producer processes insert items and from which consumer processes remove them?

  1. The dining-philosophers problem
  2. The readers–writers problem
  3. The bounded-buffer (producer–consumer) problem
  4. The sleeping-barber problem
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The bounded-buffer problem, also called the producer–consumer problem, is exactly this: a buffer of fixed capacity N, producers that insert while the buffer is not full, and consumers that remove while it is not empty.

Step by step.

  1. A producer must block when the buffer is full; that is the empty semaphore.
  2. A consumer must block when the buffer is empty; that is the full semaphore.
  3. Mutual exclusion on the buffer itself is the third semaphore, mutex.
  4. The description therefore matches option (C).

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is about five philosophers competing for forks.
  • (B) is about concurrent readers against an exclusive writer.
  • (D) is about a barber and a waiting-room of limited chairs.

Common misconception. The three classic problems look similar in a one-line summary. The distinguishing feature here is a fixed-capacity container.

Takeaway. Fixed-capacity container with inserters and removers = bounded buffer.

OS-U2-Q08MCQeasy

In the dining-philosophers problem with five philosophers seated around a circular table, how many forks must a philosopher hold in order to eat?

  1. One
  2. Two — the fork on the left and the fork on the right
  3. Three
  4. Five
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A philosopher needs both adjacent forks, which is what makes the problem interesting: the resources are shared between neighbours, so a philosopher can hold one fork and still be unable to proceed.

Step by step.

  1. Five forks are placed on the table, one between each pair of neighbouring philosophers.
  2. A philosopher can pick up only the two forks that are adjacent to their own place.
  3. Eating requires both, so two forks are needed.
  4. This is option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the naive reading, but then no contention would arise and the problem would be trivial.
  • (C) and (D) exceed the two forks that are adjacent to any one philosopher.

Common misconception. The common mistake is to forget that the two forks are shared with the neighbours, which is the whole source of deadlock.

Takeaway. Two forks per philosopher, each shared with one neighbour — that sharing is the problem.

OS-U2-Q13MCQmedium

In the producer–consumer problem with a bounded buffer of capacity N, which invariant must always hold?

  1. full + empty = N
  2. full − empty = N
  3. full × empty = N
  4. full = empty
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. full counts the items present and empty counts the free slots. Between them they always account for the whole buffer, so their sum is the capacity N at every instant.

Step by step.

  1. Initially full = 0 and empty = N, so the sum is N.
  2. A producer inserts an item: it decrements empty and increments full, so the sum is unchanged.
  3. A consumer removes an item: it increments empty and decrements full, again unchanged.
  4. The sum is therefore the invariant N, which is option (A).

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) would force full above N.
  • (C) is dimensionally meaningless here and fails for full = 0.
  • (D) would hold only for a half-full buffer.

Common misconception. Candidates confuse empty with 'the buffer is empty'. empty is a count of free slots, not a Boolean.

Takeaway. full = items present, empty = slots free, and they always sum to N.

OS-U2-Q16NATmedium

A producer–consumer system uses a bounded buffer of capacity 9 together with the counting semaphores full and empty. At some instant full = 4 and no producer is blocked. How many further items can be inserted before a producer blocks? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of free slots is empty, and empty = capacity − full.

Step by step.

  1. Capacity N = 9.
  2. full = 4, so 4 slots are occupied.
  3. Free slots = 9 − 4 = 5, and this equals the current value of empty.
  4. Each insertion consumes one free slot, so 5 more insertions are possible before empty reaches 0 and the next producer blocks.

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Candidates sometimes answer 4, reading full as the number of free slots. full is the number of items present.

Takeaway. empty = N − full is the number of insertions still possible.

OS-U2-Q22NAThard

Seven philosophers sit around a circular table with seven forks, one between each pair of neighbours. Each philosopher needs both adjacent forks in order to eat. Using the asymmetric solution, in which one designated philosopher picks up the right fork first while every other philosopher picks up the left fork first, what is the maximum number of philosophers that can be eating at the same time? (Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each eating philosopher occupies two forks, and every fork sits between exactly two philosophers, so at most ⌊7/2⌋ = 3 can eat at once. The asymmetric rule guarantees that this maximum is actually reachable without deadlock.

Step by step.

  1. There are 7 forks and each eating philosopher needs 2 distinct forks.
  2. Forks are never shared between two eating philosophers, so the number eating is at most ⌊7 / 2⌋ = 3.
  3. That bound is achievable: philosophers 1, 3 and 5 can hold forks (7,1), (2,3) and (4,5) respectively, and none of them blocks the others.
  4. The asymmetric rule removes the circular wait, so this configuration is actually reached. Answer: 3.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Candidates often answer 7, thinking every philosopher can hold one fork simultaneously — but holding one fork is not eating.

Takeaway. Maximum simultaneous eaters = ⌊forks / 2⌋; the asymmetric (hierarchy) rule only makes that maximum reachable without deadlock.

OS-U2-Q23MCQmedium

In the first readers–writers solution, in which readers are never made to wait, what is the main hazard?

  1. Readers can starve.
  2. Writers can starve, because a continuous stream of readers keeps the resource occupied.
  3. A reader and a writer can be inside the critical section at the same time.
  4. Two readers can deadlock against each other.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The first readers–writers solution gives readers priority: a reader waits only while a writer is actually writing. If new readers keep arriving, the count of active readers never falls to zero, so a waiting writer is never admitted.

Step by step.

  1. A writer waits until the reader count reaches 0.
  2. Every arriving reader increments the count without regard to waiting writers.
  3. If readers arrive faster than the existing ones leave, the count never reaches 0.
  4. The writer therefore waits indefinitely: writer starvation. Option (B).

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the hazard of the second (writer-priority) solution.
  • (C) is prevented by the semaphores in both solutions.
  • (D) is impossible: readers never block one another in this solution.

Common misconception. The two readers–writers solutions have mirror-image starvation hazards. Read the priority direction carefully before answering.

Takeaway. First solution → writers starve. Second solution → readers starve.

OS-U2B-Q20MCQeasy

In the bounded-buffer producer–consumer problem with capacity N, the counting semaphore full counts the items in the buffer and empty counts the free slots. A producer blocks when:

  1. full = 0.
  2. empty = 0.
  3. mutex = 0.
  4. empty = N.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The producer consumes a free slot before it produces; it blocks when no free slot is left.

Step by step.

  1. The producer executes P(empty) before inserting and V(full) after.
  2. It can only block inside P(empty), which happens when empty has reached 0.
  3. At that instant the buffer holds N items, so full = N as well; the two statements describe the same state, which is why only one of them appears in the options.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the state in which a consumer blocks.
  • (C) means another process is inside the buffer update.
  • (D) is the initial state, with an empty buffer.

Common misconception. Swapping the two semaphores is the classic bug: a producer that waits on full blocks as soon as the buffer is empty, which is exactly when it should be producing.

OS-U2B-Q21MCQmedium

In the second readers–writers solution, a writer that arrives while readers are active blocks new readers from entering. The main hazard of this solution is that:

  1. two writers can enter the shared data at the same time.
  2. readers and writers can deadlock on the shared mutex.
  3. a reader can be kept waiting indefinitely while new writers keep arriving.
  4. readers can no longer read concurrently with one another.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Giving writers priority removes writer starvation by creating reader starvation.

Step by step.

  1. When a writer is waiting, arriving readers are made to queue behind it, so writers are served promptly.
  2. If writers arrive continuously, each new writer re-blocks the readers before the previous queue drains.
  3. A reader can therefore wait without any bound, which is starvation rather than deadlock — the system keeps making progress, just not for that reader.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) are prevented by the writer mutex and the read-count protocol.
  • (D) is false: the first reader still holds the mutex on behalf of all readers, so readers continue to share the data.

Common misconception. The two readers–writers solutions are mirror images: the first starves writers, the second starves readers. Neither is 'the correct one' without a stated priority policy.

OS-U2B-Q22MCQhard

In the dining-philosophers problem with five philosophers and five forks, the solution that admits at most four philosophers to compete for forks at any time prevents deadlock. Which one of the following is the reason?

  1. Four philosophers cannot all be hungry at the same time, so contention disappears.
  2. The fifth philosopher permanently holds the extra fork, which breaks the cycle.
  3. It removes the mutual-exclusion requirement on the forks.
  4. With at most four competitors for five forks, at least one philosopher can always obtain both neighbours' forks, so the circular-wait condition cannot hold.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deadlock needs all four Coffman conditions; the four-at-a-time rule removes circular wait.

Step by step.

  1. If each of four philosophers held one fork, that accounts for four of the five forks.
  2. The fifth fork is free and belongs to one of those four philosophers as its second fork, so that philosopher can pick it up and eat.
  3. Eating releases both forks, which lets the others proceed in turn; a cycle in which every philosopher waits for a neighbour can therefore never close.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: hunger has nothing to do with the bound.
  • (B) invents a fork that does not exist.
  • (C) is false: each fork is still used by one philosopher at a time.

Common misconception. The bound is not about politeness; it is a structural argument that some philosopher always has both forks available. Without that argument the rule looks arbitrary.

OS-U2B-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the classic synchronisation problems are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The dining-philosophers problem with one fork between neighbours models both deadlock and starvation.
  2. The first readers–writers solution allows several readers to be inside the shared data at the same time.
  3. A monitor is a semaphore with an associated counter of the processes waiting on it.
  4. With counting semaphores full and empty in a bounded buffer of capacity N, the relation full + empty = N holds at every instant.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each of these three statements is a standard structural fact about the classical problems.

Step by step.

  1. Dining philosophers: if every philosopher picks up the left fork first, all five can hold one fork and wait for ever — deadlock; a philosopher can also be overtaken indefinitely — starvation.
  2. First readers–writers: the first reader takes the mutex, later readers only increment a count, so readers share the data concurrently.
  3. In the bounded buffer every slot is either full or empty, so the two counters always sum to the capacity.
  4. A monitor is a language-level construct with mutual exclusion built in plus condition variables; it is not a semaphore with a counter.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (C) is the statement to reject: condition variables and semaphores are different primitives, and a monitor is a module, not a counter.

Common misconception. Monitors are often introduced right after semaphores, which invites the idea that one is a renamed version of the other. They differ in where the mutual exclusion lives.

OS-U2B-Q24NATeasy

A bounded buffer has capacity 8 and is managed with the counting semaphores full and empty. At some instant full = 3 and no process is blocked. What is the value of empty?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every slot of the buffer is either occupied or free, so the two counters partition the capacity.

Step by step.

  1. The capacity is N = 8.
  2. Three slots hold items, so full = 3.
  3. The remaining slots are free: empty = N - full = 8 - 3 = 5.

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Reporting 3 by symmetry with full, or 8 by treating empty as the capacity.

OS-U2B-Q25NATmedium

Five philosophers sit around a circular table with one fork between each pair of neighbours, and the solution admits at most four philosophers to compete for forks at any instant. What is the maximum number of philosophers that can be eating at the same instant?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each eater needs two distinct forks, and a table of five has five forks.

Step by step.

  1. Two eaters use four forks and cannot be adjacent, because neighbours would share a fork.
  2. Three eaters would need six forks, which the table does not have.
  3. The bound is therefore ⌊5 / 2⌋ = 2, and it is achievable: philosophers 1 and 3 eat while 2, 4 and 5 wait.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Answering 4 by reading the four-at-a-time rule as a count of eaters rather than of competitors.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 3 — Deadlocks
four-conditions-resource-allocation-graph · 13
OS-U3-Q01MCQeasy

Which one of the following is NOT one of the four necessary conditions for a deadlock to occur?

  1. Mutual exclusion
  2. Hold and wait
  3. Preemption of resources
  4. Circular wait
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four Coffman conditions are mutual exclusion, hold and wait, no preemption and circular wait. Each is necessary, and deadlock requires all four to hold at the same time.

Step by step.

  1. Mutual exclusion: at least one resource must be held in a non-shareable mode, so a second requester must wait.
  2. Hold and wait: a process must be holding at least one resource while it waits for another.
  3. No preemption: a resource can be released only voluntarily, by the process that holds it.
  4. Circular wait: there is a closed chain of processes in which each waits for a resource held by the next.
  5. Option (C) states the negation of the third condition. Deadlock needs preemption to be impossible, so “preemption of resources” is not a condition — it is the escape route.

Answer: Option (C) — preemption of resources is the opposite of the required condition, which is no preemption.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Mutual exclusion is the first Coffman condition.
  • (B) Hold and wait is the second.
  • (D) Circular wait is the fourth.

Common misconception. Many candidates tick “preemption” because the word does appear in the standard list. The list says no preemption: if the system could preempt a resource from a waiting process, the wait could never be permanent.

Takeaway. All four conditions must hold simultaneously. Denying any one of them makes deadlock impossible — that is exactly what deadlock prevention does.

OS-U3-Q02MCQeasy

A resource allocation graph contains exactly one instance of every resource type. Which of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. A cycle is necessary but not sufficient for deadlock.
  2. A cycle is both necessary and sufficient for deadlock.
  3. A cycle is sufficient but not necessary for deadlock.
  4. A cycle is neither necessary nor sufficient for deadlock.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When every resource type has a single instance, each resource node has at most one outgoing edge, so the graph collapses into a wait-for graph on processes. In that graph deadlock and cycle are the same thing.

Step by step.

  1. Merge each resource node with its holder: draw Pᵢ → Pⱼ exactly when Pᵢ is waiting for a resource held by Pⱼ.
  2. Deadlock ⇒ cycle. If a set of processes is deadlocked, each member waits for a resource held by another member; following the waits around the set closes a cycle.
  3. Cycle ⇒ deadlock. Take a cycle P₁ → P₂ → … → Pₖ → P₁. Each Pᵢ waits for the single instance held by its successor, and that successor cannot release it, because it is itself waiting. No member can ever proceed.
  4. Hence with single instances the cycle test is a complete decision procedure.

Answer: Option (B) — with one instance per resource type, a cycle is necessary and sufficient for deadlock.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the correct statement only when a resource type may have several instances.
  • (C) reverses the quantifiers: a deadlock always contains a cycle, so a cycle cannot be merely sufficient.
  • (D) denies a standard theorem.

Common misconception. Candidates learn “cycle is necessary, not sufficient” as a slogan and apply it everywhere. That slogan belongs to the multi-instance case; with single instances the cycle test decides.

Takeaway. Single instance → wait-for graph → cycle test. Multiple instances → graph reduction.

OS-U3-Q06MCQmedium

A system has two resource types: R₁ with 2 instances and R₂ with 1 instance. Three processes hold and request them as follows. Allocated : R₁ → P1 , R₁ → P2 , R₂ → P3 Requested : P1 → R₂ , P2 → R₂ , P3 → R₁ Which of the following statements about the current state is TRUE?

  1. The system is deadlocked.
  2. The system is not deadlocked, because R₁ has two instances in total.
  3. The system is not deadlocked, because each process holds at most one resource.
  4. The system is not deadlocked, because a cycle in a graph with multiple instances never implies deadlock.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With more than one instance per resource type a cycle is only a necessary condition, so the verdict comes from graph reduction: repeatedly let a process whose outstanding request can be met finish, and add all of its resources back to the pool.

Step by step.

  1. Compute what is free. R₁: 2 − 2 = 0 available. R₂: 1 − 1 = 0 available.
  2. P1 and P2 both request R₂, whose only instance is held by P3. P3 requests R₁, whose two instances are held by P1 and P2.
  3. No process's request can be satisfied from the available pool, so no process can finish and no edge can be removed. Reduction stalls immediately.
  4. The processes left behind wait only on one another, so they are deadlocked. The cycle is P1 → R₂ → P3 → R₁ → P1.

Answer: Option (A) — the graph cannot be reduced, so the system is deadlocked.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Two instances in total is irrelevant once both are already allocated; the free count is zero.
  • (C) Holding one resource each is exactly the arrangement that closes the cycle.
  • (D) States the correct general rule but misapplies it: here reduction confirms the deadlock rather than dissolving it.

Common misconception. Candidates stop at “multiple instances, so a cycle proves nothing” and never run the reduction. The rule is a warning to check further, not a licence to answer “no deadlock”.

Takeaway. Always compute the free counts first, then reduce. If nothing can finish, the remaining processes are deadlocked regardless of how many instances exist.

OS-U3-Q15MCQeasy

Which of the following statements about denying the mutual-exclusion condition is TRUE?

  1. It can be denied for every resource by declaring all resources shareable.
  2. It cannot be denied for intrinsically non-shareable resources such as a printer or a tape drive, so it is rarely usable as a general prevention technique.
  3. Denying mutual exclusion also denies circular wait.
  4. It is the only condition that cannot be denied in any system.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A resource is non-shareable when concurrent use would corrupt it. For such resources mutual exclusion must hold, so denying it is not a general prevention strategy.

Step by step.

  1. Mutual exclusion is required whenever simultaneous access is unsafe — a printer, a tape drive, a write lock on a file.
  2. For these resources the condition cannot be denied at all, so prevention must attack hold-and-wait or circular wait instead.
  3. The classic partial workaround is spooling: several processes write to a disk queue and one spooler process owns the printer. The printer is then shared only in the sense that the queue is.
  4. Spooling works only for resources whose output can be buffered; it does not generalise to a tape drive or a shared memory region.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Declaring a printer shareable does not make it shareable.
  • (C) The two conditions are independent; denying one says nothing about the other.
  • (D) Mutual exclusion is not the only condition that resists denial, and the phrasing of the option is too absolute.

Common misconception. Assuming every condition is equally deniable. In practice prevention targets hold-and-wait and circular wait, because mutual exclusion usually cannot be removed.

Takeaway. Spooling is the one well-known attack on mutual exclusion, and it applies only to bufferable resources.

OS-U3-Q16MSQeasy

Which of the following are among the four necessary conditions for deadlock? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Mutual exclusion
  2. Hold and wait
  3. No preemption
  4. Priority inversion
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four Coffman conditions are mutual exclusion, hold and wait, no preemption and circular wait. They must hold simultaneously for deadlock to be possible.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Mutual exclusion — a resource is held in a non-shareable mode. Condition.
  2. (B) Hold and wait — a process holds one resource while waiting for another. Condition.
  3. (C) No preemption — a resource is released only voluntarily. Condition.
  4. (D) Priority inversion — a scheduling anomaly in which a low-priority task holding a lock blocks a high-priority one. It is a real-time scheduling problem, not a deadlock condition. Not a condition.
  5. The fourth Coffman condition, circular wait, is absent from the list, so three of the four appear here.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Priority inversion is often grouped with deadlock because both involve blocking. Inversion concerns who gets the CPU while a lock is held; deadlock concerns a closed wait set.

Takeaway. Four conditions, all necessary, all simultaneously required: mutual exclusion, hold and wait, no preemption, circular wait.

OS-U3-Q20MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about a resource allocation graph are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A cycle is a necessary condition for deadlock, whatever the number of instances per resource type.
  2. If every resource type has exactly one instance, a cycle implies deadlock.
  3. If the graph contains no cycle, the system is deadlock-free.
  4. A cycle in a graph in which some resource type has several instances always implies deadlock.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A cycle is always necessary; it is sufficient only when every resource type has a single instance.

Step by step.

  1. (A) In a deadlock every blocked process waits for a resource held by another blocked process; following those waits around the set closes a cycle. TRUE
  2. (B) With single instances no process outside the cycle can release the needed resource, so the cycle can never be broken. TRUE
  3. (C) This is the contrapositive of (A): no cycle ⇒ no deadlock. TRUE
  4. (D) With several instances a process outside the cycle may hold a spare instance and release it, dissolving the cycle; only graph reduction decides. FALSE

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. The pair (B) and (D) is where marks are lost. The correct summary is one sentence: necessary always, sufficient only for single instances.

Takeaway. Cycle ⇒ check further, never a verdict — unless every resource has exactly one instance.

OS-U3B-Q01MCQeasy

A resource allocation graph may contain more than one instance of a resource type. The graph contains a directed cycle. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. A cycle is both necessary and sufficient for deadlock in such a graph.
  2. A cycle is necessary but not sufficient for deadlock in such a graph.
  3. A cycle is sufficient but not necessary for deadlock in such a graph.
  4. A cycle has no bearing on deadlock once a resource type has several instances.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A cycle in the resource allocation graph is always a necessary condition for deadlock. It becomes a sufficient condition only when every resource type has exactly one instance.

Step by step.

  1. Every deadlock corresponds to a closed chain of waiting, which appears as a cycle. So a cycle is necessary.
  2. With multiple instances, several processes may hold different instances of the same resource type, and a cycle can be formed while another free instance still exists.
  3. That free instance can satisfy one of the requests, breaking the apparent chain, so no process need wait forever.
  4. Hence a cycle is necessary but not sufficient; the exact test in that case is graph reduction, not cycle detection.

Answer: Option (B) — a cycle is necessary but not sufficient.

Common misconception. Carrying over the single-instance rule. 'Cycle means deadlock' is valid only when each resource type has one instance; with several instances a cycle can be harmless.

OS-U3B-Q02MCQeasy

In a resource allocation graph, an assignment edge is directed:

  1. from a process to a resource type, showing what the process has asked for.
  2. from a resource instance to the process that holds it.
  3. from one process to another process.
  4. from one resource type to another resource type.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The graph has two edge kinds: a request edge Pᵢ → Rⱼ and an assignment edge Rⱼ → Pᵢ. The direction always runs from the holder of the authority to the receiver.

Step by step.

  1. A request edge leaves the requesting process and points at the resource type it wants: Pᵢ → Rⱼ.
  2. An assignment edge leaves the resource instance and points at the process holding it: Rⱼ → Pᵢ.
  3. So an assignment edge runs resource → process. Option B.
  4. Options C and D describe edges that do not exist in a resource allocation graph; process-to-process edges appear only in the derived wait-for graph.

Answer: Option (B) — from the resource instance to the process holding it.

Common misconception. Reversing the two edge kinds. Request goes process → resource; assignment goes resource → process. Swapping them inverts every cycle you then read off the graph.

OS-U3B-Q03MCQeasy

A resource allocation graph has exactly one instance of every resource type and contains the cycle `P₁ → R₁ → P₂ → R₂ → P₁`. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. Both `P₁` and `P₂` are deadlocked.
  2. Neither process is deadlocked; a cycle of even length is harmless.
  3. Only `P₁` is deadlocked, because it started the cycle.
  4. Nothing can be concluded without the arrival times of the two processes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With a single instance per resource type, a cycle in the resource allocation graph is both necessary and sufficient for deadlock, and every process on the cycle is deadlocked.

Step by step.

  1. P₁ → R₁ is a request edge: P₁ is waiting for R₁.
  2. R₁ → P₂ is an assignment edge: the only instance of R₁ is held by P₂.
  3. P₂ → R₂ means P₂ is waiting for R₂, and R₂ → P₁ means the only instance of R₂ is held by P₁.
  4. P₁ waits for a resource held by P₂, and P₂ waits for a resource held by P₁. Neither can proceed, and neither will release. Both are deadlocked.
  5. The length or parity of the cycle is irrelevant; with single instances any cycle is a deadlock.

Answer: Option (A) — both processes are deadlocked.

Common misconception. Reading the cycle in the wrong direction, or believing the process that 'started' the chain is the only victim. In a closed chain every member waits on the next, so every member is deadlocked.

OS-U3B-Q04MCQmedium

Resource type `R₁` has two instances and `R₂` has one. The graph contains `P₁ → R₂`, `R₂ → P₂`, `P₂ → R₁` and `R₁ → P₁`. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. There is a deadlock, because the graph contains a cycle.
  2. There is no deadlock: a cycle exists, but a free instance of `R₁` can still satisfy `P₂`.
  3. There is no deadlock, because `R₂` is not part of any cycle.
  4. The state cannot be decided without knowing the CPU burst times of `P₁` and `P₂`.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With multiple instances a cycle is only necessary, not sufficient. You must check whether any request on the cycle can be met from the currently free instances.

Step by step.

  1. R₁ has 2 instances and only one is assigned (R₁ → P₁), so one instance of R₁ is free.
  2. R₂ has 1 instance and it is held by P₂.
  3. The cycle is P₁ → R₂ → P₂ → R₁ → P₁: P₁ waits for R₂, P₂ waits for R₁.
  4. P₂'s request is for R₁, and a free instance of R₁ exists, so P₂ can be granted it. P₂ then finishes and releases R₂, which unblocks P₁.
  5. The cycle is therefore broken by an available instance, and there is no deadlock.

Answer: Option (B) — a cycle exists but the system is not deadlocked.

Common misconception. Applying the single-instance rule to a multi-instance graph. Always count the free instances before concluding from a cycle.

OS-U3B-Q05MSQhard

Which of the following statements about resource allocation graphs are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. In a graph with a single instance of every resource type, a knot implies deadlock.
  2. If the graph contains no cycle at all, no process is deadlocked.
  3. In a graph where some resource types have several instances, the presence of a cycle guarantees that the system is deadlocked.
  4. Every deadlocked set of processes corresponds to at least one cycle in the graph.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Cycle and deadlock are linked by a one-way implication that becomes two-way only under the single-instance restriction.

Step by step.

  1. Option A: a knot is a strongly connected subgraph with no edges leaving it. With single instances nothing outside can break it, so every process in the knot is deadlocked. True.
  2. Option B: deadlock implies a closed chain of waiting, which is a cycle. Contrapositively, no cycle means no deadlock. True.
  3. Option C: with multiple instances a cycle can coexist with a free instance that satisfies one of the requests, as in the R₁ with two instances case. The cycle is then not a deadlock. False.
  4. Option D: every deadlocked set has each member waiting for a resource held by another member, and those waits form a closed chain — a cycle. True.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are true. Option (C) is false.

Common misconception. Treating 'cycle' and 'deadlock' as interchangeable. The implication deadlock ⇒ cycle always holds; cycle ⇒ deadlock holds only for single-instance resources.

OS-U3B-Q06NATmedium

Four processes share three resource types, each with exactly one instance. `R₁` is held by `P₁`, `R₂` by `P₂` and `R₃` by `P₃`. The pending requests are `P₁ → R₂`, `P₂ → R₃`, `P₃ → R₁` and `P₄ → R₁`. How many processes are deadlocked, in the sense that each is waiting for a resource held by another member of the deadlocked set?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A process is deadlocked only if it belongs to a closed chain of waits. A process that merely waits on a deadlocked process is blocked, not deadlocked.

Step by step.

  1. Build the wait-for graph: P₁ waits for R₂ held by P₂, so P₁ → P₂.
  2. P₂ waits for R₃ held by P₃, so P₂ → P₃. P₃ waits for R₁ held by P₁, so P₃ → P₁.
  3. These three edges form the cycle P₁ → P₂ → P₃ → P₁. All three processes are in a closed wait set, so all three are deadlocked.
  4. P₄ waits for R₁, held by P₁, so P₄ → P₁. P₄ lies on no cycle: it is not a member of the deadlocked set.
  5. Count of processes inside the deadlock: 3.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Counting P₄ as well, giving 4. P₄ is blocked indefinitely but is not part of the closed set, so under the standard definition it is not deadlocked.

OS-U3B-Q07NAThard

Three resource types have `R₁` with 1 instance, `R₂` with 2 instances and `R₃` with 1 instance. The single instance of `R₁` is held by `P₁`; both instances of `R₂` are held, one by `P₂` and one by `P₃`; the single instance of `R₃` is held by `P₄`. The pending requests are `P₁ → R₂`, `P₂ → R₃`, `P₃ → R₁` and `P₄ → R₂`. How many processes are deadlocked?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. When no instance of any requested resource type is free, every requester is blocked; you must then find the closed chains among the waiters to decide which of them are deadlocked.

Step by step.

  1. Free instances: R₁ = 1 − 1 = 0, R₂ = 2 − 2 = 0, R₃ = 1 − 1 = 0. Nothing is available.
  2. Every pending request is therefore blocked: P₁ needs R₂, P₂ needs R₃, P₃ needs R₁, P₄ needs R₂.
  3. Trace the waits. P₁ waits for R₂, held by P₂ or P₃. P₃ waits for R₁, held by P₁. So P₁ and P₃ wait on each other — a closed chain.
  4. P₂ waits for R₃, held by P₄; P₄ waits for R₂, held by P₂ or P₃. So P₂ and P₄ wait on each other — a second closed chain.
  5. All four processes lie on a closed chain, and with zero free instances nothing can break either chain. All four are deadlocked.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. Assuming a multi-instance graph with no free instances must have every waiter deadlocked — that happens to be true here, but it must be justified by locating the cycles. Conversely, do not assume a cycle is needed in every row of the wait set; it is the closed chain, not the count of waiters, that decides.

starvation-vs-deadlock · 10
OS-U3-Q03MCQeasy

Which of the following best captures the difference between deadlock and starvation?

  1. In deadlock a set of processes waits forever for resources held by one another; in starvation one process waits indefinitely while the other processes keep making progress.
  2. Starvation always leaves a cycle in the wait-for graph, while deadlock never does.
  3. Deadlock ends as soon as the highest-priority process finishes, while starvation can never end.
  4. Starvation requires mutual exclusion, while deadlock does not.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deadlock is a closed failure: every member of the blocked set waits for a member of that same set, so nobody can be released. Starvation is an open failure: the system as a whole progresses, but the allocation policy repeatedly passes one process over.

Step by step.

  1. In deadlock, the blocked set is closed under waiting — each process's requested resource is held by another blocked member — so no member can ever be unblocked.
  2. In starvation, other processes acquire and release the contended resource all the time; only the victim is never selected.
  3. A cycle in the wait-for graph is the signature of deadlock, not of starvation: a starving process has no cycle, only a policy that ignores it.
  4. Therefore the distinguishing feature is whether the rest of the system progresses.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) reverses the facts: a cycle accompanies deadlock, and starvation needs no cycle.
  • (C) describes no real property of either condition.
  • (D) confuses the resource conditions with the scheduling failure.

Common misconception. The two terms are often used as synonyms. A system can be completely deadlock-free and still starve a low-priority process forever.

Takeaway. Deadlock: nobody progresses. Starvation: everybody except one progresses. Cure deadlock by breaking a wait cycle; cure starvation by aging or fair queueing.

OS-U3-Q10MCQmedium

Which of the following statements about starvation (indefinite blocking) is TRUE?

  1. Starvation implies that a deadlock exists somewhere in the system.
  2. A starving process could in principle be executed, but the resource-allocation policy never selects it while other processes keep making progress.
  3. Starvation can always be removed by adding more instances of the contended resource.
  4. Starvation requires a cycle in the wait-for graph.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Starvation is a fairness failure, not a liveness failure: the victim is eligible and the resource is eventually free, but the policy never chooses the victim.

Step by step.

  1. In starvation the process is runnable, and the resource does become free from time to time.
  2. Other processes progress, so the blocked set is not closed — there is no wait cycle.
  3. The cause is the selection rule: a strict priority policy always prefers a higher-priority waiter, so a steady supply of higher-priority requests keeps the victim waiting forever.
  4. Adding instances reduces contention but does not change the rule; a policy that never selects the victim still never selects it.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Starvation and deadlock are independent failures.
  • (C) More resources ease contention but cannot fix an unfair selection rule; aging or fair queueing does.
  • (D) A cycle is the signature of deadlock, and starvation involves no closed wait set.

Common misconception. Treating more resources as the universal cure. The cure for starvation is a change of policy — aging, FIFO queueing, or a bounded number of overtakes.

Takeaway. Starvation is caused by a policy and cured by a policy. Deadlock is caused by a resource cycle and cured by breaking the cycle.

OS-U3-Q19MSQhard

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A process can starve even though no deadlock exists anywhere in the system.
  2. Priority-based resource allocation can starve a low-priority process in a system that is completely deadlock-free.
  3. Every starving process belongs to a deadlocked set.
  4. Adding more instances of the contended resource always ends starvation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Starvation is a fairness failure of the selection policy; deadlock is a closed wait cycle. Neither implies the other.

Step by step.

  1. (A) In a strict-priority system with a steady supply of higher-priority requests, a low-priority process is passed over forever while everyone else progresses. No deadlock is involved. TRUE
  2. (B) Same mechanism, stated in the language of priorities. Deadlock-freedom says nothing about which waiter is chosen. TRUE
  3. (C) A starving process is not part of a closed wait set — the resources it needs are granted, just never to it. FALSE
  4. (D) More instances ease contention, but a policy that always prefers a higher-priority waiter will still never select the victim. FALSE

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. “Starvation is a mild deadlock”. They are different failures with different cures: break a cycle for deadlock, change the policy (aging) for starvation.

Takeaway. Deadlock-free does not mean starvation-free. Test them separately: is there a cycle, and is the selection rule fair?

OS-U3-Q24NATeasy

Consider four resource-allocation policies: (i) first-come first-served, (ii) round robin with a fixed quantum, (iii) strict fixed priority without aging, (iv) shortest-job-first in the presence of a continuous stream of new short jobs. How many of these four can leave a process waiting indefinitely while the other processes keep making progress?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Starvation arises when a policy lets later arrivals overtake a waiter indefinitely. Any policy that guarantees a bounded number of overtakes is starvation-free.

Step by step.

  1. (i) FCFS. A request is served in arrival order, so the wait is bounded by the requests already queued. No starvation.
  2. (ii) Round robin. Every process is served within one full cycle of the queue, so the wait is bounded by the number of processes. No starvation.
  3. (iii) Strict fixed priority. A continuous supply of higher-priority requests always overtakes the victim, which is never selected. Starvation is possible.
  4. (iv) SJF with continuous short arrivals. A long job is overtaken by every newly arriving short job, forever. Starvation is possible.
  5. Two of the four — (iii) and (iv) — can starve a process.

Answer: 2 (range 2 to 2).

Common misconception. Counting FCFS as starvation-prone. FCFS is the textbook starvation-free policy: the only thing it can be blamed for is a long average wait, not indefinite blocking.

Takeaway. Bounded overtaking ⇒ no starvation. Aging converts an unbounded policy into a bounded one by raising the victim's priority with time.

OS-U3B-Q20MCQeasy

Which one of the following describes a process that is starved but not deadlocked?

  1. A low-priority process that has been runnable for hours while higher-priority processes keep arriving.
  2. A process waiting for a resource held by a process that is itself waiting for a resource this process holds.
  3. A process that has terminated but whose resources have not yet been reclaimed.
  4. A process that holds a resource and is waiting for nothing.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Starvation is indefinite postponement by a scheduling or allocation policy; the process is able to make progress in principle but is never selected.

Step by step.

  1. Option A: the process is runnable and could use the CPU, but the policy never picks it. No resource is held by a blocking cycle. This is starvation.
  2. Option B is a closed wait set — a deadlock, not starvation.
  3. Option C is a reclamation issue, not a scheduling one.
  4. Option D describes a process that is simply running; nothing is blocked.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Calling any long wait starvation. Starvation requires that the process could run if selected; a process blocked on a resource held by a deadlocked peer is deadlocked instead.

OS-U3B-Q21MCQeasy

Aging is used to:

  1. increase the priority of a process the longer it waits, so that it is eventually selected.
  2. reduce the priority of long-running processes so that short ones finish first.
  3. prevent deadlock by imposing an order on resource requests.
  4. detect cycles in the wait-for graph.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Aging makes waiting itself improve a process's position, which bounds the time it can be overtaken.

Step by step.

  1. Starvation arises when a process is repeatedly passed over by a policy that does not account for how long it has waited.
  2. Aging raises the process's priority gradually with waiting time.
  3. Once its priority exceeds that of the competing processes, it is selected and runs.
  4. Options B, C and D describe other mechanisms entirely: SJF-like ordering, prevention, and detection.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Confusing aging with priority scheduling. Aging is a correction applied to a priority scheme; it is not itself a scheduling policy.

OS-U3B-Q22MCQeasy

Which one of the following is TRUE about deadlock and starvation?

  1. Deadlock implies starvation, but starvation does not imply deadlock.
  2. Starvation implies deadlock, but deadlock does not imply starvation.
  3. Deadlock and starvation are two names for the same condition.
  4. Neither condition implies the other.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A deadlocked process waits forever, which is indefinite postponement — so deadlock is a special case of starvation. The converse fails because starvation can arise from policy alone.

Step by step.

  1. Every deadlocked process is blocked forever on a resource held by another member of the closed set. That is indefinite waiting, so it is starved. Deadlock ⇒ starvation.
  2. A starved process may be perfectly runnable and merely never chosen, as with strict fixed priority and a steady supply of higher-priority work. No cycle of waits exists. Starvation ⇏ deadlock.
  3. So the implication holds in one direction only. Option A.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Treating them as equivalent. The distinguishing feature is a closed wait set: present in deadlock, absent in pure starvation.

OS-U3B-Q23MCQmedium

A low-priority process holds a mutex that a high-priority process needs, so the high-priority process blocks. Medium-priority processes keep the CPU busy, so the holder never runs. This situation is called:

  1. priority inversion.
  2. a circular wait.
  3. the convoy effect.
  4. thrashing.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Priority inversion is a high-priority task waiting on a low-priority holder that cannot run because medium-priority tasks occupy the processor.

Step by step.

  1. The high-priority process is blocked on a lock, not on the CPU.
  2. The lock holder has lower priority than the medium-priority processes, so it is never scheduled.
  3. The high-priority process therefore waits for an unbounded time, effectively at the mercy of unrelated medium-priority work.
  4. This inversion of the intended priority order is priority inversion; priority inheritance or a priority ceiling is the standard fix.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Calling it a circular wait. There is no cycle — the holder is not waiting for anything the high-priority process holds; it simply never runs.

OS-U3B-Q24MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about starvation and deadlock are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Starvation can often be removed by aging, whereas a deadlock cannot.
  2. A deadlocked process is blocked; a starved process may be runnable and simply never selected.
  3. A deadlock can always be removed by raising the priority of one of the waiting processes.
  4. A strict fixed-priority scheduler can starve a low-priority process even when no deadlock exists.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Starvation is a policy failure, deadlock is a structural one; the remedies differ accordingly.

Step by step.

  1. Option A: aging changes the policy so the waiter eventually wins, which fixes starvation. A deadlock needs a process terminated or a resource preempted; priority changes do not break the cycle. True.
  2. Option B: that is the definitional difference between the two. True.
  3. Option C: raising a priority does not release any resource. If every member of a closed wait set still waits on the next, nothing changes. False.
  4. Option D: with strict priorities and a continuous supply of higher-priority work, the lowest-priority process is never chosen, and no cycle need exist. True.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are true. Option (C) is false.

Common misconception. Assuming a scheduling knob can fix a resource cycle. Priorities affect who runs, not who holds a resource.

OS-U3B-Q25NAThard

A system uses strict fixed-priority preemptive scheduling with no priority inheritance. A low-priority process `L` holds a mutex that a high-priority process `H` needs, so `H` blocks. Two medium-priority processes `M₁` and `M₂` then become runnable. How many of the runnable processes are scheduled while `L` still holds the mutex?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Without priority inheritance, blocking a high-priority process does not raise the holder's priority, so any process ranked above the holder can preempt it.

Step by step.

  1. H is blocked on the mutex, so it is not runnable.
  2. L holds the mutex but has the lowest priority, so both M₁ and M₂ outrank it and preempt it.
  3. M₁ and M₂ therefore run, in priority order, for as long as they are runnable.
  4. L never gets the processor, so it never releases the mutex, and H stays blocked. This is priority inversion.
  5. Runnable processes scheduled while L holds the mutex: M₁ and M₂, that is 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Answering 0 on the assumption that the high-priority process runs first. H cannot run — it is blocked on the mutex, not waiting for the CPU.

deadlock-prevention-avoidance-bankers · 16
OS-U3-Q04MCQeasy

Deadlock avoidance differs from deadlock prevention in that avoidance:

  1. structurally denies one of the four necessary conditions, so deadlock becomes impossible by construction
  2. uses advance information about each process's future maximum claims to keep the system in a safe state
  3. detects cycles in the resource allocation graph and rolls processes back
  4. guarantees that no process ever waits for a resource
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three strategies are distinct. Prevention removes a Coffman condition at design time. Avoidance keeps every condition intact but refuses any allocation that would leave the system without a safe completion order, using each process's declared maximum. Detection lets deadlock happen and then finds it.

Step by step.

  1. Prevention acts on structure: request all resources at once (denies hold and wait), or number the resources (denies circular wait).
  2. Avoidance acts on decisions: every request is granted only if the resulting state is still safe. This needs each process's maximum claim in advance, which is why it is a runtime technique.
  3. Avoidance denies none of the four conditions — processes still hold, still wait, still exclude. It only refuses allocations that could lead to deadlock.
  4. So the distinguishing feature is the use of future maximum claims to maintain safety.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the definition of prevention.
  • (C) is detection followed by recovery.
  • (D) describes no real strategy: waiting is normal even in a deadlock-free system.

Common misconception. Banker's algorithm is often filed under “prevention”. It is avoidance: the four conditions still hold, but the system never enters an unsafe state.

Takeaway. Prevention removes a condition; avoidance never enters an unsafe state; detection finds and breaks. Three strategies, three different guarantees.

OS-U3-Q05MCQeasy

A system state is called safe when:

  1. no process is currently waiting for a resource
  2. there exists an ordering of all processes in which each process's remaining maximum demand can be met from the currently available resources plus the resources released by the processes that precede it in that ordering
  3. every process's current allocation equals its declared maximum
  4. the resource allocation graph contains no cycle
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Safety is defined by the existence of a safe sequence: an order in which every process can be given its full remaining claim, run to completion and release everything, so that the next process in the order can do the same.

Step by step.

  1. Start from the current Available vector.
  2. Repeatedly look for a process whose remaining Need ≤ Available. Let it finish and add its whole allocation back to Available.
  3. If all processes can be finished this way, the state is safe and the order found is a safe sequence.
  4. Safety is a worst-case guarantee: if every process requests at most its declared maximum, a safe state can never deadlock, because the safe sequence always has a next step available.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A state in which processes wait can still be safe; waiting is not deadlock.
  • (C) That is the opposite extreme — every process has already taken everything it may take.
  • (D) Acyclicity is a property of single-instance graphs; it is neither the definition of safety nor equivalent to it.

Common misconception. “Unsafe” is read as “deadlocked”. Unsafe means only that no safe sequence exists at this instant, so deadlock has become possible; the processes may still finish if they request less than their maximum.

Takeaway. Safe ⊂ deadlock-free. Unsafe ⊇ deadlocked. The two inclusions are strict.

OS-U3-Q07MCQmedium

Five processes share three resource types. The current state is: Process | Allocation (A B C) | Max (A B C) | Need (A B C) --------+--------------------+-------------+------------- P0 | 0 1 0 | 7 5 3 | 7 4 3 P1 | 2 0 0 | 3 2 2 | 1 2 2 P2 | 3 0 2 | 9 0 2 | 6 0 0 P3 | 2 1 1 | 2 2 2 | 0 1 1 P4 | 0 0 2 | 4 3 3 | 4 3 1 --------+--------------------+-------------+------------- Available = (3 3 2) Total instances = (10 5 7) Which one of the following is a safe sequence?

  1. P0, P1, P2, P3, P4
  2. P1, P3, P4, P0, P2
  3. P2, P0, P1, P3, P4
  4. P4, P3, P2, P1, P0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The safety algorithm asks, for the first process of a candidate sequence, whether its Need fits the current Available. Only then may it run, and only then is its whole allocation added back.

Step by step.

  1. The table already gives Need = Max − Allocation: P0 (7 4 3), P1 (1 2 2), P2 (6 0 0), P3 (0 1 1), P4 (4 3 1).
  2. Available = (3 3 2). Only P1's need (1 2 2) fits, and P3's (0 1 1) also fits; P0, P2 and P4 do not. So a safe sequence must start with P1 or P3.
  3. Run P1: Available = (3 3 2) + (2 0 0) = (5 3 2).
  4. Run P3: (5 3 2) + (2 1 1) = (7 4 3).
  5. Run P4: (7 4 3) + (0 0 2) = (7 4 5).
  6. Run P0: (7 4 5) + (0 1 0) = (7 5 5), then P2 needs (6 0 0) and finishes. The sequence P1, P3, P4, P0, P2 completes.

Answer: Option (B) — P1, P3, P4, P0, P2.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) starts with P0, whose need (7 4 3) exceeds Available (3 3 2) in A.
  • (C) starts with P2, which needs 6 units of A when only 3 are available.
  • (D) starts with P4, which needs 4 units of A when only 3 are available.

Common misconception. Taking the processes in index order and calling it a safe sequence. A sequence is safe only if every prefix is executable, so the first element alone usually settles it.

Takeaway. Compute Need first, then test the first process of each candidate against Available. Most wrong options die on the very first step.

OS-U3-Q08MCQmedium

Which of the following techniques prevents deadlock by denying the hold-and-wait condition?

  1. Requiring a process to request all the resources it will ever need, in a single operation, before it starts execution
  2. Numbering the resource types and requiring every request to be made in strictly increasing order of number
  3. Allowing the system to preempt a resource from a process that is waiting
  4. Running the banker's safety algorithm before granting each request
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Hold-and-wait means holding one resource while waiting for another. It is denied either by acquiring everything up front, or by releasing everything currently held before issuing a new request.

Step by step.

  1. The condition is: a process holds at least one resource while it waits for another.
  2. If a process must obtain its entire future requirement in one atomic request, it either gets everything (and never waits while holding) or gets nothing (and holds nothing).
  3. The two standard implementations are all at once and release before request; both attack hold-and-wait directly.
  4. Ordering attacks circular wait, preemption attacks no-preemption, and the safety algorithm is avoidance rather than prevention.

Answer: Option (A) — all-at-once allocation denies hold and wait.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) denies circular wait, not hold and wait.
  • (C) denies no preemption.
  • (D) is avoidance: it grants requests whenever the resulting state stays safe.

Common misconception. The four techniques are memorised as a list without their pairings, so any option that “sounds like deadlock prevention” looks acceptable.

Takeaway. Pair each technique with its condition: all-at-once → hold and wait; numbering → circular wait; preemption → no preemption. Mutual exclusion is usually not deniable.

OS-U3-Q11MCQhard

For the state below, the banker's algorithm is used to decide each request. Which one of the following requests must be refused, because granting it would leave the system unsafe? Process | Allocation (A B C) | Max (A B C) | Need (A B C) --------+--------------------+-------------+------------- P0 | 0 1 0 | 7 5 3 | 7 4 3 P1 | 2 0 0 | 3 2 2 | 1 2 2 P2 | 3 0 2 | 9 0 2 | 6 0 0 P3 | 2 1 1 | 2 2 2 | 0 1 1 P4 | 0 0 2 | 4 3 3 | 4 3 1 --------+--------------------+-------------+------------- Available = (3 3 2) Total instances = (10 5 7)

  1. P1 requests (1 2 2)
  2. P0 requests (1 2 2)
  3. P2 requests (3 0 0)
  4. P4 requests (2 2 0)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A request is granted only if it passes two tests: it must be legal (request ≤ Need and request ≤ Available), and the state after the grant must still be safe. Legality alone is not enough.

Step by step.

  1. (A) P1 requests (1 2 2). Available becomes (3 3 2) − (1 2 2) = (2 1 0) and P1's need becomes (0 0 0), so P1 finishes at once and releases (3 2 2), giving (5 3 2). Then P3, P4, P0, P2 complete in that order. Safe.
  2. (B) P0 requests (1 2 2). Available becomes (2 1 0) and P0's need becomes (6 2 1). Now P1 needs (1 2 2) — only one unit of B is free; P2 needs 6 units of A — only 2 are free; P3 needs one unit of C — none is free; P4 needs 4 of A; P0 needs 6 of A. No process can finish, so the state is unsafe.
  3. (C) P2 requests (3 0 0). Available becomes (0 3 2). P3 (0 1 1) finishes, then P1 (1 2 2), then P4 (4 3 1), then P2 (3 0 0), then P0. Safe.
  4. (D) P4 requests (2 2 0). Available becomes (1 1 2). P3 finishes, then P1, then P4, then P2, then P0. Safe.
  5. Only (B) destroys every safe sequence, so only (B) must be refused.

Answer: Option (B) — P0's request for (1 2 2) leaves the system unsafe.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (C) and (D) each admit a complete safe sequence, as traced above, so each may be granted.

Common misconception. Granting a request simply because Available covers it. Covering the request is only the legality test; the safety test is what the banker's algorithm adds, and it is where (B) fails.

Takeaway. Two tests per request: legal, then safe. A request can be perfectly legal and still have to be refused.

OS-U3-Q14MCQeasy

Numbering the resource types and requiring every process to request them in strictly increasing order prevents deadlock because:

  1. a process can never hold a resource while requesting another
  2. the requests of any set of processes cannot form a cycle, so circular wait is impossible
  3. it forces every resource type to have a single instance
  4. it guarantees that the system is always in a safe state according to the banker's algorithm
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Ordering denies the circular wait condition. A process may request a resource only if its number is greater than that of every resource the process already holds.

Step by step.

  1. Assign each resource type a unique number and allow a request for type j only when j is larger than the number of every resource currently held.
  2. Suppose a cycle existed: P₁ waits for a resource held by P₂, …, Pₖ waits for one held by P₁.
  3. Each holder holds a resource numbered lower than the one it is waiting for, so the numbers strictly increase as the cycle is traversed.
  4. Returning to the starting process would require the number to drop back — a contradiction. No cycle can exist, so circular wait is impossible.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A process does hold lower-numbered resources while requesting higher-numbered ones; the rule only constrains the order.
  • (C) The number of instances is untouched by the rule.
  • (D) Safety in the banker's sense is a different guarantee; ordering prevents deadlock structurally and needs no maximum claims.

Common misconception. Confusing prevention with avoidance. Ordering never inspects future claims and never computes a safe sequence — it removes the possibility of a cycle by construction.

Takeaway. Ordering → no cycle → no circular wait. A structural guarantee, with no knowledge of future requests.

OS-U3-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about the banker's algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If the system is in a safe state and every process requests at most its declared maximum, no deadlock can occur.
  2. An unsafe state is necessarily a deadlocked state.
  3. The algorithm requires every process's maximum claim to be known in advance.
  4. If granting a request leaves the system unsafe, granting it will immediately create a deadlock.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Safety is a worst-case guarantee computed from declared maximum claims. Unsafe means no safe sequence exists now; it does not mean that a deadlock exists now.

Step by step.

  1. (A) A safe sequence can always be advanced: the first process in it can obtain its remaining claim and finish. So a safe state cannot deadlock while claims are respected. TRUE
  2. (B) Unsafe means no completion order exists under the worst case. If processes request less than their maximum, they may still finish. FALSE
  3. (C) The remaining claim Max − Allocation is exactly what the safety test compares with Available, so maxima must be declared in advance. This is the algorithm's main practical limitation. TRUE
  4. (D) Refusing an unsafe request is precautionary. Deadlock might never have materialised, because the other processes may not ask for their full maxima. FALSE

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating unsafe and deadlocked as the same. Every deadlocked state is unsafe, but an unsafe state can be deadlock-free at the moment it is examined.

Takeaway. Safe ⇒ deadlock-free (under declared maxima). Unsafe ⇒ deadlock possible, not deadlock certain. The price of the guarantee is advance knowledge of every maximum.

OS-U3-Q21NATeasy

A system has four processes, and each may need at most 3 instances of a single resource type. Instances are acquired one at a time. What is the minimum number of instances of the resource that guarantees the system can never deadlock, whatever the order in which requests are made?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The worst case is that every process holds m − 1 instances and is waiting for one more. With n processes that is n(m − 1) instances all held and stuck; one further instance lets somebody finish.

Step by step.

  1. Here n = 4 processes and m = 3 instances per process at most.
  2. Suppose there are 4 × 2 = 8 instances. Every process can take 2 and then wait for a third. No instance is free, so nobody can proceed — a deadlock is reachable.
  3. With 9 instances, at most 4 × 2 = 8 can be held in that pattern, so at least one instance remains free. The process that gets it completes, releases 3, and the chain unwinds.
  4. Hence the minimum is n(m − 1) + 1 = 4 × 2 + 1 = 9.

Answer: 9 (range 9 to 9).

Common misconception. Answering 12, which is n × m — the total maximum demand. That is sufficient, but far more than the minimum needed to rule deadlock out.

Takeaway. Minimum instances to guarantee deadlock-freedom: n(m − 1) + 1.

OS-U3-Q22NATmedium

Three processes share two resource types, A and B. The current state is: Process | Allocation (A B) | Max (A B) | Need (A B) --------+-------------------+-----------+------------ P1 | 1 0 | 1 3 | 0 3 P2 | 0 2 | 2 2 | 2 0 P3 | 1 1 | 2 1 | 1 0 --------+-------------------+-----------+------------ Available = (0 0) Resource A is not increased. What is the minimum number of additional instances of resource B that must be added to the available pool so that the state becomes safe?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution
