Concept. A state is safe exactly when some process's Need fits Available. Adding instances to B can only help a process whose need is entirely in B — here, only P1.

Step by step.

  1. With b instances of B added, Available is (0, b).
  2. b = 2: P1 needs (0 3) — three units of B, so it is short by one. P2 needs (2 0) and P3 needs (1 0), both needing A, of which none is free. No process can finish, so the state is unsafe. Any b < 3 fails for the same reason.
  3. b = 3: Available is (0 3). P1 needs (0 3) and finishes, releasing (1 0), so Available becomes (1 3).
  4. P3 needs (1 0) and finishes, releasing (1 1), so Available becomes (2 4).
  5. P2 needs (2 0) and finishes. The safe sequence is P1, P3, P2, so three extra instances of B suffice.

Answer: 3 (range 3 to 3).

Common misconception. Adding to resource A, or forgetting that a finishing process releases its whole allocation rather than only what it just received. Both mistakes give a different minimum.

Takeaway. When only one resource type can grow, find the single process whose whole need lies in that resource, and size the addition to it.

OS-U3-Q25NAThard

Three processes share two resource types, A and B, with 4 instances of A and 4 instances of B in total. The current state is: Process | Allocation (A B) | Max (A B) | Need (A B) --------+-------------------+-----------+------------ P1 | 0 2 | 2 3 | 2 1 P2 | 0 0 | 0 2 | 0 2 P3 | 2 1 | 2 2 | 0 1 --------+-------------------+-----------+------------ Available = (2 1) A safe sequence is an ordering of all three processes in which each process can be given its remaining need from the currently available instances plus those released by the processes before it, and then finishes and releases everything. How many distinct safe sequences does this state have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A safe sequence is a completion order found by repeatedly running any process whose Need ≤ Available and adding its allocation back. Counting them means exploring every choice of first process and following the consequences.

Step by step.

  1. Available is (2 1). Compare with each need: P1 needs (2 1) — fits; P2 needs (0 2) — B is short by one, so it cannot go first; P3 needs (0 1) — fits.
  2. P1 first. It releases (0 2), so Available becomes (2 3). Now both P2 (0 2) and P3 (0 1) fit, and each can go second, with the other third: two sequences, P1 P2 P3 and P1 P3 P2.
  3. P3 first. It releases (2 1), so Available becomes (4 2). Now both P1 and P2 fit, giving P3 P1 P2 and P3 P2 P1: two more sequences.
  4. P2 cannot go first at all, so no sequence begins with P2. The total is 2 + 2 = 4.

Answer: 4 (range 4 to 4).

Common misconception. Answering 6 by assuming all three orders of three processes must work. The first process is constrained by the current Available, and P2 needs two units of B when only one is free.

Takeaway. Count from the possible first moves. Any process whose need exceeds the initial Available can never open a sequence, however the rest is ordered.

OS-U3B-Q08MCQeasy

Which one of the following is an avoidance technique rather than a prevention technique?

  1. Requiring every process to request all its resources before it starts running.
  2. Numbering the resource types and forcing requests in strictly increasing order.
  3. Running the banker's algorithm before granting each request.
  4. Spooling printer output so that processes never hold the printer.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Prevention removes one of the four necessary conditions by construction. Avoidance leaves the conditions intact and instead refuses any request that would make the state unsafe, using advance knowledge of maximum claims.

Step by step.

  1. Option A denies hold-and-wait by forcing all-or-nothing acquisition — prevention.
  2. Option B denies circular wait by imposing a global order on resource types — prevention.
  3. Option C does not deny any condition. It grants a request only when the resulting state is still safe, so it needs the maximum claims in advance — avoidance.
  4. Option D denies hold-and-wait for the printer by making the output a shared spool — prevention.

Answer: Option (C) — the banker's algorithm.

Common misconception. Calling the banker's algorithm a prevention method because it stops deadlock. It stops deadlock by refusing unsafe requests at run time, not by removing a structural condition.

OS-U3B-Q09MCQeasy

In the banker's algorithm the `Need` matrix is computed as:

  1. `Need[i][j] = Max[i][j] − Allocation[i][j]`
  2. `Need[i][j] = Allocation[i][j] − Max[i][j]`
  3. `Need[i][j] = Max[i][j] + Allocation[i][j]`
  4. `Need[i][j] = Available[j] − Allocation[i][j]`
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Need is the additional amount of each resource that a process may still ask for. It is the declared maximum minus what the process already holds.

Step by step.

  1. Max[i][j] is the most of resource j that process i will ever hold.
  2. Allocation[i][j] is what process i holds right now.
  3. The remainder Max − Allocation is the most it can still request, which is Need.
  4. Option B would give a non-positive quantity whenever a process holds anything, which is meaningless; options C and D do not describe a residual claim at all.

Answer: Option (A) — Need = Max − Allocation.

Common misconception. Subtracting in the wrong order. If Need comes out negative, the subtraction has been reversed; the maximum can never be less than what is already held.

OS-U3B-Q10MCQmedium

The banker's algorithm reports a state as unsafe. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. The system is deadlocked at this instant.
  2. The system is not deadlocked now, but some sequence of future requests may lead to deadlock.
  3. Deadlock will certainly occur in the future.
  4. The state is also deadlocked, because 'unsafe' and 'deadlocked' mean the same thing.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Safety is about the future. A safe state has at least one complete sequence in which every process can finish; an unsafe state has none, which makes deadlock possible — not certain.

Step by step.

  1. A state is safe if some ordering of all processes lets each one obtain its remaining needs and finish.
  2. An unsafe state has no such ordering, so the algorithm cannot promise that every process will finish.
  3. Unsafe does not mean deadlocked. A deadlocked state is one where processes are already blocked in a cycle; an unsafe state may have all processes still running.
  4. Unsafe also does not guarantee deadlock: the processes may happen to request resources in a fortunate order and all finish anyway.
  5. So the only correct statement is that deadlock is possible, not present and not certain.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Equating unsafe with deadlocked. Every deadlocked state is unsafe, but not every unsafe state is deadlocked — the implication runs one way only.

OS-U3B-Q11MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about prevention, avoidance and detection are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Prevention works by making it impossible for all four necessary conditions to hold at once.
  2. Avoidance needs advance knowledge of each process's maximum resource claim.
  3. Detection lets deadlock occur and then removes it.
  4. Avoidance guarantees that no process ever waits for a resource.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three strategies differ in when they act: before the fact structurally, before each grant predictively, or after the fact reactively.

Step by step.

  1. Option A: prevention attacks at least one of mutual exclusion, hold-and-wait, no preemption or circular wait, so the four can never coexist. True.
  2. Option B: the safety check needs Max for every process to compute Need and to know when a process will finish. True.
  3. Option C: detection runs an algorithm on the current state and, on finding a deadlock, recovers by termination or preemption. True.
  4. Option D: avoidance only refuses requests that would make the state unsafe; processes still wait, and they may wait a long time. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true. Option (D) is false.

Common misconception. Assuming avoidance eliminates waiting. It eliminates deadlock; waiting, and even starvation, remain possible.

OS-U3B-Q12MSQhard

Which of the following statements about the banker's algorithm are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Every process must declare its maximum claim before it starts.
  2. A request may be refused even when the resources it asks for are currently free.
  3. If the state after granting a request is safe, granting it cannot lead to deadlock.
  4. The algorithm guarantees that every process finishes in bounded time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The algorithm grants a request only if the resulting state is safe; availability is a necessary but not a sufficient check.

Step by step.

  1. Option A: Max must be known for every process, otherwise Need cannot be formed and no safe sequence can be tested. True.
  2. Option B: two tests are applied. The request must first be within Need and within Available; then the state after the tentative grant must be safe. A request can pass the first test and fail the second. True.
  3. Option C: that is precisely what 'safe' guarantees — there exists an ordering in which every process completes, so no deadlock can arise from that state. True.
  4. Option D: a process may have its requests refused repeatedly while others are served, so it can be starved. There is no bounded-time guarantee. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true. Option (D) is false.

Common misconception. Confusing deadlock-freedom with starvation-freedom. The banker's algorithm rules out deadlock but leaves starvation possible.

OS-U3B-Q13NATmedium

A system has three processes. Each process needs at most 2 units of resource `A` and at most 2 units of resource `B`. Enter the minimum total number of units — instances of `A` plus instances of `B` — that guarantees the system can never deadlock.

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For one resource type, n processes each needing at most m units are deadlock-free when the instance count is at least n(m − 1) + 1. With several resource types the bound must hold for each type separately.

Step by step.

  1. The bound is applied per resource type: n(m − 1) + 1 with n = 3 processes and m = 2 units each.
  2. 3 × (2 − 1) + 1 = 4 units of A are required.
  3. By the same argument, 4 units of B are required.
  4. The question asks for the total, so add the two per-type minima: 4 + 4 = 8.
  5. Why the bound works: if only n(m − 1) units of a type existed, every process could hold m − 1 and each would need one more — a deadlock. One extra unit lets some process finish and release everything.

Answer: 8

Common misconception. Applying n(m − 1) + 1 once to the total and reporting 4. The bound is per resource type; the total is the sum over types.

deadlock-detection-recovery · 11
OS-U3-Q09MCQmedium

Which statement about how often the deadlock-detection algorithm should be invoked is TRUE?

  1. It should run as rarely as possible, because a deadlocked process holding resources harms no one.
  2. Running it more often raises the CPU cost of detection but leaves fewer processes holding resources uselessly; running it rarely saves CPU but lets deadlocks accumulate.
  3. Its cost is independent of how often it is invoked.
  4. It may be invoked only when the resource allocation graph is acyclic.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Detection is a trade-off between the overhead of running it and the loss caused by undetected deadlocked processes, which hold resources while doing no work.

Step by step.

  1. Each invocation costs time proportional to the size of the graph — the number of processes and resource types.
  2. Every deadlocked process keeps its resources but can never finish, so the longer detection is delayed the more resources sit idle.
  3. Frequent invocation therefore buys resource utilisation at the price of CPU time, and rare invocation does the reverse. A common practical heuristic is to detect when resource utilisation falls or when a request cannot be satisfied.
  4. There is no single correct frequency; it depends on how often deadlock is expected and how expensive an idle process is.

Answer: Option (B) — detection frequency trades CPU overhead against resources left idle by undetected deadlocks.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) A deadlocked process harms the system precisely by holding resources forever.
  • (C) Cost grows with the number of invocations by definition.
  • (D) An acyclic graph needs no detection at all, so this cannot be a condition on invoking it.

Common misconception. Assuming a fixed rule such as “detect on every request”. Detection frequency is a design decision, and systems often choose an intermediate point.

Takeaway. Detection frequency = cost of detection against cost of idleness. State the trade-off, not a fixed number.

OS-U3-Q12MCQhard

The graph-reduction algorithm repeatedly removes a process whose outstanding requests can all be met from the currently available instances, adding that process's resources back to the pool. Which of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. If the graph is completely reducible, no process in the system is deadlocked.
  2. If the graph is not completely reducible, every process in the system is deadlocked.
  3. Reduction can be applied only when every resource type has a single instance.
  4. A reducible graph must be acyclic, and every acyclic graph is reducible.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reducibility is an exact test for deadlock-freedom. A complete reduction exhibits a completion order for every process; a stalled reduction leaves behind exactly the processes that cannot complete.

Step by step.

  1. If every process can be reduced away, the order of removals is a completion order: each process's requests could be met at the moment it was removed. Some schedule therefore finishes every process, so nobody is deadlocked.
  2. If reduction stalls, the processes still in the graph wait only on one another, and those are deadlocked. Processes already removed are not, so “every process” in (B) overstates the conclusion.
  3. Reduction uses instance counts, so it works with any number of instances per resource type; (C) is false.
  4. A reducible graph may contain a cycle. Take R₁ with two instances, one held by P1 and one free, and R₂ held by P2, with P1 requesting R₂ and P2 requesting R₁. The cycle P1 → R₂ → P2 → R₁ → P1 exists, yet P2 can take the free instance of R₁, finish, and release R₂, after which P1 finishes. The graph reduces. So (D) is false.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Only the processes left after the reduction stalls are deadlocked.
  • (C) Reduction is designed precisely for the multi-instance case.
  • (D) Cycles are common in reducible, deadlock-free graphs.

Common misconception. Equating “reducible” with “acyclic”. With multiple instances a cycle is a warning, not a verdict; only reduction decides.

Takeaway. Reduction answers the only question that matters: can every process complete? Complete → deadlock-free. Stalled → the leftovers are deadlocked.

OS-U3-Q13MCQhard

Which of the following statements about recovery from a detected deadlock is TRUE?

  1. Recovery by termination always requires aborting every deadlocked process and restarting all of them from the beginning.
  2. Preempting a resource from a victim and rolling that process back to an earlier checkpoint is a recovery technique, and the choice of victim affects both the cost of recovery and the risk of starving the same process repeatedly.
  3. Rollback is possible only if the wait-for graph still contains a cycle at the moment of rollback.
  4. Preempting a resource can never cause starvation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recovery has two families: termination (abort all deadlocked processes, or abort one at a time and re-run detection) and resource preemption (select a victim, preempt, roll back to a checkpoint, guard against repeated victimisation).

Step by step.

  1. Aborting all deadlocked processes certainly breaks the deadlock but throws away the maximum amount of work. Aborting one at a time and re-running detection costs many detection passes but discards less.
  2. Resource preemption needs three decisions: victim selection (usually the one with the smallest cost), rollback (return the victim to a state from which it can resume, which requires checkpoints), and starvation prevention (bound the number of times any process may be rolled back).
  3. Victim selection therefore drives both how much work is lost and whether one process is picked again and again.
  4. Hence (A) is too strong — partial termination is standard; (C) is false, because rollback is a recovery action available whenever preemption is permitted; (D) is false, since repeated victimisation is the classic starvation risk of preemption.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Aborting one process at a time until the cycle disappears is usually cheaper.
  • (C) Rollback is a property of the victim and its checkpoint, not of the graph's shape.
  • (D) Repeatedly choosing the same victim is a well-known source of starvation.

Common misconception. Recovery is often reduced to “kill a process”. Preemption with rollback is the other half, and it introduces its own fairness problem.

Takeaway. Three recovery decisions: which victim, how far to roll back, and how to stop the same process being chosen forever.

OS-U3-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following are recognised techniques for recovering from a detected deadlock? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Abort all deadlocked processes.
  2. Abort one deadlocked process at a time and re-run detection after each abort.
  3. Preempt a resource from a chosen victim process and roll that process back to an earlier checkpoint.
  4. Refuse every future request for the contended resource until the deadlocked processes exit on their own.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recovery is either termination or resource preemption. Both act on processes that are already deadlocked.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Aborting every deadlocked process certainly removes the deadlock, at the maximum cost in lost work. Technique.
  2. (B) Aborting one process at a time and re-running detection after each abort trades detection overhead for less discarded work. Technique.
  3. (C) Preemption with rollback to a checkpoint is the standard non-terminating recovery. Technique.
  4. (D) A deadlocked process never exits on its own — that is the definition of the condition — so this is not recovery at all. Not a technique.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Option (D) sounds cautious and therefore plausible. But recovery must break the wait; waiting for a deadlocked set to finish is waiting forever.

Takeaway. Recovery = terminate (all at once or one at a time) or preempt with rollback. Nothing else removes the deadlock.

OS-U3-Q23NATmedium

A system has exactly one instance of every resource type, and its wait-for graph has these edges: P1 → P2, P2 → P3, P3 → P1, P2 → P4, P4 → P5, P5 → P2. What is the minimum number of processes that must be aborted so that the remaining graph contains no cycle?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Breaking every cycle means choosing a set of vertices that meets every cycle — a feedback vertex set. Cycles that share a vertex are broken by aborting that one vertex.

Step by step.

  1. Find the cycles. Following the edges: P1 → P2 → P3 → P1 is one cycle.
  2. Also P2 → P4 → P5 → P2 is a second, independent cycle.
  3. Both cycles pass through P2. Aborting P2 removes all of its outgoing edges, so neither cycle survives: the remaining edges are only those leaving P1, P3, P4 and P5 to processes that no longer close a loop.
  4. At least one abort is necessary, since the graph is not acyclic, so one is the minimum.

Answer: 1 (range 1 to 1).

Common misconception. Aborting one process per cycle and answering 2. The cycles intersect, so a single carefully chosen victim clears both — the same idea as picking a good victim in recovery.

Takeaway. Look for the shared vertex before counting cycles. One well-chosen abort can be worth several careless ones.

OS-U3B-Q14MCQeasy

The wait-for graph of a system is obtained from its resource allocation graph by:

  1. deleting the resource nodes and drawing `Pᵢ → Pⱼ` whenever `Pᵢ` waits for a resource that `Pⱼ` holds; this is exact only when every resource type has a single instance.
  2. reversing every assignment edge and keeping the request edges.
  3. merging all resource nodes into one and keeping every edge.
  4. keeping only the request edges and discarding the assignments.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The wait-for graph collapses each two-step wait into a single process-to-process edge, which is unambiguous only when a resource has exactly one holder.

Step by step.

  1. A request edge Pᵢ → Rⱼ followed by an assignment edge Rⱼ → Pₖ means Pᵢ waits for Pₖ.
  2. Collapsing that pair gives Pᵢ → Pₖ, and the resource node disappears.
  3. If Rⱼ had several instances, Pᵢ might be satisfiable by any free instance or by more than one holder, so the collapse would lose information. Hence the single-instance restriction.
  4. With single instances, a cycle in the wait-for graph is exactly a deadlock.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Using the wait-for graph on a system whose resources have several instances. The collapse is then not faithful and a cycle may be reported where none is fatal.

OS-U3B-Q15MCQeasy

For resources with several instances, the detection algorithm based on graph reduction declares a deadlock when:

  1. any process has an outstanding request.
  2. the graph contains a cycle.
  3. after reduction, at least one process remains whose request cannot be met from the currently available instances.
  4. the available vector is all zeros.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reduction is the exact multi-instance test: repeatedly satisfy a process whose entire outstanding request can be met, add its holdings back, and repeat. Processes that survive are deadlocked.

Step by step.

  1. Start from the currently available vector.
  2. Find any process whose outstanding request is at most the available vector. Grant it, let the process finish, and return its holdings to the pool.
  3. Repeat until no further process can be reduced.
  4. Any process still unreduced is waiting on resources held only by other unreduced processes — that set is deadlocked. Option C.
  5. Option B is the single-instance shortcut only; option D is neither necessary nor sufficient.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Using cycle detection as the test for multi-instance resources. Cycles can be harmless there; only reduction decides.

OS-U3B-Q16MCQmedium

Which one of the following is a resource-preemption recovery technique rather than a process-termination technique?

  1. Abort every process in the cycle.
  2. Abort one process at a time until the cycle disappears.
  3. Take a resource from one holder, give it to a waiting process, and roll the victim back to a checkpoint.
  4. Refuse all further requests from the deadlocked processes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recovery is either by terminating processes or by preempting resources; preemption requires a rollback point so the victim can be restarted consistently.

Step by step.

  1. Options A and B both end processes — termination.
  2. Option C removes a resource from a holder without ending it, and restores the victim to a saved state. That is preemption.
  3. Option D does not resolve anything; it merely freezes the deadlocked processes in place.
  4. So option C is the only preemption technique listed.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Treating preemption as free. A preempted process loses all progress since its last checkpoint, so rollback cost is the price of choosing preemption over termination.

OS-U3B-Q17MCQmedium

Rollback recovery from a deadlock requires that the system:

  1. keeps checkpoints of process state so that a victim can be restarted.
  2. guarantees that a preempted process will never need that resource again.
  3. reorders all pending requests into increasing resource order.
  4. runs the banker's algorithm before every allocation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Preempting a resource leaves the victim in an inconsistent state unless a saved state exists to return to.

Step by step.

  1. Preemption removes a resource the victim already holds, so the victim's computation is no longer valid.
  2. To recover, the victim must be rolled back to a point where it did not hold that resource.
  3. That point is a checkpoint. Without checkpoints the victim can only be terminated and restarted from the beginning.
  4. Options B, C and D describe prevention or avoidance measures, not a prerequisite for rollback.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Thinking preemption needs only the resource to be freed. It also needs a consistent state to return the victim to; otherwise the process must be killed outright.

OS-U3B-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about deadlock detection are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Running the detection algorithm on every request is exact but expensive.
  2. A detection algorithm invoked only when the CPU is idle may leave a deadlock undetected for some time.
  3. The choice of detection frequency trades CPU cost against the number of processes left blocked.
  4. Once a deadlock is detected, it can always be resolved without terminating any process.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Detection frequency is a cost-benefit decision: detecting more often costs CPU time, detecting less often costs resources held by blocked processes.

Step by step.

  1. Option A: invoking on every request gives the earliest possible detection but the algorithm runs constantly. True.
  2. Option B: idle-time invocation is cheap, but a deadlock that forms while the system is busy waits until the system empties — which it may never do, since deadlocked processes keep it non-idle. True.
  3. Option C: that is exactly the trade-off — more frequent runs cost CPU, less frequent runs waste resources. True.
  4. Option D: preemption recovery needs a checkpoint to roll back to. If none exists, the only option is termination. False.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true. Option (D) is false.

Common misconception. Believing a detected deadlock always has a non-destructive fix. Without checkpoints, preemption degrades into termination.

OS-U3B-Q19NAThard

Resource type `A` has 3 instances, `B` has 2 and `C` has 1. `P₁` holds one `A`, `P₂` holds one `A`, `P₃` holds one `B` and `P₄` holds the one `C`. The pending requests are `P₁ → B`, `P₂ → C`, `P₃ → A` and `P₄ → A`. Run the graph-reduction deadlock-detection algorithm. How many processes remain unreduced?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reduction repeatedly completes any process whose outstanding request fits the available vector, returning its holdings to the pool.

Step by step.

  1. Initial available: A = 3 − 2 = 1, B = 2 − 1 = 1, C = 1 − 1 = 0.
  2. P₁ needs one B; one is available. P₁ finishes and releases its A, so A = 2, B returns to 1.
  3. P₃ needs one A; two are available. P₃ finishes and releases its B, so B = 2.
  4. P₄ needs one A; two are available. P₄ finishes and releases C, so C = 1.
  5. P₂ needs one C; one is available. P₂ finishes.
  6. Every process was reduced, so nothing remains. The system is not deadlocked.

Answer: 0

Common misconception. Reading the pending requests as a cycle and answering non-zero. The requests form no closed chain here, and reduction shows every process can be completed in the order P₁, P₃, P₄, P₂.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 4 — Memory Management & Paging
contiguous-allocation-first-best-worst-fit · 13
OS-U4-Q01MCQeasy

A memory manager keeps five free holes in address order: 100 KB, 500 KB, 200 KB, 300 KB and 600 KB. A new process requests 212 KB. Using first fit, which hole is chosen?

  1. the 500 KB hole
  2. the 600 KB hole
  3. the 200 KB hole
  4. none — the request fails
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. First fit scans the free list in address order and takes the first hole that is large enough, without looking further.

Step by step.

  1. The 100 KB hole is smaller than 212 KB, so it is skipped.
  2. The next hole, 500 KB, is at least 212 KB, so the request is satisfied there.
  3. The remainder, 500 − 212 = 288 KB, goes back on the free list.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Choosing the 600 KB hole (worst fit) or the 300 KB hole (best fit). Both are legitimate policies, but neither is first fit, which never scans past the first adequate hole.

OS-U4-Q02MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about fragmentation is TRUE?

  1. Contiguous allocation suffers internal fragmentation but not external fragmentation.
  2. Paging suffers external fragmentation but not internal fragmentation.
  3. Contiguous allocation suffers external fragmentation, while a paging system wastes space only inside the last page of a process.
  4. Both contiguous allocation and paging suffer only external fragmentation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. External fragmentation is free space that exists but is split into pieces too small to be used. Internal fragmentation is unused space inside an allocated block.

Step by step.

  1. In contiguous allocation a process gets one run of memory, so when processes finish the free space breaks into holes between the survivors: external fragmentation.
  2. Paging hands a process whole frames, so any free frame can be used and no usable free space is left between allocations: there is no external fragmentation.
  3. The last page of a process is only partly filled, so the unused tail of that frame is lost: internal fragmentation.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Believing paging removes all waste. It removes external fragmentation only, and it trades it for internal fragmentation that averages half a page per process.

OS-U4-Q03MCQmedium

Free holes of 200 KB, 500 KB, 300 KB and 600 KB are available, in that address order, and a process requests 250 KB. Which placement policy leaves the smallest leftover hole?

  1. first fit, leaving 250 KB
  2. best fit, leaving 50 KB
  3. worst fit, leaving 350 KB
  4. next fit, leaving 250 KB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each policy selects a different hole, and the leftover is the chosen hole minus the request.

Step by step.

  1. First fit takes the first adequate hole, 500 KB, leaving 500 − 250 = 250 KB.
  2. Best fit takes the smallest adequate hole, 300 KB, leaving 300 − 250 = 50 KB.
  3. Worst fit takes the largest hole, 600 KB, leaving 600 − 250 = 350 KB.
  4. Next fit, starting from the beginning of the list here, also takes 500 KB and leaves 250 KB.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Reading "smallest leftover" as "best overall policy". Best fit does leave the smallest leftover, but that same behaviour shreds memory into holes too small to be reused, which is why it is not automatically the best choice.

OS-U4-Q04MCQhard

A system uses first fit with no compaction. The free holes, in address order, are 100 KB, 500 KB, 200 KB, 300 KB and 600 KB. Requests of 212 KB, 417 KB, 112 KB and 426 KB arrive in that order. Which requests fail?

  1. only the 417 KB request
  2. only the 426 KB request
  3. the 417 KB and 426 KB requests
  4. none — all four requests fit
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. First fit restarts its scan at the beginning of the free list for every request, and a hole is split, never merged with a neighbour that is still in use.

Step by step.

  1. 212 KB: the 100 KB hole is too small; the 500 KB hole is taken and split into 288 KB. Free list: 100, 288, 200, 300, 600.
  2. 417 KB: the first adequate hole is 600 KB, split into 183 KB. Free list: 100, 288, 200, 300, 183.
  3. 112 KB: the first adequate hole is 288 KB, split into 176 KB. Free list: 100, 176, 200, 300, 183.
  4. 426 KB: the largest hole is now 300 KB, so no hole can hold it and the request fails.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Adding up the free space (100 + 176 + 200 + 300 + 183 = 959 KB, far more than 426 KB) and concluding the request must succeed. First fit needs one contiguous hole, not the total of several.

OS-U4-Q05MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about first fit, best fit and worst fit are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Best fit tends to leave very small holes that are often too small to be reused.
  2. First fit can stop at the first adequate hole, so it is usually faster than best fit.
  3. Worst fit always produces less external fragmentation than best fit.
  4. Next fit resumes the scan where the previous allocation ended, which spreads allocations across memory instead of concentrating them at the low addresses.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three policies differ only in which adequate hole they choose; the cost of searching and the shape of the holes left behind are the practical consequences.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: best fit minimises the leftover, so it produces many slivers of unusable size.
  2. (B) is true: first fit stops at the first adequate hole, while best fit must scan the whole list to be sure it has the smallest adequate one.
  3. (C) is false: worst fit leaves a large remainder that is more likely to be reusable, but it is not guaranteed to beat best fit on external fragmentation — simulations show it is often worse.
  4. (D) is true: next fit continues from the last allocation point, so it spreads allocations across memory rather than always splitting the low-address holes.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (D)

Common misconception. Treating "worst fit" as the cure for fragmentation. It leaves big remainders, but it also destroys the large holes that big requests need.

OS-U4-Q06MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about contiguous allocation are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Compaction can move a running process only if its addresses are translated at run time, that is, with dynamic relocation.
  2. Internal fragmentation is wasted space inside a block that has already been allocated to a process.
  3. When a process terminates, adjacent free blocks must be coalesced, or the free space stays split.
  4. A paging system can suffer external fragmentation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Contiguous allocation binds a process to one run of physical memory, so the layout of free space and the binding of addresses are both policy questions.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: compaction copies processes to new addresses, which is impossible if the program was bound to absolute addresses at load time; base and limit registers make it possible.
  2. (B) is true by definition: the allocated block is larger than the request, and the surplus is unusable by anyone else.
  3. (C) is true: without coalescing, neighbouring free blocks stay separate, so a later request may fail even though the combined space would satisfy it.
  4. (D) is false: frames are all the same size, so any free frame fits any page. Paging has internal fragmentation but no external fragmentation.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (C)

Common misconception. Assuming compaction is always available. It costs CPU time and needs dynamic relocation, and it is impossible in some systems.

OS-U4-Q07NAThard

Free holes of 300 KB, 600 KB, 350 KB, 200 KB and 450 KB are available. Requests of 250 KB, 400 KB and 200 KB arrive in that order and are placed by worst fit. What is the size, in KB, of the largest hole remaining after all three requests have been placed?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Worst fit always takes the largest available hole, so the largest hole is consumed first and the remainders are as large as possible.

Step by step.

  1. Holes: 300, 600, 350, 200, 450. The largest is 600, so the 250 KB request takes it and leaves 350. Holes: 300, 350, 350, 200, 450.
  2. The largest hole is now 450, so the 400 KB request takes it and leaves 50. Holes: 300, 350, 350, 200, 50.
  3. The largest hole is 350, so the 200 KB request takes it and leaves 150. Holes: 300, 150, 350, 200, 50.
  4. The largest hole remaining is 350 KB.

Answer: 350

Common misconception. Sorting the holes once and using that order throughout. The list must be re-examined after every placement, because the hole just split is no longer the largest.

OS-U4-S2-Q01MCQeasy

A memory manager keeps four free holes in address order: 250 KB, 600 KB, 400 KB and 300 KB. A process requests 380 KB. Using best fit, which hole is chosen?

  1. the 250 KB hole
  2. the 600 KB hole
  3. the 400 KB hole
  4. none — the request fails
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Best fit scans the whole free list and takes the smallest hole that is at least as large as the request. Only holes that can hold the request are candidates.

Step by step.

  1. The 250 KB hole is smaller than 380 KB, so it is not a candidate.
  2. The 600 KB hole fits, leaving 220 KB.
  3. The 400 KB hole fits, leaving 20 KB, and the 300 KB hole does not fit at all.
  4. Of the candidates 600 KB and 400 KB, the smaller is 400 KB, so best fit chooses it.

Option (C)

Common misconception. Answering 600 KB, which is what worst fit would choose, or scanning for the hole whose address is closest to the request. Best fit compares sizes, never addresses.

OS-U4-S2-Q02NATeasy

Free holes of 500 KB, 200 KB, 700 KB and 300 KB lie in address order. Two requests of 450 KB and 260 KB arrive in that order and are placed by first fit, each scan restarting at the beginning of the list, with remainders split off and no coalescing. What is the size, in KB, of the largest hole remaining after both requests?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. First fit takes the first hole in address order that is large enough, splits off the remainder and leaves it in place. Each new request restarts the scan at the beginning of the list.

Step by step.

  1. The 450 KB request meets the 500 KB hole first and it is large enough, so it is taken at once, leaving 500 − 450 = 50 KB. Free list: 50, 200, 700, 300.
  2. The 260 KB request scans from the start: 50 is too small and 200 is too small, so the 700 KB hole is taken, leaving 700 − 260 = 440 KB. Free list: 50, 200, 440, 300.
  3. The largest hole is therefore 440 KB.

Answer: 440

Common misconception. Scanning the holes from the largest instead of from the lowest address. That is worst fit, and it would have placed the 450 KB request in the 700 KB hole.

OS-U4-S2-Q03MCQmedium

Free holes of 400 KB, 250 KB, 550 KB and 300 KB are available. Requests of 200 KB, 350 KB and 150 KB arrive in that order and are placed by worst fit. What is the size, in KB, of the largest hole remaining after all three requests?

  1. 300 KB
  2. 350 KB
  3. 200 KB
  4. 250 KB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Worst fit always allocates from the largest hole, so the largest hole must be recomputed after every placement.

Step by step.

  1. 200 KB takes the 550 KB hole, leaving 350. Holes: 400, 250, 350, 300.
  2. 350 KB takes the 400 KB hole, leaving 50. Holes: 50, 250, 350, 300.
  3. 150 KB takes the 350 KB hole, leaving 200. Holes: 50, 250, 200, 300.
  4. The largest hole left is 300 KB.

Option (A)

Common misconception. Sorting the holes once and then walking that order. The 350 KB remainder created by the first placement becomes the largest hole at the next step, so the order must be rebuilt each time.

OS-U4-S2-Q04MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about contiguous-allocation policies are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Worst fit leaves a remainder at least as large as best fit would leave for the same hole set and request.
  2. Best fit tends to leave many holes so small that they cannot be reused.
  3. Compaction is possible only when a process can be relocated at run time.
  4. First fit always produces less external fragmentation than best fit.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Best fit picks the smallest adequate hole, worst fit the largest. Since the largest adequate hole is at least as big as the smallest one, worst fit always leaves a remainder at least as large.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: remainder = chosen hole − request, and the largest adequate hole ≥ the smallest adequate hole, so worst fit leaves the bigger remainder.
  2. (B) is true: shaving the request off the closest-fitting hole produces slivers too small to be reused.
  3. (C) is true: compaction moves processes, which is impossible unless addresses are bound at run time (base and limit registers or page tables).
  4. (D) is false: no policy dominates. Simulation shows first fit is usually faster and often as good, but there are sequences on which best fit fragments less.

Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Treating best fit as the policy that wastes least memory. It wastes least per allocation and therefore creates the most unusable slivers.

OS-U4-S2-Q05MCQhard

Free holes of 200 KB, 600 KB, 150 KB and 400 KB lie in address order. Two requests, 500 KB then 250 KB, are placed by first fit, each scan restarting at the beginning of the list, with remainders split off and no coalescing. What is the largest single request, in KB, that can still be satisfied afterwards?

  1. 100 KB
  2. 150 KB
  3. 200 KB
  4. 600 KB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The answer is the largest hole that survives, because a request must fit inside a single contiguous hole. The total of all free holes is irrelevant.

Step by step.

  1. 500 KB scans 200 (too small), then takes the 600 KB hole and leaves 100 KB. Holes: 200, 100, 150, 400.
  2. 250 KB scans 200 (too small), 100 (too small), 150 (too small), then takes the 400 KB hole and leaves 150 KB. Holes: 200, 100, 150, 150.
  3. The largest surviving hole is 200 KB.

Option (C)

Common misconception. Adding the free holes (200 + 100 + 150 + 150 = 600 KB) and concluding a 600 KB request fits. First fit needs one hole large enough, not the sum of several.

OS-U4-S2-Q06NATmedium

Holes of 300 KB, 150 KB, 500 KB and 250 KB are available. Requests of 140 KB, 480 KB, 200 KB and 260 KB arrive in that order and are placed by best fit. What is the size, in KB, of the largest hole remaining after all four requests?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Best fit takes the smallest adequate hole at every step, so the leftover is always as small as the free list allows. The largest surviving hole must be recomputed after each of the four placements.

Step by step.

  1. 140 KB: the smallest adequate hole is 150, leaving 10. Holes: 300, 10, 500, 250.
  2. 480 KB: the smallest adequate hole is 500, leaving 20. Holes: 300, 10, 20, 250.
  3. 200 KB: the smallest adequate hole is 250, leaving 50. Holes: 300, 10, 20, 50.
  4. 260 KB: the smallest adequate hole is 300, leaving 40. Holes: 40, 10, 20, 50.
  5. The largest hole left is 50 KB.

Answer: 50

Common misconception. Reading the holes in address order. Best fit compares every hole on each request, so the 500 KB hole is passed over for the 480 KB request only because no smaller hole fits it.

paging-page-table-structures · 13
OS-U4-Q08MCQeasy

In a paging system, an entry of a per-process page table normally maps:

  1. a physical frame number to the virtual page that occupies it
  2. a segment number to the base address of that segment
  3. a virtual page number to the physical frame number that holds it
  4. a virtual address to the disk block that holds the page
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each process has its own page table, indexed by virtual page number; the entry at that index holds the frame number where the page currently sits.

Step by step.

  1. The virtual address is split into a page number and an offset.
  2. The page number indexes this process's page table.
  3. The entry read out is the frame number; the offset is copied unchanged into the physical address.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Thinking the table maps frames back to pages. That is the inverted page table, which is a single system-wide structure indexed by frame number, not the ordinary per-process page table.

OS-U4-Q09MCQeasy

A machine has a 32-bit logical address and a page size of 4 KB. How many pages can a single process address?

  1. 2²⁰
  2. 2¹²
  3. 2¹⁰
  4. 2³²
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The page size fixes the number of offset bits; the remaining bits of the logical address are the page number.

Step by step.

  1. 4 KB = 2¹² bytes, so the offset needs 12 bits.
  2. The page number therefore has 32 − 12 = 20 bits.
  3. The number of distinct pages is 2²⁰ = 1 048 576.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Reporting 2¹², which is the number of bytes in a page, or 2³², which is the number of bytes addressable, not the number of pages.

OS-U4-Q10MCQmedium

A TLB is searched in 10 ns, a main-memory access costs 100 ns, and the system uses a single-level page table. The TLB hit ratio is 0.9. What is the effective memory access time?

  1. 110 ns
  2. 120 ns
  3. 130 ns
  4. 210 ns
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With a single-level page table a TLB hit costs one memory access, while a TLB miss costs two: one to read the page-table entry and one to read the data.

Step by step.

  1. Hit: 10 + 100 = 110 ns, and this happens 90% of the time.
  2. Miss: 10 + 100 + 100 = 210 ns, and this happens 10% of the time.
  3. EAT = 0.9 × 110 + 0.1 × 210 = 99 + 21 = 120 ns.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Charging a miss only one memory access (the data access) and forgetting the page-table read, which gives 0.9 × 110 + 0.1 × 110 = 110 ns.

OS-U4-Q11MCQhard

A system uses a two-level page table, a TLB search of 1 ns and a main-memory access of 100 ns. The measured effective memory access time is 141 ns. What is the TLB hit ratio?

  1. 0.70
  2. 0.75
  3. 0.80
  4. 0.90
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A two-level page table needs two page-table memory reads on a TLB miss, plus the data access; a TLB hit needs only the data access.

Step by step.

  1. Hit: 1 + 100 = 101 ns.
  2. Miss: 1 + 100 + 100 + 100 = 301 ns.
  3. EAT = 101h + 301(1 − h) = 301 − 200h.
  4. Setting 301 − 200h = 141 gives 200h = 160, so h = 0.8.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Using the single-level miss cost of 201 ns, which gives h = 0.6, a value not even among the options. Count one memory read per level of the page table.

OS-U4-Q12MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about paging and the TLB are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A larger page size makes the page table smaller but increases internal fragmentation.
  2. Two processes can share one physical frame by mapping different virtual pages to it.
  3. Every TLB miss causes a page fault.
  4. The page table is kept in main memory, and the TLB caches recently used entries of it.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Page size trades table size against internal fragmentation; the TLB is only a cache of page-table entries, not a record of which pages are resident.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: fewer, larger pages mean fewer page-table entries, but the unused tail of the last page is larger on average.
  2. (B) is true: give both processes a page-table entry pointing at the same frame, and the code or data is physically shared.
  3. (C) is false: a miss means only that the mapping is not cached; the page-table entry may be present and valid, and the walk succeeds.
  4. (D) is true: the page table lives in memory and the TLB holds a small number of its entries.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (D)

Common misconception. Confusing a TLB miss with a page fault. A page fault means the valid bit is clear and the page must be brought in from secondary storage.

OS-U4-Q13NATeasy

A system uses pages of 8 KB and a 32-bit logical address. How many bits of the logical address specify the page number?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Logical address = page number bits followed by offset bits, and the offset must be able to address every byte inside one page.

Step by step.

  1. 8 KB = 8 × 1024 = 8192 = 2¹³ bytes, so the offset takes 13 bits.
  2. The page-number field takes the rest: 32 − 13 = 19 bits.

Answer: 19

Common misconception. Answering 13, the offset width, or 8, the power of two in "8 KB". The question asks for the page-number field.

OS-U4-S2-Q07MCQeasy

A machine has 48-bit virtual addresses and 8 KB pages. How many entries does a single-level page table for one process have?

  1. 2³⁵
  2. 2³⁶
  3. 2¹³
  4. 2⁴⁸
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A single-level page table has one entry per virtual page, so the entry count is the size of the address space divided by the page size, i.e. 2(address bits − offset bits).

Step by step.

  1. 8 KB = 2³ × 2¹⁰ = 2¹³ bytes, so the offset field is 13 bits.
  2. The page number field is therefore 48 − 13 = 35 bits.
  3. That gives 2³⁵ distinct virtual pages, and hence 2³⁵ page-table entries.

Option (A)

Common misconception. Answering 2⁴⁸, the number of addressable bytes, or 2¹³, the number of bytes in a page. The table is indexed by page, not by byte.

OS-U4-S2-Q08NATeasy

A machine has 40-bit virtual addresses and 4 KB pages. How many bits does the page-number field of the virtual address contain?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The virtual address is split into a page number and an offset, and the offset must be able to name every byte inside one page.

Step by step.

  1. 4 KB = 2¹² bytes, so the offset needs 12 bits.
  2. The page number takes the remaining bits: 40 − 12 = 28.

Answer: 28

Common misconception. Reporting 12, which is the offset width, or 40, which is the whole address. The question asks for the page-number field only.

OS-U4-S2-Q09MCQeasy

A process has a virtual address space of 2 GB and the system uses 4 KB pages. Assuming the whole space is mapped, how many page-table entries does the process need?

  1. 2¹⁸ = 262144
  2. 2²¹ = 2097152
  3. 2³¹ = 2147483648
  4. 2¹⁹ = 524288
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. One page-table entry covers exactly one page, so the entry count is the address-space size divided by the page size.

Step by step.

  1. 2 GB = 2 × 2³⁰ = 2³¹ bytes.
  2. 4 KB = 2¹² bytes.
  3. Entries = 2³¹ ÷ 2¹² = 2¹⁹ = 524 288.

Option (D)

Common misconception. Dividing by 1024 twice instead of by 2¹² once, or answering the number of bytes in the address space. 2³¹ counts bytes, not pages.

OS-U4-S2-Q10NATmedium

A TLB access takes 5 ns and a main-memory access takes 100 ns. The system uses a single-level page table. The measured effective access time is 125 ns. What is the TLB hit ratio, in percent?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With one level of paging a TLB hit costs the TLB probe plus the data access; a TLB miss costs the probe, the page-table read and then the data access.

Step by step.

  1. Hit path: 5 + 100 = 105 ns.
  2. Miss path: 5 + 100 + 100 = 205 ns.
  3. EAT = 105h + 205(1 − h) = 205 − 100h.
  4. Setting 205 − 100h = 125 gives 100h = 80, so h = 0.80, that is 80%.

Answer: 80

Common misconception. Charging a miss only one memory access (105 ns on both paths), which forces an EAT of 105 ns and makes the given 125 ns impossible. A miss reads the page-table entry and the data.

OS-U4-S2-Q11MCQmedium

A 32-bit virtual address is split for two-level paging into a 10-bit outer index, a 10-bit inner index and a 12-bit offset. How many entries does each second-level page table hold?

  1. 1024
  2. 4096
  3. 256
  4. 2²⁰ = 1048576
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each level is indexed by its own field, so the number of entries in a level is 2 raised to that field's width.

Step by step.

  1. The outer index is 10 bits, so the outer page table has 2¹⁰ = 1024 entries, each pointing at one second-level table.
  2. The inner index is also 10 bits, so each second-level table has 2¹⁰ = 1024 entries.
  3. The 12-bit offset then selects a byte inside the 4 KB page. Check: 10 + 10 + 12 = 32 bits.

Option (A)

Common misconception. Answering 4096, the page size in bytes, or 2²⁰, the total number of pages. Each level is sized by its own index field, not by the address space.

OS-U4-S2-Q12MSQhard

Which of the following statements about page-table structures are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. An inverted page table grows with the size of the virtual address space.
  2. A multi-level page table can avoid allocating table pages for unused regions of the address space.
  3. With single-level paging the table is an array indexed by page number, so it must occupy contiguous physical memory.
  4. With 4 KB pages a 32-bit logical address splits into a 20-bit page number and a 12-bit offset.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The shape of a page table is dictated by what indexes it. A single-level table is indexed by page number; a multi-level table adds an index over table pages; an inverted table is indexed by frame.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is false: an inverted table has one entry per frame, so it grows with physical memory, not with the virtual address space.
  2. (B) is true: only the second-level tables that are actually referenced need to exist, so sparse address spaces cost little.
  3. (C) is true: entry i sits at a fixed offset from the base, which requires the array to be contiguous.
  4. (D) is true: 4 KB = 2¹², so the offset is 12 bits and 32 − 12 = 20 bits remain for the page number.

Options (B), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Believing multi-level paging saves memory always. It costs more memory for a fully used address space (extra levels to walk) and saves memory only when the space is sparse.

OS-U4-S2-Q13MCQmedium

A 32-bit address space is paged with 4 KB pages and a two-level scheme whose outer index is 10 bits. Each page-table entry is 4 bytes. How much memory does the outer page table occupy?

  1. 1 MB
  2. 4 KB
  3. 8 KB
  4. 2 MB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The outer page table has one entry per value of the outer index, and every entry is one page-table entry wide.

Step by step.

  1. Outer index = 10 bits, so the table has 2¹⁰ = 1024 entries.
  2. 1024 × 4 bytes = 4096 bytes = 4 KB.

Option (B)

Common misconception. Sizing the table from the whole 32-bit address space (2³² × 4 bytes) instead of from the outer index. Each level is sized only by its own field.

inverted-page-tables · 12
OS-U4-Q14MCQeasy

An inverted page table contains exactly one entry per:

  1. virtual page of every running process
  2. segment of every process
  3. process in the system
  4. physical frame of main memory
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The inverted page table is indexed by frame number, so it has one entry for each frame of physical memory, whatever the number of processes or the size of the virtual address space.

Step by step.

  1. Index = physical frame number.
  2. Entry = the (process id, virtual page number) currently held in that frame.
  3. Number of entries = number of frames = physical memory size ÷ page size.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Expecting one entry per virtual page. That is the ordinary per-process page table, whose size grows with the virtual address space.

OS-U4-Q15MCQeasy

The main advantage of an inverted page table is that:

  1. it removes the need for a TLB
  2. its size depends on the size of physical memory, not on the size of the virtual address space
  3. it makes page sharing between processes automatic
  4. it stores the replacement policy in the TLB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A per-process page table can be enormous when the virtual address space is sparse; an inverted table is one array with one slot per frame.

Step by step.

  1. With 2ⁿ frames the table has 2ⁿ entries, independent of how large each process's address space is.
  2. So the memory spent on translation is bounded by physical memory, not by the virtual size.

Answer: Option (B)

Common misconception. Believing the TLB becomes unnecessary. Translation on an inverted table is a search, so a TLB is more important there, not less.

OS-U4-Q16MCQmedium

The main cost of an inverted page table is that:

  1. each page entry must be duplicated for every process
  2. page faults can no longer be detected
  3. a TLB miss requires searching the table for the entry whose (process id, virtual page) pair matches
  4. every byte read needs one extra memory access
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The inverted table is keyed by frame, but translation needs the reverse direction: given (pid, page) find the frame. That is a search, usually over a hash table.

Step by step.

  1. A TLB hit is unaffected: the frame number comes straight from the TLB.
  2. On a miss the hardware must locate the entry that records this process and this virtual page.
  3. With linear search that costs time proportional to the number of frames; a hash table on (pid, page) reduces it to a few probes, at the cost of handling collisions.

Answer: Option (C)

Common misconception. Thinking the table stores the frame directly at the index of the virtual page. It cannot: the index is the frame, and the virtual page is stored inside the entry.

OS-U4-Q17MCQhard

A machine has 1 GB of physical memory, a page size of 4 KB and an inverted page table with 8-byte entries. What is the total size of the inverted page table?

  1. 2 MB
  2. 4 MB
  3. 8 MB
  4. 512 KB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Table size = (physical memory ÷ page size) × entry size, because there is one entry per frame.

Step by step.

  1. 1 GB ÷ 4 KB = 2³⁰ ÷ 2¹² = 2¹⁸ = 262 144 frames.
  2. 262 144 × 8 bytes = 2 097 152 bytes.
  3. 2 097 152 bytes = 2 MB.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Multiplying by the virtual address space, or forgetting that the entry count follows frames. A 64-bit virtual space would not change this answer at all.

OS-U4-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about inverted page tables are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. An inverted page table needs one entry per physical frame, so its size is fixed by the amount of physical memory.
  2. Sharing a frame between two processes is awkward, because a frame records only one (process, page) pair.
  3. A linear search of the table on a TLB miss costs time proportional to the number of frames in the worst case.
  4. An inverted page table removes the need for a TLB, because the table is small.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. One slot per frame saves memory but makes translation a search, and a frame belongs to exactly one recorded (process, page) pair.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: frames determine the entry count.
  2. (B) is true: a shared frame can only record one owner, so sharing needs extra machinery such as a separate alias record or a chain of sharers.
  3. (C) is true for a linear scan; hashing reduces the expected cost but not the worst case.
  4. (D) is false: the search cost makes a TLB essential to acceptable performance.

Answer: Options (A), (B), (C)

Common misconception. Assuming a small table means fast translation. Size and lookup cost are separate properties, and here the small table is the slow one.

OS-U4-Q19NATmedium

A machine has 64 MB of physical memory and pages of 4 KB. Its inverted page table has one 8-byte entry per frame. What is the size of the table, in KB?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Frames = physical memory ÷ page size; table size = frames × entry size.

Step by step.

  1. 64 MB = 2²⁶ bytes and 4 KB = 2¹² bytes, so there are 2¹⁴ = 16 384 frames.
  2. 16 384 × 8 = 131 072 bytes.
  3. 131 072 ÷ 1024 = 128 KB.

Answer: 128

Common misconception. Dividing by 1024 twice, or multiplying the number of pages of one process by the entry size instead of the number of frames.

OS-U4-S2-Q14MCQeasy

Physical memory is 4 GB and the page size is 8 KB. How many entries does an inverted page table have?

  1. 2¹³ = 8192
  2. 2¹⁹ = 524288
  3. 2³²
  4. 2²⁹
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An inverted page table is indexed by frame number, so it has exactly one entry per frame of physical memory, whatever the number of processes.

Step by step.

  1. 4 GB = 2³² bytes.
  2. 8 KB = 2¹³ bytes per frame.
  3. Frames = 2³² ÷ 2¹³ = 2¹⁹ = 524 288, so the table has 2¹⁹ entries.

Option (B)

Common misconception. Multiplying by the number of processes. That is the cost of ordinary per-process page tables; the inverted table is a single system-wide array sized by physical memory.

OS-U4-S2-Q15MCQeasy

An entry of an inverted page table records which of the following?

  1. the frame number that holds a given virtual page
  2. the virtual page number and the process identifier whose page currently occupies this frame
  3. the physical address of the next page-table page
  4. the segment base and limit of the process
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The table is indexed by frame, so the frame number is implicit in the position of the entry; what the entry must store is the owner of that frame.

Step by step.

  1. Index = frame number.
  2. Entry = (process id, virtual page number) of the page currently resident in that frame.
  3. Translation therefore runs backwards: given (pid, page) the hardware must find the frame.

Option (B)

Common misconception. Choosing (A), which describes an ordinary page table. An inverted table cannot store the frame at an index given by the virtual page number, because the index is the frame.

OS-U4-S2-Q16MCQmedium

A system uses one inverted page table keyed by frame number, plus a hash table on (pid, page). Which statement about a TLB miss is TRUE?

  1. The frame number follows directly from the virtual page number, so no search is needed.
  2. The entry must be located by a search, because the table is indexed by frame while translation needs the reverse direction.
  3. A TLB becomes unnecessary because the inverted table is small.
  4. Each process keeps its own inverted page table, so the lookup is per-process.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Translation asks "which frame holds (pid, page)?"; an inverted table answers "which (pid, page) is in this frame?". The direction is reversed, so a lookup cannot be an index.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is wrong: the virtual page number is not the index of an inverted table.
  2. (B) is right: the hash table on (pid, page) is what makes the reversed lookup fast, and without it the hardware would have to scan every entry.
  3. (C) is wrong: the table is small but slow to search, which makes a TLB more valuable, not less.
  4. (D) is wrong: the whole point is one shared table, one entry per frame.

Option (B)

Common misconception. Concluding that a small table means cheap translation. Size and lookup cost are independent: the inverted table is the small one and the slow one.

OS-U4-S2-Q17MSQmedium

An operating system replaces the per-process page tables of every running process with one inverted page table. Which statements about the resulting system are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The table has exactly one entry per physical frame.
  2. It removes the need for a TLB.
  3. Its size is independent of the number of processes and of the size of each virtual address space.
  4. Each process keeps its own inverted page table.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The inverted table is a single array with one slot per frame, so its size is fixed by physical memory alone.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true by construction: the index is the frame number.
  2. (B) is false: translation becomes a search, which is exactly why a TLB is needed.
  3. (C) is true: adding processes or enlarging virtual address spaces does not add frames.
  4. (D) is false: one table is shared by all processes, which is the source of both its small size and its sharing problem.

Options (A) and (C)

Common misconception. Assuming the table must grow with the address space, as ordinary page tables do. Its independence from the virtual address space is the whole reason it exists.

OS-U4-S2-Q18MCQhard

A system has 256 MB of physical memory, 4 KB pages and an inverted page table whose entries are 16 bytes each. How much memory does the table occupy?

  1. 1 MB
  2. 2 MB
  3. 512 KB
  4. 4 MB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Table size = number of frames × entry size, and the frame count is physical memory divided by page size.

Step by step.

  1. 256 MB = 2²⁸ bytes, and 4 KB = 2¹² bytes, so there are 2²⁸ ÷ 2¹² = 2¹⁶ = 65 536 frames.
  2. 65 536 × 16 bytes = 1 048 576 bytes = 1 MB.

Option (A)

Common misconception. Sizing the table from a 64-bit virtual address space, which would be astronomically larger. The inverted table knows nothing about virtual addresses.

OS-U4-S2-Q19MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about an inverted page table are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Its size depends on physical memory rather than on the virtual address space.
  2. One table is shared by all processes.
  3. Translation on a TLB miss requires a search or a hash lookup.
  4. It eliminates the page-table walk on a TLB miss.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three properties follow directly from the index being the frame number: the size, the sharing and the need to search.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: one entry per frame, so physical memory fixes the size.
  2. (B) is true: a single system-wide table describes every frame.
  3. (C) is true: given (pid, page) the hardware must find the matching entry, which a hash table makes fast but does not make free.
  4. (D) is false: it adds work on a miss, since the entry must be searched for rather than indexed.

Options (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Reading "small table" as "fast translation". The inverted table trades size for lookup cost.

segmentation-paged-segmentation · 12
OS-U4-Q20MCQeasy

In a segmented memory system, a logical address consists of:

  1. a single linear offset from the start of physical memory
  2. a page number and a frame number
  3. a frame number and an offset within that frame
  4. a segment number and an offset within that segment
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Segmentation is a variable-sized, logical division of the address space, so a reference names the segment and then a position inside it.

Step by step.

  1. The segment number indexes the segment table.
  2. The entry supplies the base and the limit of that segment.
  3. The offset is checked against the limit and then added to the base.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Confusing the segment number with a page number. Pages are fixed-size and invisible to the programmer; segments are variable-sized and usually correspond to code, data and stack.

OS-U4-Q21MCQmedium

A segment table entry holds base 8000 and limit 900. A program refers to offset 950 within that segment. What happens?

  1. a trap is raised, because the offset exceeds the segment limit
  2. the reference is translated to physical address 8950
  3. the reference is translated to physical address 8000
  4. the segment is extended automatically to 950 bytes
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The limit is the length of the segment. A valid offset satisfies 0 ≤ offset < limit; anything else is an addressing error caught by the hardware.

Step by step.

  1. The limit is 900, so valid offsets are 0 to 899.
  2. The offset 950 is outside that range, so the check fails before any address is formed.
  3. The hardware raises a trap; no physical address is produced.

Answer: Option (A)

Common misconception. Adding base and offset anyway to get 8950. Without the limit check, an out-of-range offset would silently read another segment's memory — the very protection segmentation exists to provide.

OS-U4-Q22MCQeasy

A pure segmentation system, with no paging underneath, suffers from:

  1. internal fragmentation only
  2. neither internal nor external fragmentation
  3. internal fragmentation in the last segment only
  4. external fragmentation
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Segments are variable-sized, so a segment is allocated as one contiguous run and no space inside it is wasted.

Step by step.

  1. Each segment gets exactly the space it needs, so there is no internal fragmentation.
  2. As segments of different sizes come and go, the free memory breaks into holes of assorted sizes.
  3. Those holes may be too small for a new segment even when their total is large: external fragmentation.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Thinking variable-sized units avoid fragmentation altogether. They avoid internal fragmentation and pay for it with external fragmentation, which is exactly why paged segmentation exists.

OS-U4-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about segmentation and paged segmentation are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Segmentation makes the limit check unnecessary, because the offset field is wide enough for any segment.
  2. Segmentation matches the programmer's view of a program as code, data and stack.
  3. Two processes can share a segment by pointing their segment-table entries at the same base.
  4. In paged segmentation a segment-table entry points to a page table, and the segment offset is split into a page number and a page offset.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Segmentation is a logical division with a base and a limit per segment; paged segmentation keeps that structure but pages each segment so that physical allocation is in fixed frames.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is false: the limit check is what enforces the segment boundary; a wide offset field would still allow an out-of-range value.
  2. (B) is true: segments correspond to the program's own modules, which is what makes protection and sharing natural.
  3. (C) is true: identical base and limit in two segment tables gives shared memory.
  4. (D) is true: the segment is paged, so its offset addresses a page of that segment and then a byte within the page.

Answer: Options (B), (C), (D)

Common misconception. Believing paging makes segmentation redundant. Paged segmentation keeps the logical segment structure and adds paging only to remove external fragmentation.

OS-U4-Q24NAThard

In a paged segmentation scheme a logical address is (8-bit segment number, 24-bit offset), pages are 4 KB, and each page-table entry is 4 bytes. What is the size, in KB, of the page table of one segment?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each segment has its own page table with one entry per page of that segment, so the table size follows the maximum number of pages a segment can contain.

Step by step.

  1. A 24-bit offset addresses 2²⁴ bytes within a segment.
  2. With 4 KB = 2¹² pages, a segment holds at most 2²⁴ ÷ 2¹² = 2¹² = 4096 pages.
  3. 4096 entries × 4 bytes = 16 384 bytes = 16 KB.

Answer: 16

Common misconception. Multiplying by the number of segments (256). The question asks for one segment's page table; the segment number only selects which table to use.

OS-U4-Q25NATeasy

A segment table entry holds base 3000 and limit 500. A reference to offset 400 within that segment is valid. What physical address does it produce?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. After the limit check succeeds, the physical address is the segment base plus the offset.

Step by step.

  1. The offset 400 is below the limit 500, so the reference is legal.
  2. Physical address = 3000 + 400 = 3400.

Answer: 3400

Common misconception. Adding the limit as well, or treating the base as a byte count to be divided. The base is the starting physical address of the segment.

OS-U4-S2-Q20MCQeasy

A segment table entry holds base 8000 and limit 1200. A reference to offset 500 of that segment produces which physical address?

  1. 9200
  2. 7500
  3. 8000
  4. 8500
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The limit is a bound on the offset, not part of the address; once the offset passes the bound check the physical address is simply base + offset.

Step by step.

  1. Check the bound: 500 < 1200, so the reference is legal.
  2. Physical address = 8000 + 500 = 8500.

Option (D)

Common misconception. Adding the limit as well (8000 + 1200 + 500 = 9700), or subtracting the offset. The limit only decides whether the reference is allowed.

OS-U4-S2-Q21MCQeasy

A segment table entry holds base 8000 and limit 1200. A reference to offset 1500 of that segment causes which outcome?

  1. physical address 9500
  2. physical address 8000
  3. an addressing trap, because the offset is not less than the limit
  4. the offset is reduced modulo the limit, giving physical address 8300
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The limit is the length of the segment. A legal offset satisfies 0 ≤ offset < limit; anything else is an addressing error detected by the hardware before any address is formed.

Step by step.

  1. Valid offsets for this segment are 0 to 1199.
  2. 1500 is outside that range, so the comparison fails.
  3. The hardware raises a trap (an addressing or segmentation fault) and no physical address is produced.

Option (C)

Common misconception. Computing 8000 + 1500 = 9500 anyway. Without the bound check the reference would silently read memory belonging to another segment — the protection that segmentation exists to provide.

OS-U4-S2-Q22MCQmedium

A process has this segment table: segment 0 base 219 limit 600; segment 1 base 2300 limit 14; segment 2 base 90 limit 100. Which logical address causes a trap?

  1. (0, 430)
  2. (1, 10)
  3. (1, 20)
  4. (2, 99)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each segment has its own bound. A reference is legal exactly when its offset is less than that segment's limit.

Step by step.

  1. (0, 430): 430 < 600, legal, physical 219 + 430 = 649.
  2. (1, 10): 10 < 14, legal, physical 2300 + 10 = 2310.
  3. (1, 20): 20 is not less than the limit 14, so this reference traps.
  4. (2, 99): 99 < 100, legal, physical 90 + 99 = 189.

Option (C)

Common misconception. Checking the offset against the wrong segment's limit, or against the base. Every segment carries its own limit, and segment 1 is tiny (14 bytes).

OS-U4-S2-Q23MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about segmentation and paging are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. Segmentation has no internal fragmentation, because a segment is exactly as large as the logical unit it holds.
  2. Segmentation can suffer external fragmentation as segments of different sizes come and go.
  3. The segment size is fixed by the hardware at 4 KB.
  4. Paging avoids external fragmentation because every frame has the same size.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Segmentation allocates variable-sized logical units; paging allocates fixed-size frames. That single difference decides which fragmentation each one suffers.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true: the block handed to a segment is exactly the segment length, so nothing inside it is wasted.
  2. (B) is true: freeing segments of assorted sizes leaves holes that may be too small for the next segment.
  3. (C) is false: segment sizes are set by the program's logical structure and the hardware allows them to vary; 4 KB is a page size.
  4. (D) is true: any free frame can hold any page, so no usable free space is left between allocations.

Options (A), (B) and (D)

Common misconception. Mixing up the two kinds of fragmentation: segmentation trades internal for external, paging trades external for internal.

OS-U4-S2-Q24NAThard

In a paged segmentation system the page size is 4 KB and each page-table entry is 8 bytes. One segment is 1 MB long and is mapped by a single-level page table. How many bytes does that segment's page table occupy?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In paged segmentation each segment has its own page table with one entry per page of that segment, so the table is sized by the segment length, not by the whole address space.

Step by step.

  1. 1 MB = 2²⁰ bytes, and 4 KB = 2¹² bytes, so the segment spans 2²⁰ ÷ 2¹² = 2⁸ = 256 pages.
  2. 256 entries × 8 bytes = 2048 bytes.

Answer: 2048

Common misconception. Sizing the table from the 32-bit address space or from the maximum segment size. Only the pages actually in this segment are described.

OS-U4-S2-Q25MCQhard

In a paged segmentation system each segment has a single-level page table and the segment table itself resides in main memory. On a TLB miss, how many main-memory accesses are needed to fetch one word of a segment, ignoring the TLB probe itself?

  1. 1 access
  2. 2 accesses
  3. 4 accesses
  4. 3 accesses
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Translation goes segment table → segment page table → data, and every one of those structures lives in memory.

Step by step.

  1. Access 1: read the segment table entry to obtain the base and limit of the segment and the origin of its page table.
  2. Access 2: read the page-table entry for the page that holds the wanted offset, obtaining the frame.
  3. Access 3: read the data word from that frame at the given offset.
  4. A TLB hit removes the first two accesses and leaves only the data read.

Option (D)

Common misconception. Counting 2 by analogy with plain paging. Plain paging walks one page table and then reads the data; paged segmentation walks the segment table as well.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 5 — Virtual Memory & Page Replacement
demand-paging-page-fault-rate · 15
OS-U5-Q01MCQeasy

Demand paging means that

['every page of a process is copied into memory before its first instruction executes.', 'a page is brought into memory only when it is referenced, and the faulting instruction is restarted once the page has arrived.', 'the operating system reads pages ahead of the reference stream and keeps a pool of spare frames ready.', 'page tables are kept on disk and are read again on every memory access.']
Show answer & solution

Concept. Demand paging loads a page lazily, at the moment it is first touched. The process starts with almost nothing resident; the hardware consults the valid–invalid bit of the page-table entry, and a reference to a page marked invalid raises a page fault that brings that one page in.

Step by step.

  1. The process is launched with its page table built but its frames empty apart from the first pages it needs.
  2. A reference whose valid bit is 1 proceeds normally: the frame number is used directly.
  3. A reference whose valid bit is 0 traps to the operating system, which finds a free frame, reads the page from backing store, updates the table and restarts the instruction.
  4. Only referenced pages are ever loaded, which is why the scheme is called demand paging.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) describes pure (non-demand) paging with the whole process preloaded.
