  • (D) The last block of a file is partly empty in every block-based scheme; only the amount differs.

Common misconception. Crediting contiguous allocation with the absence of fragmentation. It has no internal fragmentation in the paging sense, but external fragmentation is its signature defect.

Takeaway. Contiguous: fastest access, worst growth. Linked: any growth, no direct access.

OS-U6-Q09NATeasy

A file system uses indexed allocation in which each file has exactly one index block, and the index block holds 256 block pointers. The block size is 1 KB. What is the largest file, in KB, that this scheme can represent?
(Enter an integer.)

[]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With one index block, the file can point at exactly as many data blocks as the index block has entries. Maximum file size = entries × block size.

Step by step.

  1. The index block holds 256 pointers.
  2. Each pointer addresses one 1 KB data block.
  3. Maximum size = 256 × 1 KB = 256 KB.

Answer: 256 KB.

Common misconception. Adding the index block itself (257 KB) or subtracting it. The index block holds metadata, not file data, so it does not count towards the file's size.

Takeaway. Single-level indexing caps a file at (pointers per block) × (block size); to go beyond it, a second level is needed.

OS-U6-Q13MCQmedium

An inode holds 12 direct pointers, one single-indirect pointer and one double-indirect pointer. The block size is 4 KB and a block pointer is 4 bytes. Counting logical blocks from 0, which pointer chain is used to reach logical block 5000?

['A direct pointer', 'The single-indirect pointer', 'The double-indirect pointer', 'No chain — a file of this size cannot be represented']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Multi-level indexing partitions the logical block numbers into ranges: the direct pointers cover the first few blocks, the single-indirect pointer the next (block size ÷ pointer size) blocks, and the double-indirect pointer everything after that.

Step by step.

  1. Pointers per block = 4096 ÷ 4 = 1024.
  2. Direct pointers cover blocks 0 to 11 — that is 12 blocks.
  3. Single indirect covers 1024 blocks: 12 to 1035.
  4. Double indirect covers 1024² = 1,048,576 blocks: 1036 to 1,049,611.
  5. 5000 lies between 1036 and 1,049,611, so the double-indirect pointer is used: the first-level index gives the second-level index block, and that block gives the data block.

Answer: Option (C) — the double-indirect pointer, with two index blocks traversed.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) a direct pointer reaches only blocks 0 to 11.
  • (B) the single-indirect pointer stops at block 1035.
  • (D) the structure addresses over a million blocks, so 5000 is well within reach.

Common misconception. Forgetting that the 12 direct blocks shift every later range by 12, or computing 1024² = 1,048,576 as if the pointer size were the block size. Keep the three ranges explicit.

Takeaway. Ranges: direct 0–11, single 12–1035, double 1036–1,049,611 for 4 KB blocks and 4-byte pointers.

OS-U6-Q17MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about indexed allocation are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

['It supports direct access, because the index block gives the address of every data block of the file.', 'It suffers no external fragmentation, because each data block of a file is allocated on its own.', 'The index block costs space even for a very small file, and it must be read before any data block can be touched.', 'A file can be extended without limit in place, because its index block grows with the file.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Indexed allocation gives each file a block of pointers. One level of indirection buys direct access at the price of a fixed-size index.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. The k-th entry of the index block is the address of the k-th data block, so one index read plus one data read reaches any block.
  2. (B) True. Data blocks are taken individually from the free pool, so there are no unusable holes between files.
  3. (C) True. A file of one block still needs a whole index block, and every access begins with a read of the index. Internal fragmentation of the last data block also remains.
  4. (D) False. The index block has a fixed number of entries. Growth beyond it needs a second level of indexing, or an extension mechanism such as a linked chain of index blocks.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true; (D) is false.

Common misconception. Assuming indexed allocation removes every limit and every kind of waste. It removes external fragmentation and the growth restriction of contiguous allocation, but the index block is a fixed-size ceiling and a per-file space cost.

Takeaway. Indexed: direct access and no external fragmentation, at the cost of an index block and a one-block ceiling per level.

OS-U6-Q21MCQmedium

A FAT-based file system manages 2²⁰ blocks. What is the minimum number of bits that each FAT entry must have?

['16', '20', '24', '32']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The file allocation table has one entry per disk block, and the entry holds the number of the next block in the chain. An entry must therefore be wide enough to name any block, and — in practice — to hold a few reserved markers.

Step by step.

  1. Block numbers run from 0 to 2²⁰ − 1, so 2²⁰ distinct values must be representable.
  2. A field of b bits holds 2b values, so 2b ≥ 2²⁰ gives b ≥ 20.
  3. 16 bits would address only 2¹⁶ = 65,536 blocks, far fewer than 2²⁰.
  4. 24 or 32 bits would work but are not the minimum.

Answer: Option (B) — 20 bits is the minimum; real systems use 16, 28 or 32 bits for convenience.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 2¹⁶ blocks is 65,536, which cannot name block 1,048,575.
  • (C) and (D) are sufficient but larger than needed.

Common misconception. Dividing the disk size by the block size and forgetting to take the base-2 logarithm — the entry width depends on the number of blocks, not on their size.

Takeaway. Entry width ≥ log₂(number of blocks). The whole FAT then occupies (blocks × width ÷ 8) bytes, which is why large FAT disks keep it partly on disk.

OS-U6-Q24MSQhard

Which of the following statements comparing file-allocation methods are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

['Contiguous allocation gives the best sequential and direct access, but a file cannot grow unless the blocks immediately after it are free, so growth often means copying the file.', 'Linked allocation never needs to move a file in order to grow it, since growth is just linking another block, and the price is the loss of direct access.', 'Multi-level indexed allocation removes internal fragmentation, because every block of the file is addressed individually.', 'A file stored with multi-level indexing cannot be sparse, because every block must have a pointer in the index.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The three allocation methods sit on a single trade-off: how cheaply a block can be found against how freely the file can grow. Fragmentation and sparse files are separate properties that follow from how blocks are assigned.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. Contiguity is what makes start + k work; it is also what makes growth expensive, and it leaves holes behind when files are deleted.
  2. (B) True. A new block can be linked at the tail wherever free space happens to be, so no file ever has to move. Reading block k then costs k pointer chases.
  3. (C) False. Internal fragmentation is the unused tail of the last allocated block, and it is present in every block-based scheme. Multi-level indexing removes external fragmentation, not internal.
  4. (D) False. A sparse file is one whose index entries are absent or marked invalid for blocks never written; reads of those blocks return zeros. Indexing is exactly the structure that makes sparse files practical, because no data block need exist for a hole.

Answer: Options (A) and (B) are true; (C) and (D) are false.

Common misconception. Believing that per-block allocation eliminates all waste. It eliminates the waste between files and keeps the waste inside the last block.

Takeaway. Contiguous: best access, worst growth. Linked: best growth, worst access. Indexed: both good, at the cost of index blocks — and it is the scheme that makes sparse files cheap.

OS-U6-Q26MCQeasy

A FAT file system keeps one entry per disk block in the FAT, and the directory entry of a file stores only its first block number. Which one of the following follows directly from that design?

  1. Caching the FAT in main memory turns every step of the block chain into a table lookup, so the chain is followed at memory speed rather than at one disk read per block.
  2. The directory entry has to be rewritten every time the file grows, because the FAT cannot record where the new block went.
  3. The FAT stores the same block numbers as the directory entries, so the two structures can disagree about where a file starts.
  4. A FAT entry is needed only for blocks that a file actually uses, so the FAT grows with the number of files rather than with the size of the disk.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The FAT is an array indexed by block number; entry i holds the number of the block that follows block i in the file that owns it. The directory entry supplies only the head of the chain, so growth appends a FAT entry and never moves the directory entry.

Step by step.

  1. A file is a chain: directory entry → first block → FAT[first] → FAT[FAT[first]] → … → end-of-file marker.
  2. Following that chain without caching costs one disk read of the FAT per link, which is why plain linked allocation is slow for random access.
  3. The FAT is small enough to be held in memory (one entry per block), so the same traversal becomes a sequence of array accesses.
  4. That is the whole point of FAT: it converts a per-block disk read into a memory lookup, at the cost of keeping the table resident and writing it back on change.

Answer: Option (A) — the FAT converts chain traversal into an in-memory table lookup.

Common misconception. Option (C) is the seductive one: students assume two structures holding block numbers must duplicate each other. The directory entry stores the first block only, so there is nothing to disagree about.

Takeaway. FAT = linked allocation with the links lifted out of the data blocks into one central table.

OS-U6-Q30MCQeasy

A file is stored by linked allocation in 5 disk blocks. The block size is 512 bytes, and 4 bytes of every block are used to hold the pointer to the next block. What is the largest amount of file data those 5 blocks can hold?

  1. 2560 bytes
  2. 2556 bytes
  3. 2540 bytes
  4. 2048 bytes
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In linked allocation each block spends part of its payload on the link to the next block. Only the remainder is file data, and the last block still carries a pointer (an end-of-file marker) unless the design uses a separate terminator.

Step by step.

  1. Payload per block = block size − pointer size = 512 − 4 = 508 bytes.
  2. Five blocks therefore hold 5 × 508 = 2540 bytes of data.
  3. The pointer overhead across the file is 5 × 4 = 20 bytes, so 2560 − 20 = 2540. ✔
  4. 2540 bytes is the answer; 2560 would be the answer only if the pointers lived outside the blocks.

Answer: Option (C) — 2540 bytes.

Common misconception. Answering 2560 forgets the per-block pointer entirely; answering 2556 subtracts a single pointer instead of one per block.

Takeaway. Linked allocation costs one pointer per block, so usable capacity is (block size − pointer size) × blocks.

OS-U6-Q33MCQmedium

A FAT file system uses 12-bit FAT entries and a cluster (allocation unit) size of 4 KB. What is the largest volume this design can address?

  1. 4 MB
  2. 16 MB
  3. 64 MB
  4. 2 GB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A FAT entry numbers the next cluster, so an n-bit FAT entry can name at most 2n distinct clusters. Volume size is that count multiplied by the cluster size.

Step by step.

  1. 12-bit entries give 212 = 4096 addressable clusters.
  2. Cluster size = 4 KB.
  3. Maximum volume = 4096 × 4 KB = 16 384 KB = 16 MB.
  4. The 4 MB option corresponds to 1024 clusters, i.e. 10-bit entries; 64 MB needs 14 bits.

Answer: Option (B) — 16 MB.

Common misconception. The frequent mistake is to multiply the entry width (12 bits = 1.5 bytes) by the cluster size, or to use 212 bytes instead of 212 clusters.

Takeaway. Volume limit = 2entry bits × cluster size.

OS-U6-Q36NATmedium

An inode holds 12 direct block pointers and one single-indirect pointer. The block size is 2 KB and a block pointer is 4 bytes. What is the largest file, in KB, that this inode can describe?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Direct pointers address data blocks one for one; a single-indirect pointer addresses one index block, and every pointer inside that index block addresses one further data block.

Step by step.

  1. Pointers per index block = block size / pointer size = 2048 / 4 = 512.
  2. Blocks reachable = 12 direct + 512 through the single-indirect block = 524 data blocks.
  3. Each data block holds 2 KB of file data.
  4. Maximum file size = 524 × 2 KB = 1048 KB.
  5. Note that the index block itself is metadata, not file data, so it is not counted.

Answer: 1048 KB.

Common misconception. Two errors recur: counting the index block as 513 addressable data blocks, and using 256 pointers because the solver assumes 1 KB blocks.

Takeaway. Capacity = (direct + pointers per indirect block) × block size, for one level of indirection.

OS-U6-Q40MCQmedium

A file of 8 blocks is stored by contiguous allocation. It must grow by one block, but the block immediately after the file already belongs to another file. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. The file system can simply link the new block on, because a contiguous file is always followed by free space.
  2. The new block can be placed anywhere, because the k-th block is still found at start + k.
  3. Only the file's index block has to be updated, since the data blocks never move.
  4. The file must be relocated to a hole large enough to hold 9 blocks, copying all 8 existing blocks, unless the system reserved room for growth in advance.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Contiguous allocation buys one-read direct access by promising that block k of a file lives at start + k. That promise is exactly what makes growth expensive: the file cannot be spliced.

Step by step.

  1. The address of block k is computed as start + k, so the blocks must be physically adjacent.
  2. If the neighbour is taken, there is no free slot at start + 8; the file cannot be extended in place.
  3. The system must find a hole of at least 9 blocks, copy the 8 existing blocks, and free the old extent.
  4. This copying cost, and the external fragmentation it leaves behind, is why systems use extents or reserve slack space when a file is created.

Answer: Option (D) — the file is relocated, or growth must have been reserved in advance.

Common misconception. Option (C) is the standard confusion: “start + k” only works while the blocks stay adjacent. Placing the ninth block elsewhere destroys the addressing rule that is the whole point of contiguous allocation.

Takeaway. Contiguous allocation trades growth cost for address arithmetic.

OS-U6-Q43MCQhard

An inode holds 12 direct pointers, one single-indirect pointer and one double-indirect pointer. The block size is 4 KB and a block pointer is 4 bytes. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. The double-indirect pointer alone can address 1024 × 1024 = 1 048 576 data blocks, that is, 4 GB of file data.
  2. A file of 8 MB is described entirely by the direct pointers and the single-indirect pointer.
  3. The single-indirect block addresses 1024 data blocks, so the first 1024 blocks of every file are reached through it.
  4. Every file needs at least one indirect block, because the inode can hold only 12 direct pointers.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Pointers per block = block size / pointer size = 4096 / 4 = 1024. One level of indirection addresses 1024 data blocks; two levels address 1024 × 1024.

Step by step.

  1. Direct range: logical blocks 0–11, that is 48 KB.
  2. Single-indirect range: 1024 blocks, that is 4 MB, covering logical blocks 12–1035.
  3. Double-indirect range: 1024 × 1024 = 1 048 576 blocks, that is 4 GB, covering logical blocks 1036 onwards.
  4. Check (B): 8 MB = 2048 blocks, and direct plus single-indirect reaches only 12 + 1024 = 1036 blocks, so a double-indirect block is required. False.
  5. Check (C): the first 12 blocks are direct; the single-indirect block starts at block 12. False.
  6. Check (D): a 1 KB file uses one direct pointer and no indirect block at all. False.

Answer: Option (A) — the double-indirect pointer addresses 1 048 576 blocks, i.e. 4 GB.

Common misconception. The common error is to multiply 1024 by the pointer size instead of the block size, or to assume the single-indirect block also serves the first 12 blocks.

Takeaway. One indirection multiplies capacity by 1024; each further level multiplies again.

OS-U6-Q47MSQmedium

A FAT file system uses 4-byte FAT entries and manages 232 blocks. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The FAT occupies 232 × 4 bytes = 16 GB, so it is read on demand or partially cached rather than held whole in memory on an ordinary machine.
  2. The directory entry of a file needs only its first block number and its length, because the rest of the chain lives in the FAT.
  3. Because each FAT entry is 4 bytes wide, no single file can be larger than 4 GB.
  4. A FAT-based file system can reach the k-th block of a file with a single disk read, just as contiguous allocation can.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The FAT is one table with an entry per block, so its size scales with the disk, not with the files. Traversal is a chain walk through that table.

Step by step.

  1. Entries = 232, each 4 bytes, so the table is 234 bytes = 16 GB. That is far beyond a resident table, so it is cached in pieces. (A) is true.
  2. The directory entry stores the first block and the file length; the successor of every block is in the FAT. (B) is true.
  3. (C) confuses the width of one link with the length of the chain. A 4-byte entry can name any of 232 blocks; the file size limit comes from the number of entries available, not from the entry width. False.
  4. (D) is false: reaching block k means following k links, so it costs k table lookups — cheap in memory, but not one disk read in the way contiguous addressing is.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Option (C) is the popular error: “4-byte entry means 4 GB file”. The entry width bounds the number of blocks addressable, not the size of one file.

Takeaway. FAT size scales with disk capacity; access cost scales with the position of the block in the chain.

free-space-management · 12
OS-U6-Q02MCQeasy

A disk has 2²⁴ blocks, and free space is tracked by a bitmap with one bit per block. What is the size of the bitmap?

['256 KB', '1 MB', '2 MB', '16 MB']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A bitmap stores one bit per block, so its size in bits equals the number of blocks; converting to bytes divides by 8.

Step by step.

  1. Bits needed = 2²⁴ = 16,777,216 bits.
  2. Bytes = 16,777,216 ÷ 8 = 2,097,152 bytes.
  3. Megabytes = 2,097,152 ÷ 2²⁰ = 2 MB.
  4. Shortcut: 2²⁴ bits = 2²⁴ ÷ 2³ bytes = 2²¹ bytes = 2 × 2²⁰ bytes = 2 MB.

Answer: Option (C) — 2 MB.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 256 KB is what you get by dividing by 8 twice, i.e. treating the answer as 2²⁴ ÷ 64.
  • (B) 1 MB forgets that one byte holds 8 bits.
  • (D) 16 MB is 2²⁴ bytes, i.e. one byte per block.

Common misconception. Losing the factor of 8 in one direction or the other. Write the unit chain explicitly: bits → bytes (÷8) → KB (÷2¹⁰) → MB (÷2²⁰).

Takeaway. Bitmap size in bytes = number of blocks ÷ 8; here 2²⁴ ÷ 8 = 2 MB.

OS-U6-Q06NATeasy

A disk has 1,000,000 blocks and its free-space bitmap uses one bit per block. How many bytes does the bitmap occupy?
(Enter an integer.)

[]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. One bit per block, so the number of bits equals the number of blocks; dividing by 8 gives bytes.

Step by step.

  1. Bits required = 1,000,000.
  2. Bytes = 1,000,000 ÷ 8.
  3. 1,000,000 ÷ 8 = 125,000 exactly, because 1,000,000 is divisible by 8.

Answer: 125,000 bytes (about 122 KB).

Common misconception. Reporting 1,000,000 bytes by keeping one byte per block, or rounding to 125,001 to “allow a spare bit”. No spare bit is needed: 1,000,000 is an exact multiple of 8.

Takeaway. Bitmap bytes = blocks ÷ 8; only round up when the block count is not a multiple of 8.

OS-U6-Q10MCQmedium

In the counting method of free-space management, each free block stores a count together with a pointer to the next free block. What does the count record?

['The number of files that currently share the block.', 'The number of consecutive free blocks that follow this one, so a run of k free blocks is described by a single entry.', 'The number of bits in the bitmap that are still unused.', 'The number of times the block has been written since it was formatted.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The counting method compresses the free list: instead of one entry per free block, one entry describes a whole run of contiguous free blocks.

Step by step.

  1. A free block stores (count, next), where next is the address of the next entry and count is how many contiguous free blocks start at this address.
  2. A disk with large free runs is then represented by a short list, so the list fits in memory more easily.
  3. Allocation takes the first entry with a large enough count and reduces that count.
  4. The count is therefore a length of a run, not a usage statistic.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) sharing is not tracked in the free-space structure at all.
  • (C) the counting method does not use a bitmap.
  • (D) a write counter has no role in allocation.

Common misconception. Confusing counting with grouping. Grouping stores many free-block addresses in one free block; counting stores one address and a run length.

Takeaway. Counting = run-length encoding of the free list.

OS-U6-Q14MCQeasy

Which free-space method makes it cheapest to decide whether the disk holds a run of 8 consecutive free blocks?

['A linked free list', 'The counting method', 'A bitmap', 'A grouping list']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four free-space methods differ in what one entry describes: one block (linked list, bitmap), one run (counting), or many addresses at once (grouping). A question about consecutive blocks is a question about the bitmap's shape.

Step by step.

  1. A bitmap stores the state of block i in bit i, so consecutive blocks occupy consecutive bits. A run of 8 free blocks is simply 8 consecutive zero bits — a word-sized test on a machine whose word is 8 bits or wider.
  2. A linked free list holds only the addresses of free blocks in no particular order, so a run must be detected by sorting or by repeated lookups.
  3. The counting method records runs, but a run of 8 may be split across several entries and its neighbours still have to be checked.
  4. A grouping list is designed for bulk collection of free blocks, not for testing adjacency.

Answer: Option (C) — the bitmap.

Common misconception. Choosing the counting method because it is “about runs”. Counting summarises runs that already exist; the bitmap is what lets you test adjacency directly, which is what the question asks.

Takeaway. Bitmap = positional, so adjacency is free; list methods = address only, so adjacency is not represented.

OS-U6-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about free-space management are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

['If the bitmap is held in memory, finding a free block needs no disk access, but the in-memory copy must eventually be written back to disk.', 'A linked free list needs one pointer per free block and is easy to keep consistent on disk, but finding a run of consecutive free blocks means walking the list.', 'Grouping stores the addresses of several free blocks inside the first free block, which reduces the number of disk reads needed to collect a large number of free blocks.', 'The counting method represents a run of k free blocks with k separate entries.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four methods are alternative encodings of the same information — which blocks are free. They differ in memory cost, disk cost and the operations they make cheap.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. A memory-resident bitmap answers the question with a bit scan, but it is a cache of on-disk state and must be flushed, which is why a crash can leave it inconsistent unless it is rebuilt at mount time.
  2. (B) True. Each free block stores the address of the next, so consistency needs only one pointer update per allocation; adjacency is not represented, so runs require a walk.
  3. (C) True. Grouping packs n − 1 free-block addresses plus a pointer to the next group into one block, so a single read yields a whole group of addresses.
  4. (D) False. That is the opposite of the counting method, which represents a run of k blocks with one entry.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true; (D) is false.

Common misconception. Mixing up counting and grouping. Counting stores (address, run length); grouping stores many addresses in one block.

Takeaway. Bitmap: cheap test, memory cost. Linked list: cheap update, no adjacency. Counting: run-length. Grouping: bulk collection.

OS-U6-Q22NAThard

A 4 TB disk is divided into 4 KB blocks, and free space is tracked by a bitmap with one bit per block. What is the size of the bitmap, in MB? (Use 1 TB = 2⁴⁰ bytes and 1 MB = 2²⁰ bytes; enter an integer.)

[]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Bitmap size = number of blocks ÷ 8 bytes. The number of blocks is the disk size divided by the block size, so both quantities should be kept as powers of two.

Step by step.

  1. Disk = 4 TB = 4 × 2⁴⁰ = 2⁴² bytes.
  2. Block = 4 KB = 2¹² bytes.
  3. Blocks = 2⁴² ÷ 2¹² = 2³⁰ = 1,073,741,824.
  4. Bits = 2³⁰, so bytes = 2³⁰ ÷ 2³ = 2²⁷.
  5. MB = 2²⁷ ÷ 2²⁰ = 2⁷ = 128.

Answer: 128 MB.

Common misconception. Losing one power of two by treating 1 KB as 1000 bytes and 1 MB as 10⁶ bytes, which gives about 119 MB, or by forgetting the ÷8 and answering 1024 MB.

Takeaway. Bitmap MB = (disk in MB) ÷ (block in bytes) ÷ 8. Here 4,194,304 MB ÷ 4096 ÷ 8 = 128 MB.

OS-U6-Q27NATeasy

A file system tracks free space with a bitmap holding one bit per disk block. The bitmap is kept entirely in main memory and occupies 64 KB. How many blocks does the disk have?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. One bit per block means the number of blocks equals the number of bits in the bitmap. Convert bytes to bits with a factor of 8.

Step by step.

  1. Bitmap size = 64 KB = 64 × 1024 = 65 536 bytes.
  2. Each byte carries 8 bits, so the bitmap holds 65 536 × 8 = 524 288 bits.
  3. One bit per block, therefore 524 288 blocks.
  4. Check the other way: 524 288 blocks need 524 288 bits = 65 536 bytes = 64 KB. ✔

Answer: 524288 blocks (a 64 KB bitmap covers 219 blocks).

Common misconception. The usual slip is to answer 65 536 by forgetting the factor of 8, or to use 1000 instead of 1024 for KB.

Takeaway. Bitmap bits = blocks; bytes = blocks / 8; so blocks = 8 × bytes.

OS-U6-Q34MCQeasy

Why is a bitmap usually preferred to a linked free list for tracking free space on a large disk?

  1. A bitmap needs fewer bits per block than a free list needs bytes per block, so it is always the smaller structure on disk.
  2. A bitmap never has to be written back to disk, because it can always be recomputed from the directory structure.
  3. A bitmap is the only structure that allows a file to occupy non-contiguous blocks.
  4. A bitmap can be held in main memory, so a free block is found without any disk I/O, whereas walking a free list costs a disk read for every block considered.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The dominant cost in free-space management is not the size of the structure but how many disk reads it takes to use it. A bitmap is dense and fixed-size, so it fits in memory and supports scanning.

Step by step.

  1. A bitmap uses 1 bit per block; a linked free list uses at least one pointer per free block, so on disk the list is larger, not smaller. Option (A) is therefore backwards.
  2. Because the bitmap is a dense array, it is normally cached in main memory and the first free block is found by scanning words — no disk access at all.
  3. A free list is scattered through the free blocks themselves, so each step of the walk is a disk read.
  4. Options (B) and (C) are simply false: the bitmap is persistent state that must be written back, and every allocation method supports non-contiguous blocks.

Answer: Option (D) — the bitmap is compact and dense, so it lives in memory and costs no I/O to search.

Common misconception. Students often pick the option that says the bitmap is smaller on disk. It is smaller, but the decisive advantage is that it is searchable in memory.

Takeaway. Bitmaps win on search cost, not on disk footprint.

OS-U6-Q38MSQmedium

A file system keeps its free-space bitmap in main memory and writes it to disk lazily, only when the buffer is flushed. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A crash before the bitmap reaches disk can leave blocks marked free that are in fact in use, so a consistency check must rebuild the free list at boot.
  2. Because the bitmap is in memory, a free block is located by scanning words in memory rather than by reading blocks from disk.
  3. A bitmap needs no consistency check at all, because it is a derived structure that the file system can always recompute from the allocation chains.
  4. If the bitmap reaches disk but the corresponding directory update does not, blocks can be marked in use while no file refers to them, which leaks space until the check repairs it.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Caching a metadata structure in memory decouples its on-disk copy from the real allocation state. Every crash between the two writes leaves the pair inconsistent in one of two directions.

Step by step.

  1. Bitmap written, allocation not: blocks are marked used but unreachable — a space leak. So (D) is true.
  2. Allocation written, bitmap not: blocks are marked free while a file points at them, so a later allocation can hand out a live block and corrupt two files. So (A) is true.
  3. The in-memory copy is what makes allocation cheap: the search is a memory scan. So (B) is true.
  4. Option (C) is false in the sense that matters: the bitmap is persistent state, not a view. Rebuilding it is exactly what a consistency check (fsck) does precisely because it can disagree with the chains.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. The tempting answer is (C): “the bitmap is derived, so it can always be rebuilt”. It can be rebuilt — but only by a check that walks every inode, which is why the inconsistency is a real hazard, not a non-issue.

Takeaway. Lazy metadata caching always buys speed with a consistency window; fsck exists to close it.

OS-U6-Q42MCQeasy

Free space on a disk consists of a few very long contiguous runs. Which free-space structure represents this state most compactly?

  1. A bitmap, because one bit per block is the smallest possible per-block record.
  2. A linked free list, because it needs no space outside the free blocks themselves.
  3. The counting method, because one entry describes a whole run of consecutive free blocks.
  4. A grouping list, because it stores several free block addresses in one block.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The counting method is run-length encoding for free space: each entry stores a first free block and the length of the run that starts there. Its cost is proportional to the number of runs, not to the number of free blocks.

Step by step.

  1. A bitmap always costs one bit per block on the disk, however the free blocks are arranged, so it does not exploit long runs.
  2. A linked free list stores one pointer per free block, so a run of a million blocks costs a million pointers — worse than the bitmap.
  3. Counting stores one (address, count) pair per run, so a few long runs collapse to a few entries.
  4. Grouping reduces I/O when allocating many blocks, but it still records addresses individually, so it does not compress a long run.

Answer: Option (C) — the counting method, which is run-length encoding.

Common misconception. The trap is to reason about worst case rather than this case. Counting is excellent for long runs and terrible for a disk of scattered single free blocks, where every run has length one.

Takeaway. Choose the free-space structure by the shape of the free space: runs favour counting, scatter favours a bitmap.

OS-U6-Q45MCQeasy

A disk has 231 blocks and free space is tracked by a bitmap with one bit per block. What is the size of the bitmap?

  1. 128 MB
  2. 256 MB
  3. 512 MB
  4. 2 GB
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The bitmap holds one bit per block, so its size in bits equals the block count; convert bits to bytes by dividing by 8, then bytes to MB by dividing by 220.

Step by step.

  1. Bits = 231.
  2. Bytes = 231 / 8 = 228 bytes.
  3. MB = 228 / 220 = 28 = 256 MB.
  4. Sanity check: 231 blocks at 8 blocks per byte is 228 bytes, and 228 bytes is a quarter of a gigabyte. ✔

Answer: Option (B) — 256 MB.

Common misconception. The standard slip is to forget the factor of 8 and answer 2 GB, or to mix powers of 2 with powers of 10.

Takeaway. Bitmap bytes = blocks / 8; keep everything in powers of two.

OS-U6-Q49MCQmedium

In the grouping method of free-space management, a free block stores as many free block addresses as will fit, together with the address of the block holding the next group. With a 4 KB block and 4-byte addresses, how many free blocks can be described by two chained group blocks?

  1. 1024
  2. 1023
  3. 2046
  4. 2047
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. One block of size B with addresses of size P holds B/P slots. One of those slots is spent pointing at the next group block, so B/P − 1 free blocks are described per block.

Step by step.

  1. Slots per block = 4096 / 4 = 1024.
  2. One slot holds the pointer to the next group, leaving 1023 free block addresses per group block.
  3. Two chained blocks describe 1023 + 1023 = 2046 free blocks.
  4. The third block would describe 3069, and so on — the chain is what makes grouping scale.

Answer: Option (C) — 2046 free blocks.

Common misconception. The frequent error is 2048 (1024 slots × 2), which forgets that each group block spends one slot on the link to the next. 2047 comes from deducting the pointer only once.

Takeaway. Grouping: B/P − 1 addresses per block; n chained blocks describe n(B/P − 1) free blocks.

disk-scheduling-fcfs-sstf-scan-cscan-look · 14
OS-U6-Q03MCQeasy

Which disk-scheduling policy services requests strictly in the order of their arrival, so that the head may sweep back and forth across the disk?

['SSTF', 'FCFS', 'SCAN', 'C-SCAN']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A disk-scheduling policy decides the order in which pending requests are served. Only one of the standard policies ignores the current head position altogether: FCFS, which serves the queue as it stands.

Step by step.

  1. FCFS takes the requests in arrival order, so the head can jump from cylinder 98 to 183 and back to 37 — sweeping in both directions as the queue dictates.
  2. SSTF always takes the nearest request, so the head moves monotonically toward a locality and does not sweep the whole disk.
  3. SCAN and C-SCAN impose a direction on the sweep by construction.
  4. Hence the only policy whose order is fixed by arrival time alone is FCFS.

Answer: Option (B) — FCFS.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) SSTF reorders the queue by distance from the head on every step.
  • (C) SCAN sweeps to the end of the disk in one direction before reversing.
  • (D) C-SCAN sweeps in one direction only and returns to the start without servicing.

Common misconception. Assuming FCFS is “fair but useless”. It is the baseline: it can be very expensive in head movement, but its behaviour is easy to predict and it never reorders requests.

Takeaway. FCFS = arrival order; SSTF = nearest first; SCAN/C-SCAN = sweep with a direction.

OS-U6-Q07NATeasy

A disk has 200 cylinders numbered 0 to 199. The head is at cylinder 53, and the pending queue in arrival order is 98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67. Under FCFS, what is the total head movement, in cylinders?
(Enter an integer.)

[]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Head movement is the sum of the absolute differences between consecutive cylinders visited, starting from the current head position. Under FCFS the visit order is the arrival order.

Step by step.

  1. 53 → 98 = 45
  2. 98 → 183 = 85
  3. 183 → 37 = 146
  4. 37 → 122 = 85
  5. 122 → 14 = 108
  6. 14 → 124 = 110
  7. 124 → 65 = 59
  8. 65 → 67 = 2
  9. Total = 45 + 85 + 146 + 85 + 108 + 110 + 59 + 2 = 640.

Answer: 640 cylinders.

Common misconception. Summing the request positions (710) instead of the gaps between consecutive positions. Movement is about travel, not about where the requests are.

Takeaway. Under FCFS the head zig-zags across the queue; the 146-cylinder jump from 183 back to 37 is most of the waste.

OS-U6-Q11MCQmedium

The head is at cylinder 53 on a 200-cylinder disk (0 to 199), and the pending queue is 98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67. Which one of the following is the order in which SSTF services these requests?

['53, 65, 67, 37, 14, 98, 122, 124, 183', '53, 37, 14, 65, 67, 98, 122, 124, 183', '53, 98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67', '53, 65, 67, 98, 122, 124, 183, 14, 37']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SSTF always serves the pending request nearest to the current head position, recomputing the nearest after every service. It is a greedy policy, not a sweep.

Step by step. From 53 the distances are 45 (98), 130 (183), 16 (37), 69 (122), 39 (14), 71 (124), 12 (65), 14 (67).

  1. 53 → 65 (12 cylinders). Nearest remaining to 65 is 67.
  2. 65 → 67 (2). From 67 the nearest is 37 (30), then 14 (23).
  3. 67 → 37 → 14. From 14 the nearest is 98 (84).
  4. 14 → 98 → 122 → 124 → 183, each step taking the closest of what remains.
  5. Total movement = 12 + 2 + 30 + 23 + 84 + 24 + 2 + 59 = 236.

Answer: Option (A) — 53, 65, 67, 37, 14, 98, 122, 124, 183, total 236 cylinders.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is LOOK moving toward cylinder 0, which goes to 14 before turning; total 208.
  • (C) is the FCFS arrival order; total 640.
  • (D) is an outward sweep from 53 that ignores 37 and 14 until the end, which SSTF would never do.

Common misconception. Sweeping outward from the head and calling it SSTF. SSTF reverses direction whenever the other side becomes nearer — here it turns at 67 and heads down to 14.

Takeaway. SSTF: at each step, take the nearest; the path may zig-zag.

OS-U6-Q15NATmedium

The head is at cylinder 53 on a 200-cylinder disk (0 to 199) and is moving toward cylinder 0. The pending queue is 98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67. Under SCAN, and counting the travel all the way to cylinder 0 and all the way to cylinder 199, what is the total head movement in cylinders?
(Enter an integer.)

[]
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SCAN, the elevator algorithm, serves every request in the current direction, travels to the end of the disk, reverses, and then serves what is left. The end-of-disk travel is part of the movement.

Step by step.

  1. Moving toward 0 from 53, the requests below the head are 37 and 14, taken in decreasing order: 53 → 37 (16) → 14 (23).
  2. SCAN continues to the end of the disk: 14 → 0 (14).
  3. It reverses and serves the rest in increasing order: 0 → 65 (65) → 67 (2) → 98 (31) → 122 (24) → 124 (2) → 183 (59).
  4. Total = 16 + 23 + 14 + 65 + 2 + 31 + 24 + 2 + 59 = 236.

Answer: 236 cylinders.

Common misconception. Answering 208 by stopping at 14 and calling that SCAN. Reversing at the last request is LOOK, not SCAN; the 28 cylinders of travel to 0 and back to 14 are exactly the difference between 236 and 208.

Takeaway. SCAN pays for the full sweep to the disk edge; LOOK does not. Read the question for which one is named.

OS-U6-Q19MCQmedium

Which one of the following statements about C-SCAN is TRUE?

['It serves requests in one direction only, and when the head reaches the last cylinder it returns to the first cylinder without serving requests on the way.', 'It reverses direction at the last pending request instead of at the end of the disk.', 'It gives a longer maximum wait than SCAN to a request that lies just behind the head.', 'It is identical to LOOK except that the request queue is sorted first.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. C-SCAN, the circular SCAN, treats the disk as a ring: it sweeps in one direction only, and on reaching the end it jumps back to the start without servicing anything on the return.

Step by step.

  1. Requests ahead of the head are served in increasing cylinder order.
  2. At the last cylinder the head returns to cylinder 0. The return is travel, not service, so requests passed on the way wait.
  3. Serving then continues in the same direction from 0 upward.
  4. Because every request waits for at most one sweep plus the return, the spread of waiting times is more uniform than under SCAN, where requests just behind the head wait almost two sweeps.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) describes LOOK, which turns at the last request.
  • (C) reverses the truth: C-SCAN is the policy that removes that long wait, at the cost of a full return sweep.
  • (D) confuses C-SCAN with LOOK; the defining feature is the unserviced return, not the sorting.

Common misconception. Thinking C-SCAN is strictly better than SCAN. It equalises waiting time but moves the head over the whole disk on every cycle, so total movement is usually larger.

Takeaway. SCAN = sweep both ways. C-SCAN = sweep one way, fly back empty.

OS-U6-Q23MCQhard

On a 200-cylinder disk (0 to 199) the head is at cylinder 53 and the pending queue is 98, 183, 37, 122, 14, 124, 65, 67. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

['FCFS moves the head 640 cylinders, which is the least of the four policies named in the other options.', "LOOK, moving first toward cylinder 0, moves the head 208 cylinders, which is less than SSTF's 236 and less than SCAN's 236.", 'SCAN, moving toward 0 and travelling to cylinder 0, moves the head 208 cylinders.', 'SSTF moves the head 640 cylinders, because it reverses direction at every request.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four policies produce four different orders on the same queue, so their total movements must be computed separately. Only the comparison is common to them.

Step by step.

  1. FCFS, arrival order: 45 + 85 + 146 + 85 + 108 + 110 + 59 + 2 = 640.
  2. SSTF, nearest first: 12 + 2 + 30 + 23 + 84 + 24 + 2 + 59 = 236, order 53, 65, 67, 37, 14, 98, 122, 124, 183.
  3. SCAN toward 0: down to 14, on to 0 (53 cylinders), then up through 65, 67, 98, 122, 124, 183 (183 cylinders) = 236.
  4. LOOK toward 0: down to 14 (53 cylinders), then straight up to 183 without going to 0: 16 + 23 + 51 + 2 + 31 + 24 + 2 + 59 = 208.
  5. So LOOK is the cheapest of the four here, and it beats both SSTF and SCAN on this queue.

Answer: Option (B) — LOOK toward 0 gives 208, less than SSTF's and SCAN's 236.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) FCFS does give 640, but that is the largest of the four totals, not the least.
  • (C) SCAN gives 236; 208 is LOOK, which does not travel to cylinder 0.
  • (D) 640 is the FCFS figure, and SSTF does not reverse at every request.

Common misconception. Assuming SSTF is always best. It is greedy on the next step only: here LOOK's single sweep serves the same requests with less travel, because SSTF wastes movement diving to 14 and climbing back.

Takeaway. Compute, do not rank. On this queue the four totals are 640, 236, 236 and 208.

OS-U6-Q25MSQhard

Which of the following statements about disk-scheduling policies are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

['SSTF can starve a request that lies far from the head if nearer requests keep arriving.', 'SCAN bounds the waiting time of any request, because the head sweeps the whole disk in each direction.', 'C-SCAN gives a more uniform waiting time than SCAN, because it always sweeps in one direction and returns without servicing requests.', 'LOOK differs from SCAN only in that LOOK never reverses its direction of travel.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each policy is defined by a victim rule for the next request, and each rule has a fairness consequence. Greedy policies can starve; sweep policies bound the wait; the circular variant equalises it.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. SSTF always takes the nearest pending request. If a steady stream of near requests arrives, the far one is never nearest and may wait indefinitely.
  2. (B) True. SCAN passes every cylinder once per sweep, so a request waits at most the time for the head to reach it — one full sweep plus the return leg. That is a bound, unlike SSTF.
  3. (C) True. Under SCAN a request just behind the head waits for almost two full sweeps, while one just ahead waits very little; under C-SCAN every request waits between zero and one sweep plus the fixed return, so the spread is narrower.
  4. (D) False. LOOK reverses at the last pending request in each direction, exactly as SCAN reverses at the end of the disk. Both reverse; they differ in where they reverse.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C) are true; (D) is false.

Common misconception. Believing a smarter policy cannot starve. SSTF is the classic starvation example on disks, just as shortest-job-first is on the CPU. Fairness comes from the sweep, not from the greedy choice.

Takeaway. Greedy = fast average, possible starvation. SCAN = bounded wait. C-SCAN = bounded and uniform wait, at the cost of the return sweep.

OS-U6-Q28MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about the LOOK disk-scheduling policy is TRUE?

  1. LOOK travels to the physical end of the disk in each direction, so its total head movement always equals that of SCAN for the same queue.
  2. LOOK reverses direction at the last pending request in the current direction, so the head never travels past the outermost request.
  3. LOOK serves requests strictly in arrival order, but only while the head is moving in the same direction as the request.
  4. LOOK is C-SCAN with the return sweep removed, so its waiting time is unbounded.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SCAN and LOOK are both elevator policies. SCAN goes all the way to the disk edge (cylinder 0 or the last cylinder) before turning; LOOK turns as soon as no further request lies ahead.

Step by step.

  1. In SCAN the head reaches the physical end of the disk even when the last request is far short of it, and that empty travel is wasted movement.
  2. LOOK examines the queue: if nothing lies beyond the current position in the direction of travel, it reverses at the last request it has just served.
  3. So LOOK's movement is never larger than SCAN's for the same queue, and it is strictly smaller whenever the outermost request is not at the disk edge.
  4. Option (C) describes FCFS, not LOOK.

Answer: Option (B) — LOOK turns back at the last request, not at the disk edge.

Common misconception. Students often think LOOK and SCAN are the same algorithm. They differ exactly in where the turn happens, and that difference changes the head-movement total whenever the outermost request is inside the edge.

Takeaway. SCAN = elevator to the wall. LOOK = elevator to the last passenger.

OS-U6-Q31NATeasy

A disk has 200 cylinders numbered 0 to 199. The head is at cylinder 100 and the pending requests, in arrival order, are 55, 58, 39, 18, 90, 160, 150, 38, 184. Under FCFS, what is the total head movement, in cylinders?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. FCFS serves the queue in arrival order and ignores where the head is, so the total is the sum of the absolute differences between consecutive positions, starting from the initial head position.

Step by step.

  1. 100 → 55 = 45
  2. 55 → 58 = 3
  3. 58 → 39 = 19
  4. 39 → 18 = 21
  5. 18 → 90 = 72
  6. 90 → 160 = 70
  7. 160 → 150 = 10
  8. 150 → 38 = 112
  9. 38 → 184 = 146
  10. Sum = 45 + 3 + 19 + 21 + 72 + 70 + 10 + 112 + 146 = 498.

Answer: 498 cylinders.

Common misconception. The frequent error is to sort the queue first, which is what SSTF or SCAN would do; FCFS keeps arrival order. A second error is to omit the move from the initial head position to the first request.

Takeaway. FCFS total = Σ |positioni+1 − positioni| in arrival order, beginning at the head.

OS-U6-Q35MCQmedium

The head is at cylinder 100 on a 200-cylinder disk (0 to 199), and the pending queue is 55, 58, 39, 18, 90, 160, 150, 38, 184. In which order does SSTF service these requests?

  1. 90, 58, 55, 39, 38, 18, 150, 160, 184
  2. 90, 150, 160, 184, 58, 55, 39, 38, 18
  3. 55, 58, 39, 18, 90, 160, 150, 38, 184
  4. 90, 58, 55, 39, 38, 18, 184, 160, 150
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SSTF always serves the pending request nearest the current head position, recomputing the nearest after each service. It is greedy and ignores direction of travel and arrival order.

Step by step.

  1. From 100 the distances are: 90 → 10, 58 → 42, 55 → 45, 39 → 61, 18 → 82, 150 → 50, 160 → 60, 184 → 84, 38 → 62. Nearest is 90.
  2. From 90: 58 → 32 is nearest, then from 58 the nearest is 55 (3).
  3. From 55: 39 → 16, then from 39 the nearest is 38 (1), then from 38 the nearest is 18 (20).
  4. From 18 the remaining requests are 150, 160, 184: nearest is 150 (132), then 160 (10), then 184 (24).
  5. Order: 90, 58, 55, 39, 38, 18, 150, 160, 184. Total movement 10 + 32 + 3 + 16 + 1 + 20 + 132 + 10 + 24 = 248 cylinders.

Answer: Option (A) — 90, 58, 55, 39, 38, 18, 150, 160, 184.

Common misconception. The trap is to sweep to one side and then the other (option B), which is SCAN-like, or to keep arrival order (option C), which is FCFS. SSTF interleaves: it can reverse direction at every step.

Takeaway. SSTF recomputes the nearest neighbour after every service; the path may zig-zag freely.

OS-U6-Q39MCQmedium

On a 200-cylinder disk (0 to 199) the head is at cylinder 100 and the pending queue is 55, 58, 39, 18, 90, 160, 150, 38, 184. The head is moving toward cylinder 0. Under SCAN, counting the travel to cylinder 0 and the full sweep back, what is the total head movement?

  1. 208 cylinders
  2. 248 cylinders
  3. 284 cylinders
  4. 314 cylinders
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. SCAN is the elevator: the head keeps moving in its current direction until it reaches the physical end of the disk, serving every request it passes, then reverses and serves the rest.

Step by step.

  1. Moving toward 0 from 100, the requests passed are 90, 58, 55, 39, 38, 18 in that order.
  2. The head does not stop at 18: SCAN runs on to the physical end of the disk, cylinder 0. That leg costs 100 − 0 = 100 cylinders.
  3. The head then reverses and climbs, serving 150, 160 and finally 184. The upward leg is 184 − 0 = 184 cylinders.
  4. Total = 100 + 184 = 284 cylinders. (The travel stops at the last request; no request lies between 184 and 199, so that stretch is not counted.)

Answer: Option (C) — 284 cylinders.

Common misconception. The two wrong answers come from the two classic slips: 208 forgets the leg to cylinder 0 (LOOK, not SCAN, would give 82 + 166 = 248), and 314 counts the jump back to 199 as if this were C-SCAN.

Takeaway. SCAN pays for the trip to the wall; LOOK turns at the last request and saves that empty travel.

OS-U6-Q44NAThard

On a 200-cylinder disk (0 to 199) the head is at cylinder 100 and the pending queue is 55, 58, 39, 18, 90, 160, 150, 38, 184. The head is moving toward cylinder 0. Under C-SCAN, and counting every cylinder crossed including the return sweep from cylinder 0 to cylinder 199, what is the total head movement?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. C-SCAN treats the disk as circular: it serves requests while moving in one direction only, then returns from one edge to the other without serving anything, and starts again in the same direction.

Step by step.

  1. Downward leg: from 100 to cylinder 0, serving 90, 58, 55, 39, 38, 18 on the way. Movement = 100.
  2. Return sweep: cylinder 0 to cylinder 199, serving nothing. Movement = 199.
  3. Downward leg again from 199: the remaining requests 184, 160, 150 are served in that order, and the travel counted stops at 184. Movement = 199 − 184 = 15.
  4. Total = 100 + 199 + 15 = 314 cylinders.
  5. For comparison, SCAN on the same queue costs 100 + 184 = 284, because it reverses at 0 and climbs straight to 184 without the trip to 199.

Answer: 314 cylinders.

Common misconception. The classic error is to drop the 199-cylinder return sweep and answer 115, or to treat C-SCAN as SCAN and answer 284. The sweep is real head movement even though it serves nothing.

Takeaway. C-SCAN = one-directional service + a full-edge return sweep; the sweep is what makes waiting times uniform.

OS-U6-Q48MCQhard

On a 200-cylinder disk (0 to 199) the head is at 100 and the pending queue is 55, 58, 39, 18, 90, 160, 150, 38, 184. Which one of the following statements about total head movement is TRUE?

  1. FCFS moves 498 cylinders, which is more than SCAN but less than C-SCAN.
  2. LOOK, moving first toward cylinder 0, moves 248 cylinders — exactly the same total as SSTF for this queue.
  3. SCAN, moving toward 0 and travelling to cylinder 0, moves 314 cylinders.
  4. C-SCAN moves 284 cylinders, which is less than SCAN.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four totals have to be computed separately for this queue; the interesting fact is that LOOK and SSTF coincide here even though they are different policies.

Step by step.

  1. FCFS, in arrival order: 45 + 3 + 19 + 21 + 72 + 70 + 10 + 112 + 146 = 498.
  2. SSTF: 90, 58, 55, 39, 38, 18, 150, 160, 184 gives 10 + 32 + 3 + 16 + 1 + 20 + 132 + 10 + 24 = 248.
  3. SCAN toward 0: 100 down to 0, then up to 184 = 100 + 184 = 284.
  4. LOOK toward 0: down to the last request 18 (82 cylinders), then up to 184 (166 cylinders) = 248.
  5. C-SCAN toward 0: 100 + 199 + 15 = 314.
  6. So (A) is false because 498 is greater than C-SCAN's 314; (C) is false because SCAN is 284, not 314; (D) is false because C-SCAN is 314, not 284. (B) is true.

Answer: Option (B) — LOOK and SSTF both total 248 cylinders on this queue.

Common misconception. The coincidence is the trap in reverse: students see 248 twice and assume one of the two figures must be wrong. LOOK and SSTF agree here by accident of the request positions, not by any theorem.

Takeaway. Compute each policy separately; never infer one total from another.

OS-U6-Q50MSQhard

Which of the following statements about disk-scheduling policies are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. C-SCAN bounds the waiting time of every request by roughly one full sweep plus the return sweep, so no request can be starved, whereas SSTF can postpone a distant request indefinitely while nearer ones keep arriving.
  2. LOOK and SCAN differ only when no request lies beyond the current head position in the direction of travel: LOOK turns back at the last request, while SCAN continues to the disk edge.
  3. SSTF minimises the total head movement over the whole pending queue, which is why it is preferred to FCFS.
  4. For the same queue and starting position, C-SCAN always moves the head less than SCAN, because it never has to reverse direction.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Fairness and optimality are different questions. Sweeping policies bound waiting time; greedy policies lower the average but can starve.

Step by step.

  1. C-SCAN always moves in one direction for service, so every request waits at most one pass plus the return sweep — a bound independent of the arrival pattern. SSTF, being greedy, can keep a far request waiting while nearer ones arrive. (A) is true.
  2. SCAN and LOOK are identical until the queue runs out in the direction of travel; then SCAN goes on to the disk edge and LOOK turns immediately. (B) is true.
  3. (C) is false. SSTF is greedy, not optimal: choosing the nearest request now can force a long jump later, and the truly minimal tour is a travelling-salesman problem, not a nearest-neighbour walk.
  4. (D) is false. C-SCAN adds the return sweep from one edge to the other, so for a queue whose requests lie away from that edge it usually moves more than SCAN, not less.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Option (C) is the most common error in the whole topic: SSTF is described everywhere as “shortest seek time first”, which sounds optimal. It minimises the next seek, not the total.

Takeaway. Greedy lowers the average and forfeits the bound; sweeping buys the bound and pays for the trip to the edge.

access-control-protection · 10
OS-U6-Q04MCQeasy

A Unix-like file has permission mode 640, read as rw- r-- ---. Which one of the following is TRUE?

['The owner may read and write, members of the group may read, and all other users have no access.', 'The owner may read and write, members of the group may read and write, and all other users may read.', 'The owner may read only, the group may read and write, and all other users have no access.', 'The owner may read, write and execute, the group may read, and all other users may read.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A three-digit octal mode gives the rights of the owner, the group and everyone else, one digit each. Within a digit, read = 4, write = 2 and execute = 1, added together.

Step by step.

  1. Owner digit 6 = 4 + 2 = read + write, no execute.
  2. Group digit 4 = read only.
  3. Other digit 0 = no rights at all.
  4. So the file is writable by its owner, readable by the group, and closed to everyone else.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) would be mode 664.
  • (C) would be mode 460, which also gives the group write.
  • (D) would be mode 744.

Common misconception. Reading the digits as counts of users rather than as bit sums. The digit is an octal number, not a quantity.

Takeaway. 6 = rw, 5 = r-x, 4 = r--, 0 = ---. Three digits, three classes of user.

OS-U6-Q08MCQeasy

Which statement correctly distinguishes an access-control list from a capability list?

['An access-control list is stored with the object and names the subjects allowed to use it; a capability is held by a subject and names an object together with the rights the subject may exercise on it.', 'An access-control list is held by the subject and a capability list is stored with the object.', 'An access-control list can express only read and write rights, while a capability can express any right.', 'The two are the same mechanism under different names, differing only in whether the list is sorted.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Protection information can be attached to the object or to the subject. Attached to the object it is an access-control list; attached to the subject it is a capability.

Step by step.

  1. An ACL on a file lists pairs (user or group, rights). The check at open time is “does this subject appear in the file's list?”
  2. A capability is a token held by the subject, for example a descriptor that names a file and carries read/write rights. The check is “does the subject hold a token for this object?”
  3. ACLs are easy to inspect and revoke for one object, but hard to answer “what can this user reach?”. Capabilities are the reverse.
  4. The two therefore differ in where the list lives, not in the kind of rights it can express.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) swaps the two placements.
  • (C) both mechanisms can express any right.
  • (D) they are duals, not the same thing: one is per-object, the other per-subject.

Common misconception. Treating a capability as “an ACL entry copied to the user”. A capability is a protected token the user can present; losing control of it means losing the rights it carries.

Takeaway. ACL = column of the access matrix stored with the object. Capability list = row of the access matrix held by the subject.

OS-U6-Q12MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about file protection are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

['Revoking a capability is expensive, because capabilities are held by subjects and must be found one by one.', 'An access-control list answers “what may this user do with this file?” easily, but answers “what may this user do with anything?” only by scanning every object.', 'The three permission classes rwx for owner, group and others carry the same information as a full access-control list over all users of the system.', 'In Unix, the execute bit on a directory grants the right to traverse that directory in order to reach a named file inside it.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Protection can be organised per object (ACLs) or per subject (capabilities), and the cost of the two operations that matter — checking a right and revoking one — falls differently on each.

Step by step.

  1. (A) True. A capability is a token in the subject's own table, so revoking it means locating every holder; there is no single place to delete an entry.
  2. (B) True. An ACL is the natural per-object view; the per-subject question needs a scan of all objects.
  3. (C) False. Three classes cannot express per-user entries: two users in the same group are indistinguishable under the mode bits, and there is no way to name an individual outside the group.
  4. (D) True. On a directory, x means “may pass through”, r means “may list the names”, and w means “may create or delete entries”. Traversal needs x, not r.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D) are true; (C) is false.

Common misconception. Reading the directory r bit as “permission to open files inside”. Listing and traversal are separate rights; a directory with mode 711 can be traversed by everyone while its contents remain unlistable.

Takeaway. ACLs answer per-object questions cheaply, capabilities answer per-subject questions cheaply; directory x is the traversal right.

OS-U6-Q16MCQmedium

Directory /d has mode 750 and is owned by user ann, with group staff. The file /d/notes.txt has mode 644. User bob, who is neither ann nor a member of staff, tries to read the file. What happens?

['The read succeeds, because the file itself is world-readable.', 'The read fails with permission denied, because bob lacks the execute permission on /d and so cannot resolve the name inside it.', 'The read succeeds but the file appears empty.', 'The read fails because bob lacks the read permission on /d.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reaching a file means walking every directory on its path. On a directory, x is the right to pass through and to look up a name in it; r is only the right to list the names.

Step by step.

  1. Mode 750 gives the owner rwx, the group r-x, and others ---.
  2. Bob is neither the owner nor in the group, so he falls into the “others” class and has no rights on the directory at all.
  3. To open notes.txt, the system must resolve the name inside /d, which requires traverse permission on /d.
  4. That permission is missing, so the lookup fails with permission denied — before the file's own mode 644 is ever consulted.

Answer: Option (B) — denied at the directory, not at the file.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) the file's mode is irrelevant once the path cannot be resolved.
  • (C) a permission failure is an error, not an empty file.
  • (D) the missing right is x, not r; r on the directory would control listing its names, and bob lacks both anyway.

Common misconception. Checking only the file's own permission bits. Every component of the path is a gate, and the first gate that fails stops the operation.

Takeaway. Directory x = traverse and look up; directory r = list. A world-readable file inside an unreachable directory is unreachable.

OS-U6-Q20MCQhard

A program file has mode 4755 and is owned by root. Which one of the following is TRUE?

["The leading 4 is the set-user-ID bit: the program runs with the effective user ID of the file's owner, and the final digit 5 gives read and execute to all other users.", 'The leading 4 means the file may be opened by four users at the same time.', "The set-user-ID bit changes the file's owner to root each time the program runs.", 'The final digit 5 grants write and execute to all other users.']
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The fourth octal digit carries the three special bits: set-user-ID (4), set-group-ID (2) and sticky (1). Set-user-ID makes the process adopt the effective user ID of the file's owner for the duration of the run.

Step by step.

  1. Mode 4755 = special digit 4, owner 7, group 5, others 5.
  2. 4 in the special digit sets set-user-ID; owner 7 = read, write, execute.
  3. Group 5 and others 5 are both r-x, so every user may read and execute the program.
  4. When any user runs it, the process gains root's effective user ID, which is why such a program can perform a privileged action for an unprivileged caller — and why it must be written carefully.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) the special digit is not a count of users.
  • (C) ownership is a property of the file's metadata; set-user-ID changes the process, not the file.
  • (D) 5 is r-x, not write — write would need 7 or 3.

Common misconception. Reading the leading digit as part of the owner's permissions. It is a separate field of three bits; 4755 and 755 differ only in set-user-ID.

Takeaway. 4 = set-user-ID, 2 = set-group-ID, 1 = sticky. Set-user-ID elevates the process to the owner's identity, not the file to the caller's.

OS-U6-Q29MCQeasy

A file report.txt is owned by user u1 and its group is g1; its mode is 644. User u2 is a member of both g1 and g2, but is not u1. Which permission class decides what u2 may do with the file?

  1. The group class, because the file's group is g1 and u2 is a member of g1; membership of g2 is irrelevant.
  2. The owner class, because u2 belongs to the file's group and so inherits the owner's rights.
  3. The union of the group class and the other class, since u2 qualifies for both.
  4. The other class, because u2 is not the owner of the file.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Unix checks the three permission classes in the fixed order owner, then group, then other, and applies the first class that matches. Exactly one class applies, and it is the file's group — not the union of the user's groups.

Step by step.

  1. The file records one group, here g1. There is no per-user list in a mode word.
  2. u2 is not the owner, so the owner class does not apply.
  3. u2 is a member of g1, so the group class applies: mode 644 gives the group read only.
  4. The check stops there. The other class is never consulted, and the extra membership in g2 grants nothing, because g2 is not the file's group.

Answer: Option (A) — the group class of the file's own group decides, giving u2 read access only.

Common misconception. The common error is to take the union of every group the user belongs to, or to let a group member be treated as the owner. A mode word names one group and the first matching class wins.

Takeaway. Owner, then group, then other — first match, no union.

OS-U6-Q32MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about Unix file and directory permissions are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A directory needs the read bit for a user to list its entries, and the execute bit to traverse it or to reach a named file inside it.
  2. Clearing the write bit on a file is enough to stop its owner from deleting it, because deleting a file requires write permission on that file.
  3. The sticky bit on a directory restricts deletion of an entry to the owner of that entry, or to the owner of the directory, even when the directory is world-writable.
  4. The set-group-ID bit on a directory makes files created inside it inherit the directory's group rather than the creator's primary group.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A directory is a table mapping names to inode numbers, so its own permission bits govern name lookup (execute), listing (read) and creating or removing entries (write). A file's own bits never govern whether its name may be unlinked.

Step by step.

  1. Read on a directory lets a user list the names; execute lets the user traverse the directory to reach a known name. Both are needed for a full listing that includes attributes. So (A) is true.
  2. Deleting a name edits the directory, not the file: the user needs write and execute on the directory. A file whose own write bit is clear can still be deleted by anyone with write access to its directory. So (B) is false.
  3. The sticky bit on a shared directory such as /tmp stops one user from deleting another user's file there. So (C) is true.
  4. Set-group-ID on a directory is the inheritance rule: new entries take the directory's group. So (D) is true.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Option (B) is the classic trap: it confuses permission on the file with permission on the name. Removing a name is a directory operation.

Takeaway. On a file the bits guard its contents; on a directory the same three bits guard listing, traversal and name editing.

OS-U6-Q37MCQmedium

The access matrix has one row per subject and one column per object, with the permitted operations in each cell. Which implementation stores the matrix row by row?

  1. An access-control list, because each object carries the set of subjects allowed to use it.
  2. A bitmap of free blocks, because one bit per block records one row of the matrix.
  3. A protection ring, because the ring number of a process is its row in the matrix.
  4. A capability list, because each subject carries, for every object it may use, an unforgeable token naming that object and the rights held on it.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The access matrix can be decomposed by columns or by rows. Column decomposition stores, per object, the subjects and their rights — that is an access-control list. Row decomposition stores, per subject, the objects and its rights — that is a capability list.

Step by step.

  1. A row of the matrix answers “what may this subject do?”. Storing one row per subject gives each subject a list of (object, rights) pairs: a capability list.
  2. A column answers “who may use this object, and how?”. That is an ACL, so option (A) is the opposite decomposition.
  3. A protection ring orders privilege levels; it is not a row of the matrix.
  4. A free-space bitmap has nothing to do with protection.

Answer: Option (D) — capabilities are the row-wise decomposition of the access matrix.

Common misconception. Because both structures ultimately describe the same matrix, students treat them as interchangeable. They are transposes of each other, which is why revocation is cheap for ACLs and expensive for capabilities.

Takeaway. Columns → ACLs (per object). Rows → capabilities (per subject).

OS-U6-Q41NATmedium

A file is to be readable and writable by its owner, readable and executable by its group, and inaccessible to everyone else. What is its permission mode as a three-digit octal number?
(Enter an integer.)

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each permission class is one octal digit built from three bits in the order read (4), write (2), execute (1). The digits are given owner, group, other.

Step by step.

  1. Owner: read + write = 4 + 2 = 6.
  2. Group: read + execute = 4 + 1 = 5.
  3. Other: nothing = 0.
  4. Mode = 650, written rw- r-x ---.

Answer: 650.

Common misconception. The usual error is 750 or 640 — swapping the group digit's execute bit for write, or dropping execute from the group when the intent was “may run but not modify”.

Takeaway. 4 = read, 2 = write, 1 = execute; sum per class, concatenate owner-group-other.

OS-U6-Q46MSQhard

Which of the following statements about protection mechanisms are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. A capability is held by the subject that owns it, so it can be prevented from being forged only if the system tags it and checks the tag on every use.
  2. An access-control list that grants a right to a group and later denies it to one member of that group gives different results under first-match and deny-precedence semantics, so the system must fix one.
  3. Creating a hard link to a file gives the creator of the link the same access rights as the file's owner.
  4. The set-user-ID bit on a program lets an unprivileged user perform operations allowed to the program's owner, which makes any bug in that program a privilege-escalation risk.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A capability is a token the subject carries, so the system cannot rely on the subject's honesty; an ACL lives with the object and is consulted by the system. Both need well-defined conflict semantics.

Step by step.

  1. Because a capability is presented by the subject, a forged one would grant the rights it names. The system must therefore make it unforgeable — a tagged, protected address space, or a cryptographic signature. So (A) is true.
  2. If entry 1 grants read to group staff and entry 2 denies read to user bob, a first-match list lets bob read and a deny-precedence list refuses him. The two orders genuinely differ, so the semantics must be specified. So (B) is true.
  3. (C) is false. A hard link is a second name for the same inode, so access is still decided by that inode's owner and mode word. The user who creates the link gains no rights at all.
  4. Set-user-ID runs the program with the file owner's effective identity, which is exactly why a buffer overflow in a setuid-root program is a privilege escalation. So (D) is true.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. The trap in (C) is the word “creator”: a hard link adds a name, not a right. Ownership and the mode word belong to the inode and are unchanged by linking.

Takeaway. ACLs are checked by the system on the object; capabilities must be made unforgeable because the subject carries them.

What this test contains

25 questions on Unit 2 (Inter-Process Communication & Synchronization), Set 1 of 2 (second pass). All four subtopics are covered: the critical-section problem — the three requirements, Peterson's two-process algorithm and the filter-lock generalisation, the bakery algorithm and the cost of memory reordering; hardware primitives — atomic test-and-set and compare-and-swap, interrupt masking on a uniprocessor against a multiprocessor, spinlocks and ticket locks, and acquire against release ordering; semaphores — counting against binary semaphores, the meaning of a negative value, ownership, and building a counting semaphore out of binary ones; and the classical problems — the bounded buffer, readers and writers, and the dining philosophers, with the deadlock and starvation each one exposes. MCQ carries 1 mark, MSQ and NAT carry 2 marks, there is no negative marking, and an MSQ scores only when the exact set of correct options is selected: 35 marks in 60 minutes. This is a practice scheme, not the official GATE scheme.

Concept map used to build the questions

  • The critical-section problem. Mutual exclusion, progress and bounded waiting, and what each one forbids; the structure of an entry section, critical section and exit section; Peterson's algorithm, what the flag array and the turn variable each contribute, and which requirement dies when either is removed; the filter lock for n threads and its n − 1 levels; the bakery algorithm, the role of the choosing array, and ticket ties broken by process id; and why every software solution assumes atomic, non-reordered single-cell accesses.
  • Hardware primitives. What test_and_set and compare_and_swap return and why the old value matters; interrupt masking as a per-CPU operation, correct on a uniprocessor and insufficient on a multiprocessor; spinlocks, when spinning beats blocking, and the cache-line traffic contention creates; ticket locks and FIFO order; and the difference between an atomic acquire and an ordered release.
  • Semaphores. wait and signal, the value as free units when non-negative and as the blocked count when negative; binary semaphores against mutexes and the question of ownership; implementing a counting semaphore with two binary semaphores; and the self-deadlock that follows from nesting two waits on the same semaphore.
  • Classical problems. The bounded buffer with mutex, empty and full, and why the counting semaphore must be taken before the mutex; readers and writers, group locking of wrt, and the starvation each preference rule creates; the dining philosophers, the circular wait that a per-fork semaphore permits, and the seating limit, global fork order and atomic two-fork acquisition that break it.

Connections. The spinlocks here are the locks that Unit 1's scheduler must hold only briefly, because a preempted lock holder stalls every spinner; the circular wait built from forks is the same wait-for graph Unit 3 analyses with the resource-allocation graph and avoids with Banker's algorithm; the bounded buffer is the classic page-fault queue of Unit 5 and the disk request queue of Unit 6; and the atomic primitives reappear in Unit 4 when a TLB shootdown has to be serialised across processors.

Source categories

Each question carries a data-source tag. STANDARD_TEXTBOOK marks questions built on the standard treatment of synchronisation (Silberschatz–Galvin–Gagne, Tanenbaum, Stallings), written in original wording. ORIGINAL_SYNTHESIS marks independently designed GATE-style practice. No question on this paper is presented as an official GATE question; no synchronisation previous-year question has been verified against an official paper and answer key, so nothing here is labelled a PYQ or a PYQ variation.

Provenance: no question here is presented as an official GATE question unless its year, paper and official answer key are recorded in the Question Bank tab. All other items are practice questions written for this series.
